<meta>This page continues the alchemical discourses, specifically Discourse 27, which uses mythological allegory to explain the necessity of combining practical work with theoretical reason.</meta>

# Discourse 27

Concerning Erichthonius <note>In Greek mythology, Erichthonius was an early king of Athens who was born from the earth after Vulcan (Hephaestus) attempted to violate Pallas (Athena); he was often depicted with the tail of a serpent instead of feet.</note>, they write that he sprang from the seed of Vulcan cast upon the earth while he was striving with Pallas, the Goddess of Wisdom. He was born with serpentine rather than human feet. Very similar to him are those who, depending upon the means of Vulcan alone <note>original: "the meanes of Vulcan alone"; meaning the heat and tools of the forge or laboratory without intellectual guidance.</note>, without the wisdom of Pallas, do beget monstrous children—namely, those that are without feet and aborted, which can neither sustain themselves nor profit others.

It is a miserable thing for a man to go on all fours—that is, hands and feet together—but it is much more miserable to be altogether destitute of feet and to use arms instead. Such people seem to have degenerated to the nature of worms, who move in the manner of creeping insects. The two legs are the organs for walking in man; without them, he cannot truly walk, just as he cannot see without eyes or touch tangible things without hands.

So likewise, <term>Chymistry</term> <note>Alchemy</note> and every operative art is supposed to have two legs on which to stand: namely, **experience** and **reason**. Without either of these, that art is lame and imperfect in its tradition and practice, and it will not achieve its proposed end. 

Alchemy indeed chiefly delights in two subjects (acting as legs), of which one is the **key** and the other is the **bolt-string** <note>original: "string of the barre"; likely referring to the mechanism that pulls a door bolt or latch.</note>. By these, the <term>Philosophical Rosary</term> <note>A symbolic alchemical rose garden, often referencing the famous text *Rosarium Philosophorum*.</note> is opened on all sides, and passage is granted to those who have the right to enter. But if one of these is missing for he who is about to enter, it will be the same as a man whose feet are bound trying to outrun a hare. For that entry into the garden—which is enclosed and fenced on every side—without a key, imitates a thief who, coming in a dark night, can discern nothing that grows in the garden, nor enjoy the things he has stolen.

But the **Key** is a thing of very little esteem or value, which is properly called a "stone known in the chapter" <note>A reference to a common alchemical riddle: the "stone" is everywhere and cheap, yet ignored by the ignorant.</note>, and it is the root of Rhodes <note>A pun on the Greek *rhodon*, meaning "rose."</note>. Without this key, a twig is not put forth, nor does the bud swell, nor does the Rose become green or reveal its multitude of leaves. 

But a question might be asked: where must this Key be sought? I answer with the oracle: it must be investigated there where the bones of Orestes are said to be found—namely, where the "winds," the "striker," "that which is beaten back," and the "destruction of men" are found together. As Lychas <note>In Herodotus's *Histories*, Lychas found the bones of Orestes by interpreting a Delphic oracle about a blacksmith's shop.</note> has interpreted it, this is in the **brazier's shop** or metal-workhouse. For the oracle intended "bellows" to be understood by "winds"; a "hammer" by the "striker"; an "anvil" by "that which is beaten back"; and "iron" by the "destruction of men." 

If a man knows how to number well and distinguish the signs, he will certainly find the Key in the northern hemisphere of the Zodiac, and the bolt-string in the southern. Having obtained these, it will be easy to open the door and enter. In the very entrance, he will see Venus with her paramour Adonis; for she has tinged the white roses a purple color with her blood <note>Refers to the myth where Venus, rushing to the dying Adonis, pricked her foot on a thorn, her blood turning the white roses red.</note>. In the same place, a Dragon is also observed, as in the <term>Hesperian gardens</term> <note>The mythical garden of the Hesperides which grew golden apples and was guarded by the dragon Ladon.</note>, which watches to preserve the roses. The scent of the roses is said to be increased by garlic planted nearby, and that is by reason of an exceeding degree of heat which appears in garlic, in which respect it is a remedy against cold poisons. For Roses...

<summary>This discourse uses the myth of Erichthonius to argue that alchemy requires both practical skill (Vulcan) and theoretical wisdom (Pallas), symbolized by two "legs" or a "key" and "bolt" found in a blacksmith's shop.</summary>
<keywords>Erichthonius, Vulcan, Pallas Athena, Experience and Reason, Philosophical Rosary, Orestes' bones, Alchemy, Venus and Adonis, Hesperian gardens, Garlic and Roses</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical principles using the metaphor of the rose and its protection by nature, following a discourse on chemical transformations and the qualities of sulfur.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

-># Discourse 27.<-

...require the heat of the sun and earth before they can attain the color and scent most pleasing to the eyes and nose. Moreover, the fumes of common sulfur make red roses turn white wherever they touch; on the contrary, the spirit of vitriol <note>original: "Vitriol"; sulfuric acid</note> or *aqua fortis* <note>original: "Aqua fortis"; nitric acid</note> restores them to a deep red color that lasts. For common sulfur is an enemy to "philosophical sulfur" <note>An alchemical principle representing the active, combustible, or soul-like element, distinct from common mineral sulfur</note>, though it cannot destroy it; but the solutive <note>dissolving</note> water is friendly and preserves its color. 

The rose is dedicated to Venus regarding its beauty, in which it surpasses all other flowers. It is a virgin that nature has armed so that she might not be violated without revenge and punishment. Violets are without weapons and are trodden underfoot, but roses—protected by thorns—have yellow hairs on the inside and a green garment <note>the green sepals surrounding the bud</note> on the outside. No man can pluck them and separate them from the thorns except he who is wise; otherwise, he will feel a sting in his fingers. 

Thus, none but the most cautious philosopher will pluck the flowers, unless he wishes to find bees and stings in the hives, and gall <note>bitterness</note> with honey. Many men have secretly and lawlessly entered the Rosary <note>likely referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous alchemical text, or a metaphorical garden of knowledge</note>, but reaped nothing from it but misery—that is, they lost their labor. Regarding this, Batusser <note>A speaker in the *Turba Philosophorum*</note> says in the *Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note>: 

"Our books seem to be very harmful to those who read our writings only once, twice, or thrice; for being frustrated in their understanding and their whole study, they also—which is worst—lose their money, effort, and time, if they have spent any on this art." 

And a little later: "For when a man thinks he has finished and possesses the work, he will find nothing in his hands."

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a rose protected by thorns to describe the difficulty and danger of alchemical study, warning that only the wise can succeed while the unprepared lose their time and money.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, sulfur, vitriol, aqua fortis, Venus, Rosary, Batusser, Turba Philosophorum, philosophical sulfur, metaphor of the rose</keywords>

### Emblem 28. On the Secrets of Nature.

->The King, sitting in a steam <note>original: "vaporous"</note> bath, is bathed and freed from melancholy by Pharut.<-

### Epigram 28.

> Prince Duenech, armed with the lion's strength <note>In alchemical symbolism, the lion often represents the "Green Lion" (acid/solvent) or the "Red Lion" (purified sulfur/gold).</note>,
> Grew melancholy <note>In the 17th century, melancholy was believed to be a physical illness caused by an excess of "black bile," often associated with lead and the planet Saturn.</note> and morose at length.
> Pharut is sent for to provide a cure,
> Who, by a steam bath, ensures his health;
> This method, through frequent use, produced a good effect,
> And wholly corrected his ailments <note>original: "distempers," referring to an imbalance of the bodily humors.</note>.

<vocab>Emblem, Secrets of Nature, Prince Duenech, Pharut, steam bath, melancholy, Epigram</vocab>

<summary>This emblem illustrates an alchemical allegory where Prince Duenech is cured of his melancholy through a medicinal steam bath administered by Pharut. This represents the purification of a substance—likely antimony or lead—through heat and moisture.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Prince Duenech, Pharut, steam bath, melancholy, humoral medicine, purification, Michael Maier</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical and physiological processes, specifically focusing on the "three concoctions" or stages of digestion and refinement in both the human body and the creation of metals.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>

### Discourse 28.

Just as there are three stages of digestion <note>original: "concoctions," referring to the physiological process of ripening or refining substances in the body</note> in man—the first in the stomach, the second in the liver, and the third in the veins—so too are there three general evacuations of waste products that correspond to them and daily carry away their excesses <note>original: "superfluities"</note>. Specifically, the first is by stool, which belongs to the first digestion; the second is by urine, which belongs to the second; and the third is by evaporation through the pores of the whole body or by sweat, which is specific to the third. 

In the first stage, the chyle <note>original: "chylo," the milky fluid formed from digested food</note> is produced; in the next, the chyme; and in the last, a dewy substance is refined and applied to every part of the body. The waste or feces of the first stage are thick, bilious, and fatty; these are carried through the bowels to the anus <note>original: "back door"</note>, and if they are ever blocked, they are mildly or strongly expelled by gentle laxatives <note>original: "purgatives"</note>. The waste products of the second stage are liquid, thinner, bilious, and salty; these are brought out of the veins by the kidneys and bladder, acting as conduits, and the sediment of these is the indicator or "touchstone" of urine. 

The excesses of the third stage are even thinner, and therefore for the most part they escape <note>original: "expire"</note> on their own through the smallest pores, or are carried out together with the froth of the humors as sweat. These are assisted by sweat-inducing medicines <note>original: "sudorifics"</note>, just as the previous wastes were helped by diuretics. The ancient Greeks and Romans were very concerned <note>original: "solicitous"</note> with evacuating these final wastes. For this purpose, they used many sports and exercises, such as rubbing <note>original: "chafing"</note> all the body parts in the morning, anointing with oil, wrestling, fencing, running, handball, and tennis, as well as daily washing and bathing in rivers or artificial baths. To facilitate these, many magnificent structures were built in Rome—works so grand that we can only admire them rather than imitate them. For example, the Baths of Diocletian, which mostly remain standing today, are (if I am not mistaken) decorated with paintings of Archangels; so high, sumptuous, and splendid were these works.

The types of "concoctions" or refinement stages in the preparation of metals are almost the same as those mentioned above. The first is, in its way, carried out in <term>the Great Year</term> <note>original: "annus magnus," the period of time it takes for all the planets to return to the same relative positions</note>, which is the revolution of the highest celestial sphere; the second occurs in the revolution of the lowest; and the third in the revolution of the middle sphere. 

But the philosophers <note>the author refers here to alchemists</note>, wishing to remove this mass of waste-filled excesses more conveniently through the practice of their art, also invented several methods such as washings, purifications, baths <note>original: "balneations"</note>, and steam rooms <note>original: "laconics," a type of dry sweat bath</note>. Through these, they have achieved in the "philosophical work" <note>the creation of the Philosopher's Stone</note> what physicians achieve in the human body. 

Therefore, Duenech is introduced into the bath by Pharut to sweat and evacuate the waste of the third digestion through his pores. Now, this King's illness is "atrabilious" <note>original: "atrabilary," caused by an excess of black bile or melancholy</note>, which makes him have less authority and prestige than all other princes, as he is clouded by the gloominess of Saturn and the hot-tempered <note>original: "choleric"</note> passion of Mars. He therefore has a desire either to die or be cured, if it is possible. Among many physicians, one was eventually found who took on this responsibility...

<summary>This page compares the three stages of biological digestion and waste removal to the alchemical refinement of metals, using the ancient Roman bath culture as a metaphor for "cleansing" the material. It introduces the character of King Duenech, who suffers from a melancholic imbalance and seeks a cure through these philosophical baths.</summary>

<keywords>three concoctions, digestion, alchemy, Great Year, Baths of Diocletian, Duenech, Pharut, melancholy, Saturn, Mars, sudorifics</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical allegory of the "King's Son" and his purification through bathing, following previous discussions of the philosophical work.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>

# Discourse 28

...upon himself, he was brought with entreaties and rewards. This allegory is very frequent in the writings of the philosophers, such as Bernard <note>likely Bernard of Trevisan, a famous 15th-century alchemist</note>, Pliny, the treatise of **Duenech** <note>The "Book of Duenech" is an old alchemical text often associated with the purging of metals</note>, and innumerable others. We therefore add no other details which might be derived from them; it is sufficient here to have observed which part of the waste products <note>original: "excrement"</note> may be removed by <term>balneation</term> <note>the act of bathing</note>, for herein consists the substance of the whole matter. 

Sweat trapped within the body is commonly drawn to the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the skin, along with the blood, in hot stoves or baths. Through this, a beautiful complexion is acquired for the face and the entire body. If this beauty appears, it will be a sign that the **melancholic blackness** <note>In the medical theory of the four humors, "melancholy" was the black bile; in alchemy, this refers to the *nigredo*, the initial stage of decay and blackness</note> which infects the skin may be gradually removed, and all the humors corrected, so that pure and rosy blood may afterward be produced. 

The entire temperament of his body must necessarily be improved because the current state resists healthy blood and is cold and dry. On the contrary, healthy blood is hot and moist. Whether this transformation can be achieved or not is something a physician should be able to foresee and predict through signs <note>original: "prognostics"</note>. 

Some men have been found who have mistaken **Cordon** <note>likely a symbolic figure or a specific mineral substance in this allegory</note> for the great prince or the King’s Son, but they eventually realized his true descent and education through certain signs. Lest this should happen, the artist <note>the alchemist</note> must be careful in the first place to choose the true offspring of the king. Even though he may not be in splendid and golden attire, but rather in humble and ragged <note>original: "mean"</note> clothing, and also of a leaden <note>original: "livid"</note> and dark complexion, let the artist not therefore reject him or take another in his place. For if he is very well washed, his royal **germiny** <note>origin, seed, or budding nature</note> will soon appear, just as it did with **Cyrus**, **Paris**, and **Romulus**, who were all raised among country folk. 

By way of observation, the bath must be a <term>Laconicum</term> <gloss>a vaporous and sweat-inducing bath</gloss>, meaning it must be steamy and <term>sudorific</term> <note>causing sweat</note>. This ensures the water does not burn the tender flesh or block the pores, which would cause more damage than benefit and leave his illness uncured. 

As for the King’s garments to be put on after bathing, let no man be anxious. For just as the daughter of King **Alcinous** <note>Nausicaa, who helped the hero in Homer's *Odyssey*</note> in times past provided clothes to **Ulysses** when he was shipwrecked and naked, so will there be one who will lend the King's Son most precious robes. Then, he may be deservedly acknowledged as the **Son of Sol** <note>Sol is Latin for the Sun; in alchemy, this represents gold or the perfected, solar spirit</note>.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical process of purification through the metaphor of a "King's Son" undergoing a medicinal sweat bath to remove "melancholic blackness." It emphasizes choosing the correct raw material (the true offspring) and using a gentle, vaporous heat to reveal his hidden royal and solar nature.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, purification, balneation, humors, Laconicum, King's Son, Sol, Ulysses, nigredo, sudorific</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the series of alchemical emblems, likely from Michael Maier's 1617 work *Atalanta Fugiens*, focusing here on the relationship between the Stone and the element of fire.</meta>

## Emblem 29. On the Secrets of Nature.

->As the salamander lives in fire, so also does the Stone.<-

### Epigram 29.

> The salamander plays in scorching heat,
> And, Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and metalworking, representing the intense heat of the alchemist's furnace.</note>! bids defiance to your threats;
> The Stone, likewise, being accustomed <note>original: "inured"</note> to it,
> Withstands the strongest flames, enduring forever;
> That cold and moist creature <note>original: "vermine"; in the early modern period, this term could refer to any small animal or reptile and did not always carry the modern connotation of a "pest."</note> congeals the fire,
> Your Stone, with heat, acts in harmony <note>original: "conspire"; used here in its literal sense from the Latin *conspirare*, meaning to "breathe together" or work in perfect accord.</note> as it pleases.

<summary>This emblem compares the mythical salamander's ability to survive in flames to the alchemical Stone's permanence and stability when exposed to the intense heat of the furnace.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, salamander, Vulcan, Philosopher's Stone, fire, four elements, Atalanta Fugiens, Michael Maier</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page begins Discourse 29, which discusses the relationship between living creatures and the four classical elements (air, water, earth, and fire).</meta>

# Discourse 29.

There are two elements in which animals live—water and air—and two others in which no living thing can live—earth and fire. For just as air and water possess a moderate and temperate balance <note>original: "complexion"</note> of primary and secondary qualities, the others are extreme. Their bodies are either too thick or too subtle; so dense that they do not admit other bodies, or so thin that they penetrate and burn them. 

The reason why men can live in vaults and underground <note>original: "subterranean"</note> mines is because of the air descending into them and filling the empty spaces. But here we speak of each element on its own. In water, fish live in incredible numbers, variety, and fertility—including the largest of all animals <note>likely a reference to whales</note>. In the air live humans, four-footed beasts, birds, worms, and insects. Whatever is said of spirits wandering in the depths of the earth is a different matter, for they are not animals. 

But they say the salamander alone lives in fire. Now, the salamander is a crawling creature <note>original: "vermine"</note>, not much unlike a lizard, but with a slower pace, a larger head, and different coloring. I remember seeing such creatures in the Alps, near the Spluga <note>the Splügen Pass, a mountain pass in the Alps</note>, emerging from rock caverns after thunder and rain and resting on the path. A local man there informed me that it was called *ein Molch* <note>German for a newt or salamander</note>. It has a clammy and sticky <note>original: "viscous"</note> moisture about it, by which it passes safely through fire without harm. 

But the "philosopher's salamander" <note>a mythical creature in alchemy symbolizing the element of fire</note> is quite different, though it is compared to this real one. For the mythical one is born in fire, while this real one is not; if the real one falls into fire, it is not immediately burned because of its extreme coldness and moisture, allowing it to pass freely through the flame. The mythical one is hot and dry, while the real one is cold and moist. For everything shares the nature of its origin <note>original: "mothers wombe"</note> or resembles its environment. Fire produces nothing but what is hot and dry like itself; on the contrary, the moist and cold caverns of rocks, being full of water, bring forth this cold and moist creature. The mythical salamander rejoices in fire because they share a similar nature; the real one extinguishes fire or repels its force for a time by being its opposite. 

They say that the fly called the <term>pyrausta</term> <note>from the Greek for "fire-born," a mythical insect said to live in fire</note> is generated in fire and flies out of the brass furnaces of Cyprus; but no man has seen this to be true, except as an allegory. For fire, if it is sustained, destroys and ruins <note>original: "corrupts"</note> the bodies of all animals whatsoever. It also burns earth into glass and the most solid timber into ashes, along with all other compound things—with a few exceptions, such as <term>mercurials</term> <note>in alchemy, substances containing mercury or sharing its properties, which were thought to either resist fire or evaporate entirely without breaking into separate parts</note>, which either remain entirely or flee from the fire altogether, without their parts being separated. 

Vulcan <note>the Roman god of fire and metalworking</note> is a most cruel murderer, who brings all things mixed from the elements to his trial. Some things are exempt from his judgment as the privileges and prerogatives of Empress Nature. He gains no power over these by himself, unless he joins to himself other...

<summary>This page discusses how different animals inhabit the elements of air and water while explaining why life cannot exist in pure earth or fire. The author contrasts the biological salamander of the Alps with the mythical alchemical salamander and describes the destructive power of fire (personified as Vulcan) over most matter.</summary>

<keywords>elements, salamander, fire, air, water, Splügen Pass, alchemy, Vulcan, pyrausta, biology, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Salamander" as an alchemical symbol for the Philosopher's Stone, specifically focusing on its ability to withstand fire through a perfect balance of elements.</meta>

# Discourse 29

...judges, and Salamanders are above by violence, which they do not fear. Avicenna, in his *Porta* <note>original: "porta"; likely referring to a specific alchemical text or "gate" attributed to the Persian physician Avicenna</note>, reckons up the various temperaments of bodies, which are all unequal and therefore corruptible by fire and other injuries. However, he affirms one to be exactly equal, which has as much heat as cold, and as much siccity <term>dryness</term> as moisture—not according to weight, but according to "justice," as physicians term it. This is that which is more patient <term>passive or enduring</term> than agent <term>active</term>. In this substance, if fire endeavors to dissolve water into its familiar element, air, the earth does not permit this resolution because it is incorporated with the water. The internal fire of the compound, by consent, approves of this sentiment of the earth, because it is an intimate friend to the earth.

The judgment of Vulcan <note>the Roman god of fire, often used as a metaphor for the alchemist's furnace or the transformative power of heat</note> therefore ceases. He attempts yet another ancient trick: striving to burn the earth into ashes, as is his custom, but the water adhering to it prevails against expephory <note>likely a variant of "ecphora," referring to the carrying away or separation of parts</note>. This water shows itself united to the earth, with the air joined to it, and fire joined to the earth on the other side. Therefore, he that would incinerate <term>burn to ashes</term> the earth must also incinerate the other elements. And so Vulcan, being disappointed, suspends his judgment, lest he should be derided by all men. 

This body is like the real Salamander, in which the elements are exactly equalized in virtue. Of it, the *Rosary* <note>referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> says, quoting Geber: "That philosopher would have the substance of Mercury mortified <term>in alchemy, 'mortification' refers to the chemical 'death' or destruction of a substance's outward form to release its inner potential</term>, but naturally by Mercury which is in that venerable Stone, as is manifest to all men." Therefore, etc. Also, that philosopher would have the substance of Mercury fixed, as is evident because he set forth the ways of fixing it with many cautions and devices. But who doubts that the substance of that precious stone is fixed? Certainly no man who knows it. By this, it is demonstrated that the Stone must be reduced to the nature of the Salamander by fixation—that is, absolute fixity, which does not decline or refuse the fire.

For neither can it be the Salamander before it has learned to endure fire most patiently, which must of necessity be effected over a long process of time. Hereafter, in the 35th emblematical discourse, we will treat of Achilles and Triptolemus <note>figures from Greek mythology who were placed in fire to grant them immortality or divine strength</note>, who were placed in the nighttime under embers of fire until they could endure most vehement heat. They also attained to a Salamandrine property by custom and habit, for custom is a second nature. This habit, notwithstanding, must communicate power and, like a mistress, begin the alteration; otherwise, custom will be able to effect little or nothing. 

That is the reason why it is impossible to fix ice at a fire, but possible with crystal, because nature has already begun this process. The same must be supposed of watery and volatile Mercury, which in its own nature cannot be fixed except by marriage and coition <term>union</term> with Sulphur, which is the philosopher's tincture <term>a potent alchemical substance capable of transforming and "fixing" other materials</term> and fixes all volatile spirits.

<summary>This page explains that the Philosopher's Stone attains the nature of a Salamander through a perfect, "just" balance of the four elements, allowing it to withstand the destructive power of fire. It argues that this state of absolute fixity is achieved through a long process of habituation and the union of Mercury with Sulphur.</summary>

<keywords>Salamander, Avicenna, Vulcan, alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, fixation, Mercury, Sulphur, Geber, Rosarium Philosophorum, Achilles, Triptolemus, four elements, siccity</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
## Emblem 30. Regarding the Secrets of Nature.

->Luna <note>In alchemy, Luna represents the Moon and the metal silver, often personified as the feminine principle.</note> is as necessary to Sol <note>In alchemy, Sol represents the Sun and the metal gold, often personified as the masculine principle.</note> as a hen is to a rooster.<-

### Epigram 30.

What can you do, dear Sol, alone, if I
Do not join with you as a hen with a rooster to multiply?
Or can I, Luna, express the gifts of Nature
Without your aid? No; grant me some assistance <note>original: "redresse"</note>.
He is but a fool who keeps those separate
Whom Nature would have joined in mind and heart.

<vocab>Sol, Luna, nature, alchemy, emblem, epigram</vocab>

<summary>This emblem uses the biological metaphor of a rooster and hen to illustrate the alchemical theory that "masculine" (gold/Sol) and "feminine" (silver/Luna) elements must be combined to create new matter. It argues against the separation of these natural opposites, emphasizing their mutual dependency in the "Great Work" of alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Sol, Luna, chemical wedding, masculine and feminine principles, natural philosophy, Michael Maier</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical discourse on the "Philosophical Egg," specifically focusing on the necessity of both male and female principles (the cock and the hen) and the diligent care required in the heating process.</meta>

# Discourse 30.

Avicenna <note>original: "Avicenne," a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians and philosophers of the Islamic Golden Age</note> in his *Book of the Soul* mentions several times that no eggs are to be used for the Art <note>the alchemical process</note> except for those from hens that have been with a cock. This means that the feminine subject is of no use without the masculine virtue, and conversely, a cock is not profitable without a hen. Both of these sexes must be joined together in the philosophical couple <note>original: "coupe," referring to the alchemical pairing or the vessel of union</note> so that multiplication may occur. 

The philosophers indeed use the analogy of a cock because, in the first place, he corresponds more closely to the power of Sulphur than any other male bird. One cock is able to serve many hens and does not easily tolerate a rival on the same dunghill, believing himself suitable and sufficient for everyone. He is the bird of Mars, transformed—as the poets recount in their fables—from the youth Gallus <note>In Greek mythology, Alectryon (Gallus) was a youth stationed by Mars to watch for the sun; he fell asleep, and as punishment, was turned into a cock to crow at the sun's arrival</note>, who was entrusted to watch for Sol <note>the Sun</note> so that he might not discover the adultery of Mars with Venus. He is very martial in combat, fighting with his adversary even to the death. In the philosophical work, he represents Sol, just as the hen represents Luna <note>the Moon</note>. There is, therefore, the same necessity of joining Sol with Luna as there is a cock with a hen. 

The cock is also consecrated to Sol, rejoicing at his approach and going to sleep with him. He often looks up toward heaven and raises his <unclear>combs</unclear> <note>original: "houkes," likely referring to the hackles or the curved tail feathers of a rooster</note> with his tail held upright. He fights against serpents in defense of his hens. He is a welcome messenger of light and of Latona <note>the mother of the twins Apollo (the Sun) and Artemis (the Moon) in mythology</note>, being present at her labor. Latona indeed brought forth Sol and Luna, and therefore the cock is appropriate to both the mother and the son. But Sol, Luna, and Latona agree in alchemical subjects, just as the cock and hen do. 

These birds also proceed from an egg and, in turn, lay eggs for the production of young chickens. In the same way, the philosophers' eggs turn into birds of the same kind, provided they are kept warm with a temperate heat, like the heat of a brooding hen, which endures continually. For while among other birds the male also sits upon the eggs, the cock alone shows himself free from this duty or burden. Therefore, all the care and effort of hatching the eggs and producing chickens lies with the hen. 

Her diligence and dedication can be observed in how quickly she eats and drinks and relieves herself, running back immediately to her eggs before they grow cold. Then, consider with what force and violence she defends her chickens; with what tenderness she receives and covers their naked bodies under her wings; with what a voice, like a bell, she calls and keeps them together; and with what care she breaks the harder grains or seeds for them and offers them with her beak as if on a knife. This work of nature is worthy of admiration. All these things are so that man may lack neither eggs nor chickens for food. In the same manner, the philosopher or artist most diligently and providently...

<summary>This discourse uses the natural behavior and mythology of the cock and hen to illustrate the alchemical necessity of joining masculine (Sulphur/Sol) and feminine (Mercury/Luna) principles and the constant, temperate heat required for "hatching" the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>Avicenna, Sulphur, Sol, Luna, Mars, Gallus, Latona, alchemical marriage, philosophical egg, brooding heat, symbolic birds</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<page-num>97</page-num>
# Discourse 30.

...proceed in all his operations; for she <note>original: "her"; likely referring to the personified "Nature" or the metaphorical hen mentioned later</note> picks up eggs where a cock has been, testing them to see if they are addled <note>original: "adds"; infertile or rotten eggs</note>. Afterward, she cleans, prepares, and arranges them in her vessel as if in nests, and provides the necessary heat, whereby the subjects might daily act and be acted upon <note>original: "suffer"; in this context, to undergo a process or change</note> by one another. This continues until, after a long time passing through various colors, they come to a single color and essence. In this operation, solution, coagulation, sublimation, ascension, descension, distillation, calcination, and fixation <note>original: "fixion"; the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</note> are perfected as intermediate steps. 

For a hard and compact thing cannot be altered; therefore, solution is the prerequisite <note>original: "premises"</note>, so that the matter may be made liquid and soft. And where a thing is dissolved, it is also necessary for it to be coagulated—not to its former hardness, but to a honey-like tractability. Sublimation separates the pure from the impure and makes a lowly thing more worthy, raising the inferior to the superior. For this reason, sublimation should never be lacking, for it acts as the mistress and director of all the other steps. When sublimation is performed, some part rises higher, which is "ascension," and another part falls back down, which is "descension." 

Distillation also clarifies the whole substance by being frequently repeated <note>original: "interposed"</note>, and that which remains at the bottom is calcined <note>reduced to powder by heat</note>. Then both are fixed, and so the work is completed. But if a man gathers all these particular operations under one general term, which is <term>coction</term> <note>the process of "cooking" or ripening through steady heat</note>, he will not wander far from the truth. For just as many individual chickens, which run here and there, resort to one mother or nurse (the hen), so do these many several ways of operating converge into the "womanly work"—that is, coction alone. 

It is <term>Luna</term> <note>the alchemical symbol for Silver or the Feminine principle</note> who comes to be exalted to the sublimity of <term>Sol</term> <note>the alchemical symbol for Gold or the Masculine principle</note>, for whose sake all these things are done. That is, a durable matrimony is intended between <term>Sol</term> and <term>Luna</term>. If this is effected, all <term>ligations</term> <note>bindings</note>, <term>desponsations</term> <note>betrothals</note>, <term>copulation</term> <note>unions</note>, <term>dubitation</term> <note>uncertainty or hesitation</note>, and so on will cease. The body and flesh of both will become one: a mutual and constant love, eternal peace, and an indissoluble alliance. 

<term>Sol</term> without <term>Luna</term> is of no great esteem, and <term>Luna</term> without <term>Sol</term> is of a mean and sordid condition. But she receives splendor and dignity, along with strength and constancy of mind and body, from <term>Sol</term> her husband; and <term>Sol</term> receives from <term>Luna</term> the multiplication of offspring and the propagation of his kind. Whereupon the <term>Rosary</term> <note>Referring to the "Rosarium Philosophorum," a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> says: "If only one of them should be in our Stone, the medicine would never flow easily, nor yield the tincture; and if it should yield it, it would not stain <note>original: "tinge"</note>, unless the remainder was also present, and the <term>Mercury</term> would evaporate in smoke because it would have no receptacle for the tincture." And <term>Geber</term> <note>An influential 8th-century Arabic alchemist (Jabir ibn Hayyan)</note>, in his book of examinations, proves that if <term>Sol</term> and <term>Luna</term> are incorporated together by art, they are not easily separated.

<summary>This page compares the alchemical process to a hen hatching eggs, explaining how various chemical stages like distillation and sublimation are all part of a single process called "coction." It concludes by describing the alchemical "marriage" of Gold (Sol) and Silver (Luna) as necessary for creating the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, coction, Sol and Luna, distillation, sublimation, Rosarium Philosophorum, Geber, chemical wedding, tincture</keywords>

## Emblem 31. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->The King swimming in the sea cries out with a loud voice: he that<-
->will deliver me shall have a great reward.<-

---

### Epigram 31.

> The King, weighed down <note>original: "oppressed"</note> by a heavy crown,
> Floating in the sea and likely to drown,
> Cries out for help: "Oh, help without delay,
> And I with thanks your service will repay.
> Be wise, and restore me to my kingdom,
> So that I may show favor to the rich and poor."

<summary>This emblem uses the allegory of a drowning king to symbolize a stage in the alchemical process—likely the dissolution of matter—where the "royal" substance must be rescued and restored to a purified state to grant its rewards.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Emblem 31, King, Sea, Dissolution, Transformation, Secrets of Nature, Epigram</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse 31, discussing the importance of both physical and intellectual education, using the examples of historical figures and alchemical allegories.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>Discourse 31.</header>

In ancient times, the first basic elements <term>rudiments; the first principles or basic stages of a subject</term> of all education were the knowledge of swimming and instruction in literacy. Because of this, there was a proverb about an ignorant and unrefined man: that he could neither swim nor read. For the ancients considered swimming as a means to frequently save and deliver the body from the danger of water, and the knowledge of learning to save the mind from the "waves of fortune." Furthermore, swimming is very useful in times of war, while learning is useful in times of peace at home. 

Just as we observe that animals have weapons ready and provided by nature, man is given hands and intelligence <note>original: "witt"</note> instead to defend against external violence—intelligence to invent weapons, and hands to make and use them. Similarly, the ability to swim is naturally ingrained in those same animals, but not in humans. Even when young, animals often escape from the water by swimming, while a man, no matter how <insert>ever so</insert> strong, may be drowned. It was therefore necessary to require the exercise of swimming for children, as it is often essential to preserving a man's life; thus, what was lacking by nature might be supplied by the use of skill <note>original: "art"</note>. 

Noblemen, princes, and kings have used this same exercise for the protection of their bodies, for those descended from noble blood are not entirely exempt from the whims of fortune, but are exposed to them as much as other men. If **Dionysius** <note>Dionysius II, the tyrant of Syracuse (Sicily), who was exiled to Corinth in 343 BCE.</note> had known neither swimming nor learning when he was driven out of the kingdom of **Sicily** as a tyrant, he would have perished in the waves of the sea after being shipwrecked in the **Corinthian** gulf. But escaping the danger by swimming, he went to **Corinth**, where he set up a school to teach boys and profess human learning. From being a king, he became a schoolmaster, holding a rod <term>ferule; a flat ruler used by teachers for punishment</term> <note>original: "ferur"</note> instead of a scepter. From this came the mocking proverb: "**Dionysius of Corinth**." 

In like manner, if the royal Son of the philosopher <note>Likely an alchemical reference to the "King" or the "Philosopher's Son" representing the processed matter in the Great Work.</note> had not been able to swim, no one would have heard his voice or noticed him, as he would have been drowned in the water for a long time. Swimming, therefore, is necessary and helpful to men of all ranks. For though it cannot immediately deliver a man from the surges of the vast ocean, it buys him time so that he may be rescued by others. 

The wise King sustains himself for a long time and cries out for help, though he is heard or seen by very few because the sea is so vast and remote. By chance, he has reached a rock or great stone while swimming, which preserves him if the waves prevail. But the question is: what is the sea? I answer that it is the **Erythraean** (or Red) Sea, located near the Tropic of Cancer. In this sea, because there are great quantities of magnets <note>lodestones</note> at the bottom, it is not safe for ships reinforced with or carrying iron to sail; for they might easily be drawn to the bottom by the power of the magnets. The wise King, being ignorant of this, survived alone by swimming after the ship sank and the others perished. His crown, shining with bright <term>orient; lustrous and high-quality</term> rubies, stays with him, by which he might easily be recognized and restored to his...

<summary>This page argues for the necessity of swimming and literacy as essential life skills, citing the historical example of the tyrant Dionysius. It transitions into an alchemical allegory regarding a King shipwrecked in a magnetic "Red Sea."</summary>
<keywords>swimming, education, Dionysius II, Syracuse, Corinth, Erythraean Sea, magnets, alchemy, Red Sea, rubies</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "royal Son," an alchemical allegory for the transformation of base matter into the Philosopher's Stone, here depicted as a king being rescued from the sea and restored to health.</meta>

### Discourse 31.

Kingdom. But what are those good things which your royal Son is able or willing to bestow on the person by whom he is restored to his own kingdom? Certainly they are not such things as Ptolemy, the last King of Egypt, bestowed on Pompey (who had restored Ptolemy's father to the throne of Egypt)—namely, treachery and death. Instead, he offers health, the removal of disease, the preservation of life, the provision of necessities, a cornucopia <note>original: "horne of plenty"</note>, honor, and love. Since these are not common or lowly things, but the chief provisions <note>original: "viatick," referring to supplies for a journey or for life</note> and ornaments of this life, who but a man of brass <note>meaning someone unfeeling or cold-hearted</note> would not desire them? 

Who would not stretch out his hand and pull him into the boat? But he must ensure that while helping him, the King's crown <note>original: "Diademe"</note> does not fall into the sea. For if it did, he could hardly be recognized as the King or received by his subjects, because the venerable red gemstone <note>original: "pyropus," an ancient term for a bright red gemstone or a gold-bronze alloy</note> would perish, and the <term>Bezoar stone</term> <note>a stone-like mass found in the stomachs of animals, believed to be a universal antidote to poison</note>, which guarantees health to everyone, would be lost. 

The *Rosary* <note>original: "The Rosary," referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note>, alluding to this, quotes Aristotle <note>likely referring to pseudo-Aristotelian alchemical texts popular in the Middle Ages</note> with these words: "Choose a stone by which Kings are revered in their crowns, and by which physicians can cure their patients, because it is close to fire; for without medicinal power, a crown would have no value." 

But what must be done for the King now that he has been rescued? He must be cleared of the water he swallowed by using <term>sudorifics</term> <note>medicines used to induce sweating</note>; he must be warmed from the cold by the heat of fire; he must be relieved of numbness and physical stupor by moderately hot baths; he must be cured of hunger and lack of food by providing a proper diet; and he must be healed of other external ailments by their opposites—that is, by wholesome remedies. Then he must be provided with a royal marriage, and in due time, a most promising heir will be fathered by him. This heir will be most welcome to all people, most beautiful and productive, and will exceed all his ancestors in power, kingdom, dominion, subjects, wealth, and riches. He will subdue his enemies not through war, but through humanity; not by tyranny, but by the mercy <note>original: "clemency"</note> that is natural and unique to him.

<summary>This text uses the allegory of a rescued king to describe the final stages of the alchemical process, where the "Son" (the Stone) is purified of excess moisture and "restored" to produce a powerful, medicinal heir. It emphasizes that the true value of sovereignty and alchemy lies in the power to heal and show mercy rather than in mere wealth or tyranny.</summary>

## Emblem 32. On the Secrets of Nature.

->Just as coral grows under water and is hardened by the air,<-
->so also is the Stone.<-

### Epigram 32.

In the Sicilian waves, a sea plant grows,
Shooting forth many moist and pliant <note>original: "plyant," meaning flexible or easily bent</note> boughs:
It is called coral; its nature is such,
That, if exposed to the air, it changes much,
Becoming a blood-red rock: which alone
Exactly represents the Sophic Stone <term>Sophic Stone: The "Philosophers' Stone," the legendary substance in alchemy believed to be capable of turning base metals into gold and providing a universal cure.</term>.

<vocab>Coral, Sophic Stone, Sicilian waves, Sea plant, nature</vocab>

<summary>This emblem uses the natural phenomenon of coral—which transforms from a soft aquatic plant into a hard red stone when exposed to air—as a metaphor for the final hardening and perfection of the Philosophers' Stone.</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discourse on the nature of the Philosopher's Stone, specifically arguing for its "vegetable" or growing properties by comparing it to natural wonders like coral and amber.</meta>

# Discourse 32.

->Page 102<-

The philosophers call their Stone "vegetable" <note>original: "Stone vegetable"; in alchemy, this refers to the Stone's ability to grow or multiply like a living plant</note> because it vegetates, grows, is increased, and multiplies like a plant. To the ignorant, this indeed seems strange and contrary to the truth, as it is manifest that stones neither vegetate nor grow in this manner, nor do they relate at all to meltable <note>original: "liquable"</note> metals. But they are deceived in their judgment; for they suppose that what is unknown to them does not exist in the nature of things <note>original: *in rerum natura*</note>, measuring the immensity of the universe only by their own limited capacity. 

For who could ever have believed that a stone should grow under water, or that a plant generated there could become a stone, if experience together with the credible testimony of writers had not confirmed it? Where is that petrifying <note>the process of turning into stone</note> power, where is that coloring property <note>original: "tinging virtue"</note> which hardens and tints coral, whether in the water, in the air, or in the earth? We may reasonably believe it to be, as they affirm, a soft and flexible plant while it is under water, yet of a very terrestrial nature. When it is removed and exposed to the cold wind, it becomes hard and acquires a stony tangibility; the abundant watery part is dried up by the cold and dry air (for those South winds blowing do introduce humidity) and the terrestrial body remains congealed with cold, and finally by <gloss>the</gloss> quality of the earth. For constriction <note>the process of tightening or solidifying</note> appertains to the earth alone, not to the water or air, as the proper and genuine virtue of either.

Besides, the sea in other places yields three medicinal stones derived partly from the vegetable kingdom, partly from the animal, or rather partly from the secrets of nature: pearls, amber, and ambergris. The production of pearls and the way of harvesting them have been known to us, but not the origins of the rest. Amber is found and gathered on the sea coasts of Sudavia <note>likely Samland, a region on the Baltic coast famous for amber</note> on the shores after a most vehement South or westerly wind. It undoubtedly boils out of the veins of the earth into the sea, or is washed out by the water and driven by the waves to the shores. For we have seen some veins of iron and silver swimming <note>floating or suspended</note> with the amber, which could not happen except in <gloss>the</gloss> earth. 

But as to the flies, gnats, spiders, butterflies, frogs, and serpents which are seen in some pieces (as we have had 120 beads carved <note>original: "turn'd"</note> out of amber, every one of which contained some flies, gnats, spiders, and butterflies—and <gloss>one</gloss> particularly so, not without a singular miracle of nature), that happens by the influence and imagination of the heavens, as we have demonstrated elsewhere. That ambergris is found after the same manner on the shores of the East and West Indies cannot be denied; and though some declare it to be the juice or gum of trees, like the amber aforesaid, yet those who conceive it to proceed out of veins of the earth judge more probably. For fruitful trees of amber have been seen in no place; which, if they did exist, would most certainly grow out of the water in the open air. We...

<summary>The author explains why the Philosopher's Stone is called "vegetable" by comparing its growth to coral and the formation of amber and ambergris from the earth's "veins." He argues that natural phenomena often seem impossible to the unlearned, using the preservation of insects in amber as an example of nature's "miracles."</summary>

<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, vegetable stone, petrifaction, coral, amber, ambergris, Sudavia, Samland, spontaneous generation, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the natural and philosophical properties of minerals and organic-like stones, specifically focusing on the comparison between common coral and the "Philosophical Stone."</meta>

### Discourse 32.

Therefore, ascribe both sorts of amber <note>original: "Ambar"</note> to subterranean veins or stones, just as pearls are ascribed to zoophytes <term>organisms, such as sponges or corals, that were historically thought to be a middle nature between animals and plants</term> and coral to vegetables. The philosopher’s stone is indeed like these stones, and especially like coral; for as coral grows in water and draws nourishment from the earth, so also the philosophical stone grows in mercurial water <term>a symbolic alchemical solvent or "prime matter"</term>. From this water, it receives whatever is earthy for its own augmentation, while the superfluous moisture evaporates.

A red color is also fixed in coral by coagulation, which modern authors call the "tincture of coral." This is no different than in the physical stone <note>the philosopher's stone</note>, which becomes red in its final coagulation and appears like the reddest coral, which is the tincture. But just as coral becomes hard through cold and fixity, so does the stone through heat and humidity. When these qualities are increased, the stone likewise dissolves, contrary to the nature of other stones; other stones do indeed dissolve, but they turn into glass, a result that is not at all compatible with this stone.

And as coral is prepared into several medicines of great virtue, so also has the "philosophical coral" transferred the virtues of all herbs into itself. Thus, it alone is able to perform as much as all the medicines made from all vegetables combined. For the celestial Sun, which infuses medicinal virtue and efficacy into vegetables, seems to contribute to this stone and the terrestrial realm twice as much as to all others. This is the philosophical vegetable, animal, and mineral coral, which lies hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> in the vast ocean and is unknown, unless it is exposed to the eyes and put into the hands of the ignorant.

But it must be taken out of the water with very great caution, lest it lose its juice and blood, leaving nothing but a terrestrial chaos without its true form. For the entire difficulty of taking coral consists in this, besides which there is scarcely any other: I mean the removal of the superfluous moisture that binds the stone. Unless this moisture is separated, it prevents the coralline redness from appearing and does not allow for coagulation while it remains within.

***

<insert>
Avoid all disputes as much as possible.
Beware of all unruly passions.
Crave divine protection.
Demonstrate your care by improvement.
</insert>

<summary>This discourse compares the growth and properties of natural coral to the creation of the philosopher's stone, emphasizing the importance of removing "superfluous moisture" to achieve the stone's final red tincture. It concludes with a list of moral maxims for the practitioner.</summary>

## Emblem 33: On the Secrets of Nature.

->The Hermaphrodite, lying like a dead man in darkness, lacks <note>original: "wants"</note> fire.<-

### Epigram 33.

> See how the cold and dry Hermaphrodite <note>In alchemy, the Hermaphrodite or "Rebis" is a symbol of the completed union of opposites—masculine and feminine, or spirit and matter—but here it is shown in an inactive, "dead" state.</note>,
> Appears as dismal as a ghost by night.
> Fire is the thing he lacks <note>original: "wants"</note>; if you give him that,
> His spirit, feeling warmth, will soon revive.
> The Stone's <term>the Philosopher's Stone</term> whole virtue lies in the god Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and metalworking, representing the external heat of the furnace required for alchemical transformation.</note>,
> As does Sulphur in Sol <note>The Sun, representing Gold.</note>, and Mercury in Luna <note>The Moon, representing Silver.</note>.

<vocab>Hermaphrodite, Vulcan, Sulphur, Sol, Luna, Mercury</vocab>

<summary>This emblem describes the Hermaphrodite (the union of opposites) in a state of darkness and cold, emphasizing that the transformative power of the Philosopher's Stone requires the application of fire, symbolized by the god Vulcan.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Hermaphrodite, Rebis, Vulcan, Philosopher's Stone, Sulphur, Sol, Luna, Mercury, Elemental Fire</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical discourse on the role of heat and transformation, specifically comparing biological hibernation and mythological rebirth to the stages of the alchemical Great Work.</meta>

### Discourse 33.

It is one of the secrets of nature that in the cold of winter, frogs and toads lie as if they were dead under the water. In the spring, the new heat of the sun operating upon them allows them to recover their senses and motion, or to perform the actions of a living being. But if they are found in the water during the winter time and brought into the air or a warm house, they immediately begin to move themselves as they do in the summer. From this, it appears that they lack nothing but external heat to excite their natural internal heat and bring it into action. 

In the same manner, the philosophers speak of their **Hermaphrodite** <note>In alchemy, the "Hermaphrodite" (or Rebis) represents the union of opposing principles—usually sulfur and mercury—which must be brought together to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, who, if he appears dead while lying in darkness, requires the heat of fire. He is said to lie in "darkness" when he is lost in a dark and most cold winter night—that is, remaining in blackness <note>original: "blacknesse"; this refers to *nigredo*, the first stage of the alchemical process involving decay and purification.</note>, which is a sign of coldness. From this state, he ought to be brought to whiteness by a greater intensity of fire, and by an even greater intensity, to redness <note>These colors refer to the stages of *albedo* (whiteness) and *rubedo* (redness), signaling the perfection of the alchemical matter.</note>. 

For, as Bodillus says in the *Turba* <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum" (The Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest and most famous Latin alchemical texts.</note>, nothing is generated without heat. A bath of extreme heat causes a body to perish; if it is cold, it drives the life away; but if it is temperate, it becomes agreeable and pleasant to the body. Bonellus says in the same text: 
> "All things that live also die, if God so pleases. That nature, therefore, which is deprived of moisture and exposed by night, seems like a dead man. Then that nature requires fire until its body and spirit are turned into earth, and it becomes dust like a dead man in his tomb. When these things are accomplished, God restores a spirit and soul to it; all infirmity being taken away, our nature is comforted and improved." 

It is therefore necessary to burn that matter without fear. Fire, which destroys all other things, repairs this one; that which brings death to others gives life to this. There is only one **Phoenix** <note>A mythical bird that regenerates by burning itself to ashes and rising anew.</note> which is restored by fire, renewed by flames, and revives out of ashes. This is known to the philosopher only; the "bird" is burnt and restored to life, whatever other men dream regarding some bird that flies, residing nowhere and never seen—which is but a fable.

This **Hermaphrodite** of which the philosopher speaks is of a mixed nature, both male and female, where one passes into another by the operation of heat. The female is converted into a male, which should not seem so strange in the philosophers' work, as it is found—if we may believe historical accounts—that many humans have also changed their sex. The poets mention **Caeneus**, **Iphis**, and **Tiresias**, as Pontanus <note>original: "Pontany"; likely referring to Giovanni Pontano, a Renaissance humanist and poet.</note> does in *The Starry*:

> *They may repent of their gender and prefer to be Caeneus,*
> *Or to pass entirely into a female form.* <note>original: "Poeniteat generis tamen et se Caenea malint, / Aut in fœmineam penitus transire figuram"</note>

And Ausonius <note>Decimus Magnus Ausonius, a Roman poet.</note>:

> *Caeneus, recalled, changes back into his former shape.* <note>original: "Mutat in antiquam Caeneus revocata figuram." In myth, Caeneus was born a woman named Caenis and was transformed by Poseidon into an invulnerable man.</note>

So, in the consulship of **Quintus Crassus** and **Caius Cassius Longinus**...

<summary>This page uses the hibernation of frogs and the myth of the phoenix to explain how alchemical matter must be "killed" and "revived" through controlled heat. It also introduces the concept of the alchemical Hermaphrodite and provides mythological precedents for the transformation of gender as a metaphor for the transformation of matter.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Hermaphrodite, phoenix, nigredo, albedo, rubedo, Turba Philosophorum, gender transformation, Caeneus, heat in alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>Discourse 33.</header>

At Casinum <note>An ancient Roman town, now Cassino, Italy.</note> a boy was born of a virgin. And Licinius Mucianus, as recorded in Pliny, relates that he had seen one Aristontes at Argos, also called by the name Aristusa, who was married, and soon after discovered a beard and virility, and married a wife. Pliny himself says that in Africa he saw Lucius Cossutius <note>original: "Cossitium"</note>, a citizen of Tisidium <note>original: "Thritano"</note>, changed into a woman <note>Pliny's original text usually describes girls turning into men; the author or OCR may have inverted this.</note> on the day of marriage. It is certain that those things are true, and might be proved by many other examples, if need were. For the genital or male parts are thrust out of the body by the increase of heat. 

For a woman, being much colder than a man and having those things hidden <note>original: "absconded"</note> inwardly which a man has outwardly—as physicians say—nature was hereupon uncertain whether she should generate a man or a woman. She has expressed a woman outwardly, though inwardly she conceives a man; for which reason, as heat and motion increase through age, the hidden parts break forth and show themselves visibly to all men. In the same manner among the philosophers, the woman is turned into a man when heat is increased; that is, the hermaphrodite loses the female sex and becomes a man, stout and grave, having nothing in him of effeminacy or levity. 

For we some time since saw a noble youth—a hermaphrodite <note>The word "changed" is struck through here.</note>—or rather, one promoted by the industry of the surgical <note>original: "chirurgicale"</note> art performed by that most famous physician Gaspare Tagliacozzi <note>original: "Caspar Tagliatony"</note> of Bologna <note>original: "Bononia"</note> into a perfect man, fit (as was desired) for the generating of offspring. A new hole was made through the penis <note>original: "yard"</note>, which was wanting there, and the lower opening appertaining to the woman was stopped up. 

The philosophers are not without all these manual operations: for when the coldness and moisture of Luna <note>The Moon, representing silver or the feminine principle in alchemy.</note> appears, they call that the woman; when the heat and dryness <note>original: "siccity"</note> of Sol <note>The Sun, representing gold or the masculine principle.</note>, the man; <gloss>when</gloss> all these four qualities are together <note>The word "enter" is struck through here.</note>, their body is the hermaphrodite. And this conversion of the woman may easily be made—that is, the mutation of coldness and moisture into the man—which is done by the sole heat of fire, as has been said. For heat sequesters and separates the superfluities of moisture, and will establish the idea of itself in the philosophical subject, which is the tincture <note>In alchemy, the "tincture" is the potent substance or "Stone" capable of transmuting metals and healing bodies.</note>.

<summary>This page discusses historical and medical accounts of biological sex changes (from female to male) as a natural justification for alchemical transmutation. The author relates these physical transformations to the alchemical process of turning the "cold/moist" feminine principle (Luna) into the "hot/dry" masculine principle (Sol) to reach the perfect state of the tincture.</summary>

<keywords>Pliny the Elder, Licinius Mucianus, Gaspare Tagliacozzi, hermaphrodite, sex change, alchemy, Luna and Sol, tincture, Bologna, Casinum, Aristontes</keywords>

## Epigram 34.

->Sol <note>original: "Sol"; the Sun, representing gold or the masculine principle in alchemy</note> is conceived in baths and produced in the air,<-
->And growing red, he treads upon the water without fear.<-
->Here, the philosophers' supreme delight<-
->Is tinged white upon the mountain tops;<-
->This gift of God is a stone, and yet it is not so;<-
->How happy is he <gloss>that</gloss> shall attain it!<-

<summary>This emblem describes the paradoxical stages of creating the Philosopher's Stone, illustrating its transformation through different elements and colors—from white to red—and its identity as both a physical and spiritual "stone."</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Michael Maier, Sol, Philosopher's Stone, chemical baths, transmutation, red stone, white stone</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
# Discourse 34.

Human opinion or flattery attributes miraculous births to certain people over others, though these are certainly fables. Such is the claim that Alexander the Great was sired not by Philip, King of Macedonia, but by Jupiter Ammon; or that Romulus and Remus were fathered by Mars; or that Plato was born of the virgin Perictione, who was violated by the image of Apollo. For the pagans <note>original: "heathen"</note> wanted to show that they descended from the gods, just as Thessalus—the son of the physician Hippocrates—tries among other things to convince himself that he is a descendant of Apollo at Athens. 

But we cannot easily believe those who know that the beings to whom they trace their lineage were neither men nor gods. And even if there were divine qualities among men, we believe all those magnificent actions boasted of in their works—through the flattery of subjects, disciples, or others—were fictions invented by them and introduced into public opinion through forged writings.

It is a different matter when philosophy ascribes an unusual conception and birth to its "Son" <note>The "Son" refers to the Philosopher’s Stone or the alchemical product</note>; for he has a unique quality beyond all other things born in the world, being conceived in baths and born in the air. We know that women who are barren due to excessive coldness and dryness <note>original: "siccity"</note> may be helped by hot baths so that they become fit for conception; however, that a conception should actually occur *inside* a bath is unheard of. That seems to apply to him alone, because of a wonderful power of nature far different from all other things. 

Elsewhere, they say that he must be conceived in the bottom of the vessel and born in the alembic <note>an alchemical still used for distilling liquids</note>, which is a more enlightened opinion. For if there is any water from the baths, it will be at the bottom of the vessel—neither at the top nor in the middle—while the airy vapors are in the alembic. Therefore, where conception occurs, he ascends into the alembic and appears in a white color at birth. Blackness rules at the bottom, concerning which the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> says: 

"Conception occurs when the earth is dissolved into a black powder and begins to retain some of the Mercury; for then the male acts upon the female—that is, <term>Azoth</term> <note>a term for the universal solvent or Mercury</note> upon the earth." 

And a little later: 

"Conception and betrothal <note>original: "desponsation"</note> occur during putrefaction at the bottom of the vessel, and the generation of the things produced will occur in the air—that is, in the head of the vessel, the alembic." 

And "conception in baths" is nothing other than putrefaction in dung <note>Alchemists often used the steady heat of fermenting dung to maintain a gentle, consistent temperature</note>; for the same *Rosary* continues: 

"The body does nothing unless it is putrefied, and it cannot be putrefied except with Mercury." 

And soon after: 

"Putrefaction must be achieved with the very gentle heat of warm, moist dung and by no other means, so that nothing ascends. If anything were to ascend, there would be a separation of the parts, which should not happen until the male and female are perfectly joined together and one has received the other. The sign of this perfect dissolution is blackness on the surface <note>original: "superfices"</note>." 

His birth is white, which is completed at the top.

<summary>This page contrasts the mythical, divine origins of historical figures like Alexander the Great with the symbolic, chemical "birth" of the alchemical Son. It details the laboratory process of conception through putrefaction at the bottom of a vessel (blackness) and its eventual birth as a white vapor in the alembic.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Alexander the Great, Rosarium Philosophorum, alembic, putrefaction, Azoth, Mercury, symbolic birth, Jupiter Ammon, Hippocrates</keywords>

# Discourse 34.

of mountains, that is, in the air or alembic <note>original: Alembike; the cap or head of a distilling apparatus</note>; which is explained by Rosinus to Euthicia <note>likely referring to the *Rosarius philosophorum*, a famous alchemical text attributed to "Rosinus," a corruption of Zosimos of Panopolis</note> as follows: The wise man says, "Take things out of their mines, and exalt them to higher places, and send them from the top of their mountains, and reduce them to their roots." And afterwards: But by "mountains" he signifies cucurbits <note>original: Cucurbits; gourd-shaped glass vessels used in distillation</note>, and by the "tops of mountains" he signifies alembics. And "to send" is, by way of analogy, to receive their water through an alembic into a receiver; and "to reduce to the roots" is to return them to that from which they proceed. And he names cucurbits "mountains" because Sol <note>the Sun, representing gold</note> and Luna <note>the Moon, representing silver</note> are found in mountains; for also in the "mountains"—which are the cucurbits—their Sol and Luna are generated. 

These are his words. Afterward, he becomes red and begins to go upon the waters—that is, upon metals melted by fire, which exist in the form of a mercurial water. For he is the lord of waters, over which he exercises authority like Neptune, and is the King of the Sea and the possessor of mountains. 

Regarding Xerxes, King of Persia, they report that while on an expedition into Greece, he sent an embassy to the Sea and to the mountain Athos, requesting that they do him no harm—the former by the force of waves, the latter by fire—otherwise, he threatened to take revenge upon them both. But the elements remained deaf to him: the Sea drowned some of his ships, and Athos destroyed many with fire. Being angry, the King—acting as if he were lord of the sea and mountains—commanded a certain number of lashes to be inflicted upon the former, and he cast a great part of the mountain into the sea. But these actions demonstrate the rashness rather than the prudence of such a great King. 

The one of whom we speak <note>referring to the alchemical substance or the Philosopher's Stone</note> purges all waters of obstacles and impurities—not by decree, but by action—and freely passes through them. Furthermore, he congeals them so that their hardness can bear his chariot where ships sailed before. He makes mountains level with valleys and does not fear the flames of fire; therefore, he goes freely wherever he pleases, from the Pillars of Hercules <note>traditionally the Strait of Gibraltar</note> in the farthest part of India to the Pillars of Dionysius <note>symbolizing the eastern boundaries of the known world</note>.

<summary>This page explains the alchemical symbolism of mountains and seas as metaphors for distillation vessels and chemical states, contrasting the true power of the alchemical "King" with the futile vanity of King Xerxes.</summary>

> Achilles and Triptolemus, having been hardened,

<summary>This emblem uses the Greek myths of Ceres and Thetis—goddesses who attempted to grant immortality to children by placing them in fire—as an allegory for alchemical transmutation. It argues that fire is the essential "nourishment" required to perfect the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>Discourse 35.</header>

Lycurgus, the lawmaker of the Spartans, demonstrated to the people in an assembly at the theater by a visual example how far custom and habit—whether good or evil—may prevail. He brought in two whelps <term>young dogs or puppies</term> produced from one litter. Between them both, he set a pot full of meat and a hare. One of these puppies, seeing the food and the hare, left the food and pursued the hare because he was so accustomed; but the other, disregarding the hare, emptied the pot because he was also so accustomed. When this was done, Lycurgus said, "Behold, here you see through these little ones how far education and custom may prevail, even in those which nature has produced exactly alike and from the same litter." In this manner, therefore, it is proper to rectify or direct nature toward better things, for nature is like wax, easily flexible toward either vice or virtue.

What has been demonstrated to be true in politics, the philosophers agree is also true in the natural sciences <note>original: "physicks"</note>. Daily examples of how effective custom is in men and animals are seen throughout the whole world. Many examples are also obvious in vegetables, but in minerals and metallic bodies we do not have as much experience. Nevertheless, alchemists prepare their stone through "custom" in a fire agreeable to it, as they signify in innumerable places. For it must be nourished by fire, just as a child is nourished by milk at its mother’s breast. Upon this, Emiganus says: "Behold the sucking babe, and do not hinder him." And Bodillus says: "The newborn infant is nourished by milk and fire alone; little by little while he is very young, the more he is burned, the more his bones are strengthened <note>original: "comforted"</note>, and he is brought to youth. Upon attaining youth, he is able to provide for himself." And Arnold <note>likely Arnold of Villanova</note> in the *Rosary* <note>original: "Rosary" – referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous alchemical treatise</note>, book 2, chapter 7, says: "Yet the medicine must be roasted by fire for a long time and nourished like a child at the breast."

The most ancient philosophers were pleased to demonstrate these same things through the allegories of Triptolemus and Achilles, who were accustomed to lying in and being hardened by fire. Both of these figures denote nothing else but the chemical subject <note>the material used to create the Philosopher's Stone</note>; otherwise, the fable would be insignificant and absurd, unworthy of being applied to moral lessons or repeated to the ears of the learned. Ceres, acting as a nurse, fed Triptolemus with milk during the day and laid him in the fire at night. Because of this, the youth was very well nourished. His father, Eleusius, eventually noticed this, and Ceres then killed Eleusius and gave the youth Triptolemus a chariot drawn by winged dragons. With this, he passed through the air into all parts of the world and taught men how to sow grain. But this Triptolemus is actually the tincture <term>the alchemical substance used for transmutation</term> nourished by fire in a philosophical manner. Being drawn by serpents—which represent Mercury—he taught men how the...

<summary>This page uses the Spartan lawmaker Lycurgus’s experiment with puppies to argue that "custom" and "nourishment" (education) can overcome nature, applying this logic to alchemy. The author explains that the alchemical stone must be "nurtured" by fire like an infant, using the myths of Triptolemus and Ceres as allegories for chemical processes.</summary>
