<meta>This page continues the discussion of mythological figures as allegories for alchemical processes, specifically focusing on the transition from the seeds of the philosophers to the maturation of the "stone" through fire.</meta>

# Discourse 35

The philosophers' seeds are to be cast into their earth. These same things are ascribed to Osiris, who for that purpose surveyed the whole world, as we have demonstrated elsewhere, and to Dionysius <note>Dionysus</note>, who traveled throughout the world teaching men the use of wine. Now, these three—Triptolemus, Osiris, and Dionysius—share one intention and office; indeed, they are the same thing. 

The same applies to Achilles, who, being the most valiant, was to be sent to the Trojan War. His father was Peleus—meaning "earth" or the mountain Peleus—and his mother was Thetis, goddess of the sea or water; Achilles descended from them. However, at their wedding, the golden apple of Eris <note>the goddess of discord</note> was produced, which was the first cause of the Trojan War. Therefore, proceeding from those nuptials, it was reasonable that he should be the one to determine <note>original: "determine", meaning to bring to an end or decide the outcome</note> it. Achilles is said to have been hardened by his mother in the same way as the aforementioned Triptolemus was by Ceres <note>Demeter</note>. We have said enough about this in the sixth book of *Hieroglyphics*, so we think it superfluous to repeat it here.

Therefore, the nourishment of the stone <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note> is fire. However, the stone is not physically expanded by it in length, width, or depth, nor does it increase in weight. Instead, it only acquires virtue <note>original: "virtue", meaning power or inherent strength</note>, maturity, and color from the fire, carrying everything else with it as "viatic provision" <note>travel supplies or sustenance for a journey</note>. For when its parts are gathered from several places, purged, and prepared, it has within it everything it requires. Whereupon a philosopher says in the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium philosophorum*, a 16th-century alchemical treatise</note>:

> This water, once prepared, carries with it everything it needs. <note>original Latin: *Omnia fert secum quibus indiget haec aqua factum.*</note>

Indeed, no foreign substance is added to it from beginning to end, unless it is of the same nature <term>homogeneous</term>, nor is anything separated unless it is of a different nature <term>heterogeneous</term>. 

But every man, before he begins anything, ought to consider how he may come to the knowledge of those Dragons that are to be joined to the chariot of Triptolemus. These are winged and volatile. If you desire to know them, you will find them in the "philosophical dung" <note>an alchemical term for the base matter or the decaying state of the substance</note>. For they are dung, and are generated from dung, and are that vessel which Mary <note>Mary the Prophetess, a famous early alchemical authority</note> says is not "necromantic" <note>original: "negromanticale", implying black magic or sorcery</note>, but is the governance <note>original: "regiment"</note> of your fire, without which you will achieve nothing. I have revealed the truth to you, which I acquired through incredible effort from the monuments of the ancients, and not without the expense of many years.

<summary>This discourse equates the mythological figures of Triptolemus, Osiris, and Dionysius with alchemical principles and explains that the "stone" is matured by fire rather than increased in physical size. It emphasizes that all necessary components for the Great Work are found within the "philosophical dung" and the correct governance of fire.</summary>

<keywords>Triptolemus, Osiris, Dionysius, Achilles, alchemical fire, Philosopher's Stone, Rosarium philosophorum, Mary the Prophetess, philosophical dung, alchemy</keywords>

## Epigram 36.

> The Stone is thought by some to be mere waste <note>original: "refuse"</note>,
> Thrown upon the earth, revealed to both the rich and the poor.
> Some say it is located on the highest mountains;
> Some say in the air, and some say, finally, in the water.
> What they all say is true, but I urge you <note>original: "I do haue you"; here meaning "I would have you" or "I advise you."</note>
> To pursue that noble prize in the mountains.

<note>In alchemical philosophy, the "Stone" (the Philosopher's Stone) and "Mercury" (not just the liquid metal, but a spiritual principle) were said to be "everywhere and nowhere"—hidden in plain sight within the natural world and the four elements: earth, air, fire, and water.</note>

<summary>This emblem describes the omnipresence of the alchemical "Stone" across the elements of earth, air, and water, ultimately advising the seeker to hunt for this prize in the mountains.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the location and nature of the Philosopher's Stone, building on previous arguments regarding its accessibility and paradoxical value.</meta>

# Discourse 36.

All people who have heard even once of the name and power of the [Philosopher's] Stone—unless they are completely skeptical—usually ask where it is to be found, so that they might travel directly toward it. The philosophers give two answers. First, they say that Adam brought it with him out of Paradise; that it is within you, within me, and within every person; and that migrating birds carry it with them from distant countries. Second, they say that it may be found in the earth, mountains, air, and water.

Which of these should we follow? In my opinion, we should follow both, though in different respects; however, the second answer is more plausible and seems more reliable to us. It is said to be "thrown upon the earth" for two reasons. First, because the element of earth first appears as a dark and black substance. Second, because it is a thing of low and little value, which is trodden upon on the paths of travelers and even in dung itself. Regarding this, Rosarius <note>likely referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> says that if he were to name it, fools would not believe it could be the Stone. 

When Calid <note>Prince Khalid ibn Yazid, a legendary early patron of alchemy</note> asked Morienus <note>A legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist</note> and tried to learn much from him, he received this answer: "This [Stone], as a wise man says, is possessed by both the rich and the poor, both the generous and the greedy <note>original: "cowboy"; likely a transcription error for "covetous"</note>, whether walking or sitting. For it is thrown in the streets and trampled in dunghills; many men have already dug through dunghills for it and have been deceived." 

Mundus, in the *Turba* <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum*, or "Assembly of the Philosophers," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note>, also says that if the sellers knew its true value, they would not sell it at such a low price. And Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist</note> affirms that the Stone may be obtained for free, as much as a man wants, and for that reason, no one should even need to be questioned about where to find it. 

All of these things are true. For who, but a heartless wretch, would deny water and earth to someone who desires it? The ancient Cimbri <note>A Germanic tribe that migrated into Roman territory in the late 2nd century BCE</note>, having requested and been unable to obtain these same gifts from the Romans, entered Italy with a great army and slew many thousands of the Romans along with their Consuls, as histories record. 

For earth, as the mother of all things, is most precious; yet as the final state of rotting matter, it is most despicable. Nothing is more vile than clay or dirt, which is nevertheless nothing but earth mixed with water. What is more common than a clod of earth? Yet Euripylus, the son of Neptune, offered one as a gift to the heroic Argonauts; they did not refuse it but accepted it thankfully, and later, after it was dissolved in water, Medea used it to divine many things. For earth must be dissolved in water; otherwise, neither element is of any use. 

In this manner, the Stone is cast upon the earth, where it does not remain lowly and contemptible but is "exalted to the mountains," such as the flame-vomiting <note>original: "flammivomous"</note> Athos, Vesuvius, Etna, and the like. Many such mountains are seen in every part of the world, for a perpetual fire burns within them, which sublimates <note>original: "sublimeth"; the alchemical process of heating a substance into vapor to purify it</note> the Stone and raises it to the highest dignity. 

As in

<summary>This page discusses the dual nature of the Philosopher's Stone, explaining that while it is found in common, "worthless" materials like earth and dung, it is refined through "mountainous" fires into a substance of the highest value.</summary>

<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Prima Materia, Morienus, Khalid ibn Yazid, Arnold of Villanova, alchemy, elements, transmutation, volcanism, Cimbri, Argonauts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical discourse on the nature of Mercury, following previous discussions of its elemental properties and mythological associations.</meta>

# Discourse 36.

In the mountains, it grows in a raw form out of sulfur and argent-vive <note>original: "Argent-vive" (quicksilver or mercury)</note>, for it is matured and perfected at the top of the mountains. There, an herb is also produced without which fire cannot be regulated because, being cold and moist, if it is cast into fire, the intensity of the fire is restrained by its opposite. From the mountains, it passes to the air, where it finds a home. For the air becomes a house to it, wherein it is enclosed, which is nothing else but to be "carried in the belly of the wind" and born in the air—a matter of which we have spoken before.

At length, it is nourished in rivers, which is to say, Mercury in water. For that reason, the Athenians, in honor of this, instituted their *hydrophories* <note>original: "hydrophoryes" (water-carrying festivals)</note>, because the matter of the philosopher’s stone is water, as Rosarius <note>Likely referring to the "Rosarium Philosophorum," a famous alchemical text.</note> says, and this is understood as the water of these three elements. For this reason, Mercury was also said to have three heads—being marine, celestial, and terrestrial—because he resides in the water, earth, and air. 

He is reported to be educated by Vulcan and is notorious for thievery, because Mercury is said to be drawn toward fire; it is volatile and carries away with it that with which it is mixed <note>This refers to mercury's ability to form amalgams with other metals and then evaporate when heated, "stealing" the metal away.</note>. In the days of old, Mercury gave laws and discipline to the Egyptians, and even religion to the Theban priests, and thus to a great part of the world. This is because the Egyptians received their governance and sacred rites from chemical <note>original: "Chymicale"</note> instruction; from them the Greeks received it, from them the Romans, and from those many other nations, as has been demonstrated more extensively elsewhere. 

He slew Argos with a stone and converted Battus into a touchstone called *Index* <note>In mythology, Hermes (Mercury) turned the shepherd Battus into a touchstone for his betrayal.</note>. What need is there for many words? All the volumes of the chemists emphasize nothing else but Mercury and sufficiently confirm his power in this one short verse: "What wise men seek is contained in Hermes." He, therefore, must be sought until he is found, whether his harbor is in the air, fire, water, or earth. For he is in perpetual motion, running sometimes here and sometimes there for the service of the chemical gods as their footman. This office is noted by some men ascribing to him a daughter named Angelia <note>From the Greek word for "messenger" or "news."</note>...

<summary>This page explores the alchemical and mythological nature of Mercury, tracing its origins from mountain minerals to its role as the "messenger" found in all four elements and its central importance in the creation of the philosopher's stone.</summary>

### Emblem 37. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

->Three things are sufficient for the <term>Magistery</term> <note>The "Magistery" refers to the "Great Work" or the mastery of the alchemical process to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note>: the white fume (which is water), the Green Lion (which is the brass of Hermes), and <term>aqua fetida</term> <note>Latin: "foul-smelling water," often referring to a sulfurous or acidic solution used in the dissolution of metals.</note>.<-

### Epigram 37.

The Green Lion, white fume, and stinking juice;
Are all that to our process do contribute:
Water the mother is the primary element,
And the sole matter of the <term>Philosophical Essence</term> <note>original: "Sophicks Ens." "Ens" is a Latin term for the "being" or "essence" of a thing.</note>.
Your Lion is the <term>Hermetic Stone</term> and brass;
And the white fume has a watery nature.

<summary>This emblem identifies the three essential ingredients for the alchemical Great Work, using the metaphors of the Green Lion, white vapor, and "foul water" to represent specific chemical states and substances.</summary>
<keywords>Magistery, Green Lion, Aqua Fetida, Hermetic Brass, Sophic Ens, alchemy, transmutation, mercury</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the components and stages of the alchemical "Great Work," specifically focusing on the initial "foul water" and the transition to the "Green Lion."</meta>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>Discourse 37.</header>

Just as three essential things—the foundation, walls, and roof—are required for the construction of every building, and if one is missing, it cannot be perfect; so there are many desires <note>original: "desires", likely referring to the required components or steps in the process</note> needed to complete the philosopher's compound. These are named here by their proper names. The author of *Aurora* <note>Refers to the *Aurora Consurgens*, an alchemical manuscript often attributed to Thomas Aquinas</note>, chapter 20, speaking of the separation of the elements, says: "Earth is left in the same place so that the other three elements may be rooted <note>original: "radicated"</note> in it; if it were not for that, the elements would have no foundation upon which to build a new jewel house." 

This foundation is here called *Aqua foetida* <note>Latin: "foul water"</note>, which is the mother of all the elements, according to the *Rosary* <note>Refers to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a foundational 16th-century alchemical text</note>. From this, by this, and with this, the philosopher prepares the Elixir in the beginning and in the end. It is called *Aqua foetida* because it sends forth a sulfurous stench and the "smoke of the grave." This is the water that Pegasus struck out of Parnassus with his hoof, which Nonacris <note>original: "Nonatris", a mountain in Arcadia associated in myth with the poisonous River Styx</note>, a mountain of Arcadia, produces, springing out of a rock at the top; it can only be contained in the hoof of a horse because of its most excessive strength. 

This is that "Dragon's Water," as the *Rosary* calls it, which should be made by an alembic <term>an alchemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</term> without adding any other substance. In the making of it, there is an extraordinary stench. Some, having heard these words, took it upon themselves to distill the dung of humans or other animals; in this operation, they indeed experienced <unclear>feele</unclear> a most vehement stench, but found only "dung in the dung." 

But do not think the philosophers who work with "dung" are fools. You must know that the stench, if there is any, is immediately changed into a great fragrance. Raymond Lully <note>original: "Lulley", a 13th-century philosopher and reputed alchemist</note> asserts this of his Quintessence <term>the "fifth essence" or purest spiritual concentration of a substance</term>. He ascribes such a sweetness of smell to it—if it is rightly made—that if it were placed at the top of a house, it would allure flying birds to it and cause them to stop. He put his Quintessence in dung <note>Alchemists often used "balneum fimi" or a dung bath as a method of providing gentle, consistent heat through natural fermentation</note>, and through its most temperate heat, that fragrance follows. Some men tried this with strong wine, but in vain, and therefore accused Lully of vanity <note>here meaning falsehood or futility</note>. However, they themselves should rather be reproved for folly, as they never truly tasted Lully’s wine. But that "golden poet" better understood Lully, singing thus in his first book of purification:

-> But he indeed did not mean what at first he seemed to say, <-
-> Nor did he then mix distilled wines, etc. <-
<note>original Latin: "At non ille quidem sensit, quæ dicere prima / Est fatis visuj, neq[ue] tum stillantia vina / miscebat, &c."</note>

After *Aqua foetida* comes the "Green Lion." Concerning this, the *Rosary* says: "You have sought for greenness, supposing that brass was a leprous body because of the greenness it possesses. Therefore, I declare to you that whatever is perfect in brass is that greenness..."

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical foundation known as "Aqua foetida" (foul water), distinguishing its symbolic "stench" and "dung" from literal waste, and introduces the concept of the "Green Lion."</summary>
<keywords>Aqua foetida, Green Lion, Raymond Lully, Rosarium Philosophorum, Aurora Consurgens, Quintessence, alchemical distillation, symbolic stench</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the symbolic and chemical properties of substances used in alchemical transformation, specifically focusing on "greenness" and the interaction between the "Green Lion" and the "Dragon."</meta>

# Discourse 37.

...alone, which is in it: because that greenness is by our mastery <note>original: magistery; the demonstration of skill in alchemical practice or the substance produced by it</note> suddenly turned into our most true gold, and we have had experience of this. Yet you can in no way prepare the Stone without various green spirits and liquids, which are seen to spring in our mines. O blessed greenness, which generates all things! From this you must know that no vegetable and no fruit appears to germinate without a green color; in like manner, you must know that the generation of this thing is green, for which reason the philosophers call it "the bud." 

These are the words of Rosarius: <note>Likely referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rose Garden of the Philosophers), a foundational 16th-century alchemical text.</note> "This is the philosophers' gold, and brass, and stone noted in the chapters—a fume, vapor, and water; the spittle of Luna <note>original: Luna; the Moon, representing silver</note> which is joined to the light of Sol <note>original: Sol; the Sun, representing gold</note>. This Green Lion fights with the Dragon, but is overcome by him and, in time, devoured. When the Lion has putrefied <note>Rotted or decomposed as part of the alchemical process</note>, sweetness is expected to proceed from his mouth, just as when one was slain by Samson." <note>Refers to the biblical story in Judges 14:14: "Out of the eater came forth meat, and out of the strong came forth sweetness."</note> 

The Dragon, getting the upper hand, so fills himself with the Lion’s flesh that he bursts slightly and dies. From this—with the fat of a Lion alone curing daily <note>original: quotidian</note> fevers, and grace and favor being obtained between kings and people anointed with it—a most excellent medicine may be made, very useful <note>original: komodious</note> for many maladies. 

In the third place follows the "white fume," which, if it is coagulated <note>thickened or solidified</note>, becomes water and performs the office of water in washing, dissolving, and taking away spots like soap. This is that "fire against nature," which you find so named because it is contrary to nature—undoing and destroying that which nature compounded with diligent care. This fire is not temporary <note>original: endless; here meaning "quickly consumed" or "finite" in its burning</note> like the spirit of wine or oil, but is an incombustible matter of equal duration and heat. It is a fire without light or combustion, possessing great virtue and efficacy. Finding it in darkness is a thing of no little difficulty because it does not shine, but it is much more difficult to apply it rightly to the Work; the circumstances and properties of which we have sufficiently described in several places.

<summary>This page explores alchemical symbolism involving the "Green Lion" and "Dragon," explaining how "greenness" is essential for the generation of the Philosopher's Stone. It also introduces the "white fume" and the "fire against nature," a paradoxical substance used to dissolve and refine matter.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Green Lion, Dragon, Rosarium Philosophorum, Luna and Sol, Samson, fire against nature, putrefaction, medicinal fat, white fume</keywords>

# Emblem 38: Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

->Rebis, like Hermaphroditus, is produced from the two mountains of Mercury and Venus.<-

## Epigram 38.

Rebis is called a twin by the ancients,
Containing both male and female sexes within;
For the <term>Hermaphrodite</term> <note>In alchemy, this represents the "Rebis" or "two-thing," the union of the solar/masculine and lunar/feminine principles.</note> of <term>Hermes</term> <note>The Greek equivalent of the Roman god Mercury.</note> and <term>Venus</term>
Is said to have its birthright in two mountains.
Do not despise this double sex, for a King
Will spring from this very same man and woman.

<vocab>Rebis, Hermaphroditus, Mercury, Venus, Hermaphrodite, Ancients</vocab>

<summary>This emblem describes the alchemical concept of the Rebis, a dual-gendered figure representing the perfect union of masculine (Mercury/Hermes) and feminine (Venus) principles. It suggests that the ultimate "King" or alchemical goal arises from this synthesis of opposites.</summary>
<keywords>Rebis, Hermaphroditus, Mercury, Venus, alchemy, duality, union of opposites, Hermaphrodite</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
# Discourse 38.

When Socrates was asked what countryman he was, he answered that he was a citizen of the world. By this, his intention was to signify that although he was born at Athens as a person, yet in his mind he freely beheld the whole world as his country and the things contained within it; the whole earth is a wise man’s country, wherever he lives well. So, if anyone asks the philosophers what countryman their **Hermaphrodite** <term>A mythical or symbolic being combining both male and female qualities; in alchemy, it represents the union of opposites.</term> is, they answer that he belongs to the world, or is in all corners of the world where the Elements can be found, as the **Son of the wise** <note>original: "Sonne of the wife"; this is likely a transcription error for the common alchemical phrase *filius sapientiae*, or "Son of the Wise," referring to the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, who has a country in common with them. 

But seeing as it does not happen that any man is born twice or more often, nor first enters this light in several places but only in one—just as Socrates is acknowledged to be an Athenian—so is <term>Rebis</term> <note>From the Latin *res bina*, meaning "double-matter" or "two-thing." It is a symbol of the completed alchemical work.</note> reputed to be an inhabitant of two mountains, that is, <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Venus</term>. From these, he also received the name of **Hermaphrodite** <note>A portmanteau of the Greek gods Hermes (Mercury) and Aphrodite (Venus).</note> given to him by both parents. A noble and large country is indeed no small help to doing things excellently well, in which citizens are preferred over foreigners and promoted to public offices so that they may not lie in obscurity. (As it happens in a lowly place, they may see some light come to them from their country.) Instead, they show themselves through their own virtues, even in the small affairs of a house, and give light themselves to their own country. 

In this manner, these mountains, which are unknown to many men, acquire fame from the **Hermaphrodite** by reason of his illustrious actions and his name, which is famous throughout the whole world. For who, though they may be ever so little versed in philosophy books, has not known <term>Rebis</term>? Who has not seen or beheld the <term>Androgynus</term> <note>Another term for the Hermaphrodite, meaning "man-woman."</note> with two heads? <unclear>He</unclear> truly has been known to the Judges themselves, and his fame has been dispersed farther than the fame even of King Alexander the Great. 

Many men travel out of far countries to see and speak with some learned man, or someone otherwise remarkable for singular military industry, art, or science; but many more will betake themselves to the said mountains of <term>Rebis</term>, provided they can learn where they may be found. With how much study and care <term>Morienus</term> <note>Morienus Romanus, a legendary 7th-century alchemist said to have been the teacher of the King Khalid ibn Yazid.</note>, having departed from Rome <note>original: "Home"; likely a transcription error for "Rome," as Morienus is traditionally called "the Roman."</note>, sought and at length found <term>Adfarus Alexandrinus</term>, he himself declares in his book. He was therefore to be esteemed more happy and acceptable to God because he had learned and beheld this thing—that is, the native place of <term>Rebis</term>—from a living Father <note>A living teacher or mentor, as opposed to a book.</note> and not from "dumb masters" <note>Books or inanimate writings.</note>. No less assiduity and diligence ought those to use who seek the country of <term>Rebis</term> by themselves, being informed by reason and the teachings of books. But though some books may sometimes seem to be clear and conspicuous...

<summary>This page discusses the origins and universal nature of the alchemical "Hermaphrodite" or Rebis, comparing its "citizenship of the world" to that of Socrates while locating its symbolic birth in the union of Mercury and Venus. The author emphasizes the fame of this entity and the great effort required—as shown by the alchemist Morienus—to discover its true nature and location.</summary>

<keywords>Rebis, Hermaphrodite, Socrates, Mercury, Venus, Morienus, Adfarus Alexandrinus, alchemical union, Androgynus, citizen of the world</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Philosophical Mercury," comparing the pursuit of alchemical knowledge to a perilous sea voyage and a difficult mountain ascent.</meta>

### Discourse 38.

...yet they are so veiled and clouded with complexity and obscurity that, for that reason, it is very difficult to distinguish between them. Therefore, we must proceed cautiously with them, so that those which are prepared as a remedy might not instead act as a poison. 

They are certainly a vast ocean. In this ocean, expert mariners sailing may use astronomical instruments to know the latitude—the elevation of the Equator above the horizon—with the compass <note>original: "magnet"</note> pointing to the North Pole. However, they can by no means determine the longitude, or how many degrees they are distant from the Prime Meridian <note>original: "first Meridian"</note>, which is near the Fortunate Islands <note>The Canary Islands, traditionally considered the western edge of the world and the starting point for measuring longitude in the early modern period.</note>. Thus, they are uncertain of their exact position between west and east. 

What, then, is to be done? We must do what those same mariners do: combine experience with reason. In this way, we learn how to navigate a long voyage by observing specific signs, headlands, islands, and other landmarks, so that through lack of care we do not run aground on sandbars or rocks. There is less danger here if the endeavor does not succeed; but if it does, the gain is far greater than at sea, where goods and lives can be lost in a single hour. 

However, the mountain of the *philosophical Mercury* <note>The primary substance or "First Matter" sought by alchemists to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is not Vesuvius or Mount Atlas, where it is sometimes said to be found. Instead, it is Parnassus with its two peaks: *Hermes* resides on one, and *Venus* on the other. There also is *Apollo* with the *Muses*, and *Hippocrene*—the fountain of *Pegasus*—surrounded by evergreen laurels. It is one mountain in name, but actually two, just as *Hermaphroditus* <note>In mythology, the son of Hermes and Aphrodite (Venus) whose body was merged with a nymph's, possessing both male and female characteristics; in alchemy, this often represents the union of opposites.</note> is seen with two heads and two sets of organs in one body. 

But what man in a thousand persists in ascending to the summit of this mountain? Who does not begin to hesitate at the bottom, blocked by countless obstacles? Who ever even reaches the midpoint? For:

> The ascent is not easy if one seeks the heights; 
> much sweat carries him upward. 
> He dies to the sleeplessness of the nightly lamp <note>original: "nocturnae insomnia olivae," literally "the insomnia of the nightly olive [oil lamp]," referring to late-night study.</note>, 
> and he grieves over what he had recently praised in himself—
> he who desires to be gifted with the honor of the eternal leaf.

Therefore, it is no surprise if only one in ten thousand undertakes these Herculean labors <note>Refers to the twelve near-impossible tasks of Hercules; here meaning an extremely difficult endeavor.</note> so as to set foot on the mountain’s peak and enjoy the immortal reward of a laurel garland. It is much to be desired that all those who are upright, teachable <note>original: "docible"</note>, and devoted to virtue and learning may achieve this with joy, while the vain and envious are deprived of it.

<summary>The author uses metaphors of maritime navigation and the ascent of Mount Parnassus to illustrate the extreme difficulty of obtaining alchemical wisdom. He concludes that while the path is obscure and exhausting, the virtuous student who persists will earn an "immortal reward."</summary>

<keywords>Philosophical Mercury, Alchemy, Longitude, Navigation, Mount Parnassus, Hermes, Venus, Apollo, Hermaphroditus, Herculean labors, Virtue</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the collection of emblems, focusing on Emblem 39, which uses the myth of Oedipus to illustrate the discovery of nature's secrets.</meta>

### Emblem 39. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Oedipus, having overcome the Sphinx and killed his father Laius,<-
->married his mother Jocasta.<-

---

### Epigram 39.

> The Sphinx, whom many dreaded, dies along with her riddles,
> But subtle Oedipus reveals her wiles.
> The question was: who in the morning might
> Stand on four feet, two at noon, and three at night? <note>This refers to the famous Riddle of the Sphinx. The answer is "Man," who crawls on four limbs as a baby (morning), walks on two legs in adulthood (noon), and uses a staff or cane as a third leg in old age (night).</note>
> Without knowing him, he deprives his father of life,
> And takes his own mother to be his wife.

<summary>This page recounts the myth of Oedipus, specifically his victory over the Sphinx's riddle and his tragic fate, serving as an allegory for the intellectual mastery of nature's secrets.</summary>
<keywords>Oedipus, Sphinx, Laius, Jocasta, Riddle of the Sphinx, Secrets of Nature, Greek Mythology, alchemy, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the hidden nature of alchemical and philosophical secrets, specifically focusing on the symbolic use of the Sphinx as a guardian of knowledge.</meta>

### Discourse 39.

Bacassor, a philosopher in the *Turba* <note>original: "Turba"; referring to the *Turba Philosophorum* or "Assembly of the Philosophers," one of the earliest and most influential Latin alchemical texts</note>, says that what you seek is of no small value; for you seek the greatest treasure and the most excellent gift of God.

And Barne says, "O you students, take heed of what the philosophers have long since suggested: that the truth is not discerned except through error, and nothing brings more grief to the heart than error in your art and work. For when a man thinks he has finished and has produced a work, he will find nothing in his hands."

The ancient philosophers were pleased to signify these same things by proposing a Sphinx, as if she denotes the obscurity and the labyrinthine path <note>original: "meander"</note> of the art.

For this reason the Egyptians, in their sacred *Isiaci* <note>referring to the Isiac Mysteries, the secret religious rites of the goddess Isis</note>, which were celebrated in honor of Osiris by mitred priests with shaven heads and all parts of the body clothed in a white linen garment down to the ankles (so that they might not be known or discovered by the common people), erected a statue of silence, which was called Sigalion <note>A Roman name for the Egyptian deity Harpocrates, the god of silence and secrets</note>, at the entrance of the altar. The assistants were commanded to keep silence and turn their eyes toward that image. For the same cause, they added the figures of Sphinxes at the corners of the altar to represent the mystical knowledge of sacred things, as Boissard <note>Jean-Jacques Boissard (1528–1602), a French antiquary and Latin poet</note> demonstrates from ancient authors.

<margin>Boissard?</margin>

For the Sphinx is a certain kind of monster, most obscure to the inhabitants of Thebes, who proposed riddles not only to them, but (as she did previously to the Egyptians) to all others who aspire to the art. She waits in the books of the philosophers just as she once did before the gates of Thebes. If any man can pass by the monster, he suffers no evil from him; but he who, through presumption of mind or wit, endeavors to resolve his riddles and cannot perform the task, acquires his own destruction—that is, grief to the heart and damage to his affairs through error in his work.

He who applies such allegories to literal history has a mushroom for a head and a melon for a heart, as the Comedian <note>likely referring to a classical playwright such as Plautus or Terence</note> says, and knows nothing more than that he deviates from the right way.

These things are too childish and fit only for old wives if they are taken literally; otherwise, they are the signs and tokens of profound learning. Monstrous wild beasts are indeed said to be in Africa by the name of Sphinxes, but here we do not discourse of them, though the origin and name of this fiction seem to be derived from them.

The philosophers' Sphinx understands and uses human speech—namely Greek—and also proposes subtle, genuine, and enigmatic questions. In these, there appears a singular sharpness of understanding and learning that is not so obvious to other men, and from which brute animals are exceedingly distant. He who is familiar with the assertions of the philosophers will easily know them to be such: for where one thing is said and another thing is signified, there double meanings <note>original: "equivocation"</note> beget error, which is not only permitted but also commanded.

<summary>This discourse explains that the Sphinx is an alchemical allegory for the difficult riddles and hidden truths of the "great art," warning that failure to interpret these secrets correctly leads to ruin.</summary>
<keywords>Sphinx, Turba Philosophorum, alchemy, Egyptian mysteries, Osiris, Sigalion, Boissard, symbolism, riddles</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical interpretation of classical mythology, moving from general philosophy to the specific allegory of Oedipus and the Sphinx.</meta>

### Discourse 39.

...to philosophy alone. The city of Thebes was long perplexed by the riddles of the Sphinx. At length, a man named Oedipus arrived and gave such answers to the questions posed that the Sphinx could not help but throw herself down from a cliff. 

But who is this Oedipus? He was the son of the King of Thebes. After his birth, an oracle predicted to his father that he would be killed by his son; consequently, the King commanded that Oedipus be killed. The infant was hung from a tree with a rope fastened across his feet and left there, but he was rescued and raised by another countryman. When he reached adulthood, he indeed had swollen feet <note>The name "Oedipus" literally translates from Greek as "swollen foot."</note>, yet he demonstrated a quick wit far above other men in solving the riddle proposed by the Sphinx. 

Although the Sphinx is reported to have had many riddles, the chief one offered to Oedipus was this: "What is it that in the morning goes on four feet, at noon on two, and in the evening on three?" Oedipus’s specific answer is not fully explained here <note>In traditional myth, the answer is "Man," who crawls as a baby, walks as an adult, and uses a cane in old age.</note>, but some interpret it as the ages of man. These people are mistaken. For a quadrangle—representing the four elements of all things—must be considered first. From there, we move to the hemisphere <note>Likely referring to the semi-circle symbol for the Moon</note> having two lines (one straight, the other crooked), which represents the Moon. From there, we move to a triangle, which consists of body, spirit, and soul—or the Sun, Moon, and Mercury <note>original: Sol, Luna, and Mercury</note>. Regarding this, Rhases <note>Rhases (c. 865–925) was a famous Persian polymath and alchemist.</note> says in his *Epistle*: "The stone is a triangle in its essence, and a quadrangle in its quality." The twenty-first emblem and its explanation also relate to this. 

Moreover, Oedipus is infamous for parricide <note>parricide: the act of killing one's father</note> and incest, which are two of the most detestable sins imaginable. Nevertheless, these acts led him to a kingdom that was otherwise his by right. He unknowingly killed his father (who refused to yield the way to him) and married the Queen, the wife of Laius, who was actually his own mother. 

But this is written not to be imitated by any man as history or as a moral example; it is merely a fictional allegory introduced by the philosophers to reveal the secrets of their doctrine. For in the "Great Work" <note>The *Magnum Opus*, or the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, both occur: the first "efficient" <term>efficient cause: the primary source of change or stability; in alchemy, often the initial chemical agent</term> (the father) is killed and destroyed by his "effect" (the son). Afterward, that same effect joins the second efficient cause to himself until they become one with him. Thus, the son is joined to his mother in marriage and enjoys his father's kingdom through a triple right: by conquest, by marriage, and by succession. 

He has swollen feet because he cannot run; he is like a toad, containing the greatest secret, or like lead, moving at a slow pace. This is because he is "fixed" <note>In alchemy, "fixing" refers to making a volatile substance stable so it can withstand heat.</note> and fixes others, neither avoiding nor fearing fire. Although he is a substance of low reputation, the philosophers cannot manage without him.

<summary>This page provides an alchemical interpretation of the Oedipus myth, reframing his riddle and his crimes as allegories for the chemical transformations required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Oedipus, Sphinx, Thebes, Alchemical Allegory, Four Elements, Rhases, Parricide, Incest, Great Work, Fixed Mercury, Lead</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
## Emblem 40. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

->From two waters make one, and that will be the water of holiness <note>original: Sanctity</note>.<-

### Epigram 40.

->Here you may behold two flowing fountains,<-
->One with hot water, the other cold:<-
->That one is called the Boy's spring, but this the Virgin's spring,<-
->Join them, so that they may be only one thing:<-
->Your river will share the qualities <note>original: participate</note> of both,<-
->As the fountain of Jupiter <note>original: Jove</note> (surnamed Ammon <note>original: Hammon</note>) does.<-

<vocab>Emblem, Secrets of Nature, water of Holiness, Epigram, fountains, Jupiter, Ammon</vocab>

<summary>This emblem describes the alchemical process of uniting opposites—represented by hot and cold, masculine and feminine springs—to create a single, "holy" water. The resulting mixture is compared to the legendary fountain of Jupiter Ammon, which was said to be both hot and cold at different times of day.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, unification of opposites, Jupiter Ammon, sacred water, fountains, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
### Discourse 40

The miracles of waters are so numerous and so great that they could scarcely be contained in a large volume; several authors have written about them here and there. But above all, the two "philosophical waters" <note>In alchemy, "philosophical water" usually refers to a potent solvent, like mercury or a specialized acid, used to break down metals.</note> are celebrated with that name because they do not only imitate but also exceed the virtues and properties of all the rest.

The river Sybaris, the Axus of Macedonia, and the Melas of Boeotia <note>original: "Baotia"</note> make cattle black if they drink from them; but the river Crathis, the Clitumnus of Mevania, and the Cephisus make black cattle white. The water of Sinuessa <note>original: "Sinnessa"</note> in the country of Campania removes barrenness from both sexes. Conversely, the river Apsidisius makes women barren. Labura, a fountain of Mesopotamia, has water with a sweet smell; yet the water of Anigros <note>original: "Anygor"</note> in Peloponnesus stinks very much. The fountain of Jupiter Hammon <note>A famous ancient oracle and spring in the Libyan desert.</note> is cold during the day, hot at night, and lukewarm by turns in the morning and evening. 

We do not need to dwell upon others; all things, even those contrary to one another, are performed by the philosophical water. Of this, Raymond Lully <note>original: "Lully"; a 13th-century philosopher and alchemist.</note> says in his book *On the Quintessence* (Distinction 3, on Iteration): "And so there is a twofold consideration in the art; that is, from the single nature of one metal to make two contrary liquids in the composition. One has a fixing, congealing, and hardening virtue, and the other is volatile, unfixing, and soft. But that second liquid is hardened, fixed, and congealed by the first. From both these liquids results one 'stone'—congealed, fixed, and hardened—which has the power to congeal things that are not congealed, to harden what is soft <note>original: "mollifyes"</note>, and to soften <note>original: "mollify"</note> what is hard."

From this, it is evident what these two waters are and why they must be reduced into one water. For the "stone" <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> is called "water" because it can be melted <note>original: "fusible"</note>, and on the contrary, the water is called "stone" because it can be broken <note>original: "frangible"</note>. 

But these waters are drawn from different places, sometimes over a long distance, as may be seen in Rome with the *Aqua Virgo* <note>original: "Aqua Virginia"; one of the eleven Roman aqueducts that supplied the city with pure water.</note> and other artificial fountains; they must then be mixed by flowing together so that one may be made of two. For if one has a hot virtue and the other a cold virtue, being mixed together they will acquire mixed virtues and regulate each other in a wonderful manner. From this, most excellent medicinal and bath waters are produced, which will overcome <note>original: "profligate"</note> all sorts of diseases and maladies and restore a man to firm health. 

Nature does indeed, by her own skill of composition, blend and mix many waters in the bowels of the earth with the various virtues of minerals <note>original: "Mynes"</note>, which are wholesome and remedial to many sick and infirm persons. However, if art is added with the proper governance <note>original: "regiment"</note>—after all necessary preparations are made and other things are set aside—and mixes those things that ought to be mixed together, that composition will be far more effective. Though it may seem artificial, it is nonetheless entirely natural, because from different things, one simple, uniform <note>original: "homogenous"</note> matter is made.

<summary>This discourse explores the miraculous properties of natural springs and rivers before comparing them to the alchemical "philosophical waters," which can reconcile contrary properties like hardening and softening. The author argues that while nature creates healing mineral waters, alchemical art can refine these processes to create even more powerful medicines.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, philosophical water, Raymond Lully, mineral springs, medicinal baths, transmutation, Aqua Virgo, Roman aqueducts, properties of matter</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the limitations of human art compared to the power of nature in creating unified substances.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>Discourse 40.</header>

...which can never be achieved by art. A mixture and confusion of parts is indeed caused by art, without the help of nature, but it lacks any natural union, which is created by nature alone. There is an artificial mixing of various simples <note>original: "simples" - individual medicinal herbs or ingredients used in a compound</note> in <term>Theriac</term> <note>original: "Triacle" - a famous, complex medicinal ointment or syrup used as an antidote to poison</note>, which is performed by grinding <note>original: "contrition"</note> and fermentation. However, no one (unless being thoughtless) would claim it to be a natural composition, much less a uniform <note>original: "homogenous"</note> medicine.

Regarding the artificial mixing of substances, it is obvious that they do not truly merge into one another at the most basic level, even if they cannot be divided and separated again by human industry. But regarding the mixing of all qualities, we must ask: can the primary Theriac made of all ingredients be reduced into one <term>quintessence</term> <note>original: "Quintesence" - the purest or most essential part of a substance, sought by alchemists</note>? Or <insert>as whether they</insert> <meta>strikethrough</meta> remain in their powder or substance as they were before—like an "accident" <note>In traditional philosophy, an "accident" is a property (like color or smell) that exists within a subject but does not change its essential nature</note> in a subject, or color on a wall?

And then, what should be said of the secondary, tertiary, and quaternary qualities? It is probable that all the qualities still reside within their own original substances and are not combined through a true natural mixture. Otherwise, if qualities were to leave their "bodies" (their physical substances), there would be four quintessences in every artificial composition, corresponding to the order of the qualities (first, second, etc.), existing without their bodies and remaining separable.

They say this is not the case with the <term>coagulant</term> <note>original: "coagulum" - a substance used to curdle or clot, often referring here to hare's rennet</note> of a hare; when blood is flowing too thinly, this substance stops and, in a sense, curdles it. Yet, through the process of clotting and grinding, it releases and dissolves the blood again. For the effects of vinegar, lead, and many other things are contradictory depending on how they are used, because nature has mixed them so wonderfully.

Likewise, <term>philosophical water</term> <note>In alchemy, a specialized solvent, often associated with mercury, believed to be able to dissolve metals into their primary matter</note> possesses different and opposing virtues because nature, with the help of art, has mixed it with contrary things into one indivisible substance. This substance is nothing else but a quintessence in relation to the other things meant to be mixed with it.

<summary>This page explores the distinction between artificial mixtures and natural unions, arguing that man-made medicinal compounds like Theriac remain collections of separate ingredients rather than becoming a single, "natural" substance. The author uses alchemical concepts like "quintessence" and "philosophical water" to explain how nature can unify contrary qualities into a single indivisible essence.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Theriac, quintessence, natural philosophy, mixture vs. union, philosophical water, coagulum, medicine</keywords>

> And with that blood she stains <note>original: "tings"</note> the lily-white roses red;
> The goddess weeps, and the Syrians cannot restrain <note>their tears</note>,

### Discourse 41.

How improperly some of the mythologists interpret the allegory of Adonis—ascribing him sometimes to the Sun; the Boar, by which he was slain, to a harsh winter; and sometimes to the seed of wheat, which remains six months with Proserpina under the earth and as many above the earth with Venus—is elsewhere sufficiently explained and refuted by us. Here we do, with the consent of all men, declare the **philosophical Sun** <note>In alchemy, the "philosophical Sun" refers to the purified essence or spiritual "gold" of the Work, rather than the literal star in the sky.</note> to be meant by Adonis. From this comes the verses:

-> All are the same: Dionysus, the Sun, and Adonis. <-
<note>original: "Omnia sunt idem, Dionysus, Sol, et Adonis."</note>

And Orpheus: . . . . . , Who is called by the various name of Adonis,
-> The author of seeds as well, equally boy and girl. <-
<note>original: "Germinum et idem author, pariter puer atq; puella."</note>

All of these are by no means to be understood as the celestial Sun, but as a philosophical Sun; for this one expresses both sexes, while the other does not. So they also attribute the very same things to Dionysus and the Sun as they do to Adonis and, conversely, to Osiris. Adonis is killed by a Boar—that is, by <term>Acetum acerrimum</term> <note>literally "sharpest vinegar," referring to a potent alchemical acid or solvent</note>, or the solutive water. This water has terrible teeth like a boar, with which it strikes Adonis; because the philosophical Sun is mortally wounded by that boar, it dissolves <note>original: "loosnes in the members"</note> and puts off its form. But Venus endeavors to help her paramour; once he is dead, she lays him among lettuce and watches over him. In the same manner, Osiris is killed by Typhon and cut into several pieces, which Isis, the wife of Osiris, gathers up and buries after joining them together. 

The same mourning which follows the death of Osiris every year in Egypt was also celebrated after the death of Adonis in Syria and the bordering kingdoms. In those places, after weeping and wailing were heard for some days, signs of joy and dancing were given afterward, as if he who was dead now lives anew, being conveyed to heaven. From this arose the vanity of their religion or heathenish superstition, which grew to an excessive height as the Devil provided the occasion and produced false miracles. 

Adonis was (as they feign) born of Cinyras, King of Cyprus, and Myrrha, his daughter. He is said to be born in incest; if history is considered, this is a crime, but if it is an allegory, it is not unlawful but principally necessary. For if the mother and son, or the father and daughter, are not joined together and an offspring produced from them, nothing is perfected in this art. For here, the nearer the husband and wife are to each other in blood—in the first or second degree of kinship—the more fruitful they are. On the contrary, the more remote they are, the more unfruitful they become, which is not tolerable in the matrimony of men. For this cause, Oedipus marries his own mother, Jupiter his sister, and so also do Osiris, Saturn, and Sol. The royal servant Gabritius <note>A common figure in alchemical allegories representing the masculine principle or sulfur</note> does the same; as Sol says (in a metaphor of Belinus in the *Rosary* <note>original: "Rosary," referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, an influential 16th-century alchemical text</note>):

<summary>This page argues that the myth of Adonis represents the "philosophical Sun" of alchemy rather than literal agriculture or astronomy. It explains that alchemical "incest" and Adonis's death by a boar are allegories for the necessary joining of related substances and their subsequent dissolution by acid.</summary>

<keywords>Adonis, philosophical Sun, alchemy, Osiris, Isis, dissolution, alchemical marriage, Rosarium Philosophorum, symbolism, Acetum acerrimum</keywords>

<header>Discourse 41.</header>

...speaks thus of <term>Adonis</term> <note>original: "Adonis"; in alchemical literature, Adonis often represents the solar spirit or the "philosophical gold" that must undergo death and rebirth</note>, that is, of himself: *Know you, that my father <term>Sol</term> <note>the Sun</note> has given me power above all power, and clothes me with the garment of glory.* And a little after: *For I am his only son and more like my father, etc. I strip my servants of their power and nature, and clothe them with my beautiful splendor and light (which my father gave me) in all their works. For I am excellent, who exalts and lowers all things, and none of my servants is above me, except one, who is permitted to be opposed and contrary to me. And he destroys me, yet destroys not my nature; and he is <term>Saturn</term> <note>original: "Saturne"; Saturn represents lead, the beginning of the "Great Work," and the force of decay or "blackness" (nigredo) that precedes purification</note>, who separates all my parts. Afterwards I go to my mother, who gathers together all my divided and separated members. I illuminate all those things that belong to me, and cause light to appear openly in the way from my father Saturn, as also from my mother, who is an enemy to me.* 

The words spoken until now are so clear as to disperse darkness from the eyes of the mind—even for someone who is only moderately familiar with the reading of these authors—and they reveal the light of the sun. This light is abundantly clear in the connections implied between things and persons. For those things which are true, though hidden under the veil of an allegory, agree in wonderful harmony with one another; but those things which are false are contradictory and inconsistent with themselves and others.

<summary>This page presents an alchemical allegory where the Sun (Adonis/Sol) describes being destroyed by Saturn (lead/decay) and reunited by his mother, symbolizing the cycle of alchemical transformation. The author argues that true alchemical philosophy is revealed through the internal consistency of such allegories.</summary>

## Emblem 42. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Nature, Reason, Experience, and reading must be the guide, staff, spectacles, and lamp to him who is employed in chemical <note>original: "Chymicall" – at this time, chemistry and alchemy were often considered the same field of study</note> affairs.<-

### Epigram 42.

> In pursuing art, let Nature be your guide;
> By tracing her steps, your feet will not slip.
> Let reason be your staff, and let experience add
> Strength to your sight, so you may discern good from bad.
> In darkness, reading will direct you like a light,
> So that you may detect any uncertainty <note>original: "ambiguity"</note>.

<vocab>Nature, Reason, Experience, Chemical, art, uncertainty</vocab>

<summary>This page presents Emblem 42, which outlines the four essential tools—Nature, Reason, Experience, and Reading—required for a successful practitioner of alchemy or early chemistry. It uses the metaphors of a guide, a staff, spectacles, and a lamp to describe how these intellectual and practical virtues assist the seeker in uncovering the secrets of the natural world.</summary>

# Discourse 42

The accidents which may happen to travelers are countless, especially if they travel on foot at night through slippery and dangerous places. For such a journey, four things are required as essential—not to mention provisions, a supplier, and a strong body. 

First, a companion or guide who is familiar with the paths through which he must pass. For if one ignorant man guides another, the same thing happens to them as to blind men: if they do not both fall headlong into a ditch, they will certainly fall into errors and mistakes. Second, a staff, by which the slipperiness and perils of the path may be avoided so that they do not cause a man harm. Third, healthy eyes; for such journeys are most dangerous to the blind or those with poor vision. Fourth, a lamp or lighted torch, so that the different paths and turnings may be distinguished.

In the same way, if a man undertakes the most difficult journey in pursuit of the <term>philosophical medicine</term> <note>original: "philosophicall medicine"; the "universal medicine" or Philosopher's Stone believed to cure all ailments and transmute metals</note>, he will—in addition to the expense and physical strength required—desire four things that correspond exactly to those mentioned above: namely, Nature, Reason, Experience, and Reading. If any one of these is missing, the rest will be of little or no use at all. These are like the four wheels that move the "philosophical chariot"; if even one wheel is lost, the chariot cannot function.

Nature provides the natural bodies and spirits as the primary subjects. Art <note>meaning "alchemy" or "skill"</note> must then act upon these by preparing, purifying, and fitting them so that the final goal promised by the art may be achieved. Just as a potter takes water and earth, a glassmaker takes ashes and sand, a smith takes iron, copper, tin, lead, silver, or gold, and a tanner takes raw hides—so too does the <term>chemist</term> <note>original: "operator of Chymistry"</note> regard his materials. 

However, while other craftsmen know their materials well from the very first day, the chemist often remains ignorant of <unclear>them</unclear> for many years—if not for his entire life. Nature does indeed point toward the matter, but there are many things that obscure the impressions left by nature so that it cannot be easily known. The first goal, therefore, is to seriously contemplate how nature operates, so that the natural subjects of <term>chemistry</term> may be obtained without deficiency or excess. Therefore, Nature must be the guide and companion of so great a journey, and her footsteps must be traced.

Secondly, reason must serve as a staff to keep one's steps firm and steady so that they do not slip or waver. For without the exercise of reason, a man will be likely to fall...

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a dangerous night journey to explain the four essentials of alchemy: Nature (the guide), Reason (the staff), Experience (the eyes), and Reading (the lamp).</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, philosophical medicine, Nature, Reason, Experience, Reading, chemistry, materials, metaphorical journey</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on avoiding intellectual error by relying on reason and evidence rather than mere words.</meta>

# Discourse 42.

...into errors, and therefore the philosopher says: "Whatever you hear, consult with reason to determine whether it can be so or not." For no one is forced to believe or perform impossible things, unless he has a weak memory, a dull mind <note>original: "genius"</note>, and a foolish imagination that imposes upon him, leading him to accept false things as true and reject true things as false.

They also say that they do not care for words—regardless of what may be spoken—but only for "things," meaning that which can be understood. They believe that words exist for the sake of things, and not things for the sake of words. For example: suppose a man says that glass is made malleable <note>capable of being hammered or pressed into shape without breaking</note> by the <term>philosophical tincture</term> <note>an alchemical substance, such as the Philosopher's Stone, believed to have the power to transform or "tinge" materials</note>. Why should I not believe it, provided that reason dictates it is possible?

Thirdly, experience acts like spectacles, through which things may be seen at a distance. These are optical instruments, invented and made by art to help and correct the weakness of many eyes. Much like these instruments are experiments concerning <term>mineral matter</term> of every kind—whether colored <note>original: "dyed"</note>, seen, or truly heard. The more these experiments are held in the memory, the more a person of reason will draw from them and compare them with other things, so that he may perceive what is true and what is not.

Fourthly, reading kindles, as it were, a clear lamp in the understanding, without which there would be nothing but darkness and thick clouds. However, the reading of good authors must be repeated often; otherwise, it will not be effective at all. Regarding this, <term>Balassor</term> <note>A figure cited in early alchemical literature</note> in the <term>Turba</term> <note>The *Turba Philosophorum*, or "Assembly of the Philosophers," one of the earliest and most influential Latin alchemical texts</note> says:

> "He, therefore, who is of an even temper and practices patience without regret will attain this art as if by a direct line. But he who thinks himself able to reap benefit from our books more quickly is deceived; it would have been better for him never to have looked into them than to have ever touched them."

And so on . . .

<summary>This page argues that the pursuit of knowledge—specifically alchemy—requires the application of reason, the evidence of experience (likened to optical lenses), and the diligent, repeated study of authoritative texts. It warns that rushing through these studies or prioritizing words over the nature of physical "things" leads to error.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, reason, logic, experience, optics, scientific method, Turba Philosophorum, Balassor, philosophical tincture, malleable glass</keywords>

## Emblem 43. On the Secrets of Nature.

->Give ear to the vulture’s words, which are in no way false.<-

### Epigram 43.

The vulture stands in sight upon the mountain,
Proclaiming himself daily—black and white,
Citrine <note>original: "Citrine"; a lemon-yellow color that, in alchemy, represents a specific stage of maturation in the "Great Work."</note> and red; and he does not lie: The Raven is
The same, who by nature flies without wings
In dismal darkness and in glorious light,
For either one will lead you correctly to the art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy, specifically the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical symbolism, moving into "Discourse 43" which focuses on birds that speak and the specific symbolism of the "philosophic vulture."</meta>

### Discourse 43

We daily hear birds in many places prattling or imitating the voice of man, such as parrots, ravens, daws <note>original: "Dawes"; jackdaws</note>, and magpies <note>original: "pyes"</note>. For Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), Roman author of *Natural History*</note> writes that, at the time he published his history, Agrippina, the wife of Claudius Caesar, had a thrush that imitated the speech of men. The young Caesars also had a starling and nightingales accustomed to the Greek and Latin tongues, which spoke new things daily, even using long strings of words. But because such birds are now frequently seen, they are thought less worthy of admiration; for all birds endowed with a broad tongue may, through use and custom, be brought to speak and babble. 

But that vulture, which the philosophers <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers to alchemists</note> mention, did not learn the words he utters through practice. Instead, he expresses them naturally and silently. The philosophers indeed say that he continually cries out and pronounces with a loud voice who and what he is. In this, he imitates great princes who, in the beginning of their proclamation <note>original: "intimation"</note>, will always make their titles and inheritance known—not as a sign of pride, but for other reasons: so that they themselves may know what principalities they govern, or what right of inheritance they claim. 

So, it is very important <note>original: "materiall"</note> to know what colors—as ensigns of arms and titles—the philosophical bird is adorned with, and how he excels all others. "I," says he, as Rosarius <note>Likely referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> has it from Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure and reputed founder of alchemy</note>, "am the black of white, and the citrine <note>A lemon-yellow or greenish-yellow color, representing a specific stage in the alchemical process</note> of red, and certainly I speak the truth and do not lie." Here, he affirms himself to be black, white, citrine, and red, and he really is such; for though he does not yet actually possess the final colors, he expects the inheritance of them. 

Thereupon says Rosinus <note>An alchemical authority, often identified with the Arabic author Zosimos</note> in his book of divine interpretation: "Take the Stone, which is black, white, red, and citrine—that admirable bird which flies without wings in the darkness of the night and the clearness of the day." For coloration arises from the bitterness which is in his throat, but more water proceeds from his blood, as Alexander <note>Likely referring to alchemical legends associated with Alexander the Great</note> says. "Take (my son) the stone of four colors." The philosophers' books abundantly declare and suggest that the Stone has all these principal colors in a successive order. 

But there is a reason to be offered as to why the philosophical subject is called a vulture. Among vultures, the black ones are held in the highest esteem; they are predatory <note>original: "rapacious"</note> but fly slowly because of the large size of their bodies. They say that this bird conceives without the seed of the male and generates without mating <note>original: "coition"</note>, and that their offspring live to a great age, even to a hundred years. They build their nests in high rocks, and no man has reached them. Two chicks <note>original: "kichens"</note> are seen for the most part. They are helpful and useful against serpents. They are offended and...

<summary>This discourse explores the symbolism of the "philosophical vulture," comparing its natural ability to announce its "titles" (alchemical colors) to the way princes declare their lineage. It details the four stages of the alchemical process—black, white, citrine, and red—using the vulture as a metaphor for the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

<keywords>vulture, alchemical colors, citrine, Hermes Trismegistus, Rosarium Philosophorum, Philosopher's Stone, Pliny the Elder, Agrippina, bird symbolism, transmutation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "philosophical bird" and its symbolic similarities to the vulture, specifically focusing on its reproduction, longevity, and where it may be found.</meta>

### Discourse 43

...perplexed with the East wind. When they begin to lay eggs, they bring an object from the Indies which is like a nut, having something inside that can be moved and immediately produces a sound; when they have applied this to themselves, they produce many young, but only one remains, which is called an **Immusulus** <note>original: "Immusulus"; a term used in classical natural history, often by Pliny, to describe a specific type of vulture or bird of prey</note>. Pomponius Tonsius, as cited in Pliny, is a witness that vultures are the most innocent of all animals because they do not interfere at all with anything that men sow, plant, or breed. Moreover, they kill no animals. They also abstain from other birds, even dead ones, by a certain instinct of affinity. For this reason, they were highly esteemed in divination, as the stories of the founding of the city of Rome declare. 

The philosophical bird, expressing almost all these properties of vultures, is therefore not undeservedly called the **Vulture** by Hermes and others. It is also slow in flight and black in color; it conceives by itself. For thus says the *Rosarius* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> near the end: "And it is the Dragon which weds itself, and impregnates itself, and brings forth in its own season," etc. And Rosinus to Sarratant: "And that is the Serpent, administering pleasure to itself, impregnating itself, and bringing forth in one day," etc. It lives and endures a very long time, and multiplies itself. For what Virgil writes of the bird called a phoenix is just as appropriate to this, because they are the same.

-> *Alipedem Cervum ter vincit, at illum* <-
-> *Multiplicat novies phoenix reparabilis ales.* <-
-> <note>original Latin: "The phoenix, a bird capable of rebirth, lives nine times longer than the wing-footed deer, which itself lives three times longer [than a human]."</note> <-

-> The Raven lives three times the age of a Hart, <-
-> Which the rare phoenix survives nine times over. <-

It is very difficult to climb to this bird's nest. It fights with the **mercurial serpent** and overcomes it—that is, Sol <note>The Sun, representing Gold or Sulfur</note> with Luna <note>The Moon, representing Silver or Mercury</note>. It is conceived by the wind, carries in its belly, and breeds in the air. The stone that is white and contains a smaller stone sounding within it is called by many men the **Etites** <term>Eagle Stone; a hollow geode with a loose stone or pebble inside, once believed to assist in childbirth</term>. <insert>It</insert> is the name for this stone. Only one Immusulus is found in the philosopher's nest. The philosophical bird is also most innocent because it hurts nobody; it is beneficial to all who know it and most excellent in divination. 

But why does it make its nest on a mountain and, sitting there, cry out? Rosinus answers according to Rhasy <note>Rhazes, a Persian polymath and alchemist</note>, who says: "Behold the highest mountains, which are on both the right and left, and climb up there: there our Stone is found; and in another mountain, which produces all sorts of painters' colors and spirits <note>or chemical species</note>, it is also there." Morienus says: "Climb up the high mountain planted with trees, because there our stone is found and hidden." And Hermes says: "Take the blessed Stone, break it to pieces, and wash the red stone, from which exhales that which is found in mountains, and especially sometimes in old **sinks**" <note>original: "sinks"; referring to sewers, drains, or places of refuse, illustrating the alchemical paradox that the "Stone" is found in both the highest and lowest places</note>.

<summary>This discourse compares the philosophical bird of alchemy to the vulture and phoenix, emphasizing its self-conceiving nature and its residence in high mountains or lowly places. It draws on classical natural history and alchemical authorities like Hermes, Pliny, and Virgil to describe the bird's symbolic purity and longevity.</summary>

### Emblem 44. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

-> Typhon kills Osiris through deceit and scatters the parts of his body in all directions, but the famous Isis gathered them together. <-

### Epigram 44.

> Syria has Adonis, Greece has Dionysus,
> Egypt has Osiris; to the wise, they are all the same: <note>The author equates these mythological figures as representations of a single natural or alchemical principle.</note>
> Isis put back together Osiris, who was cut into pieces
> By cruel Typhon;
> But the generative part <note>original: "productive part"; in the myth of Osiris, his phallus was the only part of his body not recovered from the river</note> is lost in the sea,

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discourse on the alchemical interpretation of ancient mythology, transitioning from a general discussion of Osiris to a specific list of mythological figures representing alchemical components.</meta>

# Discourse 44.

The allegory of Osiris was treated elsewhere (namely in the first book of *Hieroglyphics*) where it was reduced to its true origins, which are chemical, and fully explained. Therefore, to repeat the same things here (though the same truths ought to be spoken regarding the same subjects) was judged unnecessary. Nevertheless, we will here form a parallel discourse, which will always remain within the confines of that ancient alchemy <note>original: "Chymy"</note>, which is solely expressed and decoded by the poets. 

<margin><unclear>M.</unclear></margin> Will you persuade me that Osiris was a god, or a king of the Egyptians? I will not believe it, even if you persuade me to believe; for dogs (as the proverb says) smell things quite differently, and swine even more so. I plainly deny him to be a god, and you will agree with me, unless you are a heathen or hold an opinion contradictory to right reason. And that he was no king is demonstrated by all the circumstances which are considered elsewhere. He is the Sun, but the philosophical Sun; the name of the sun is often attributed to it by many authors, which the common people <note>original: "vulgar"</note>, who know no other light but that of the world, have misinterpreted as the literal sun. This philosophical Sun takes its name from the sun of the world because it contains the properties of nature descending from that celestial Sun, or agreeable to it. 

Osiris, therefore—along with Dionysus, Bacchus, Jupiter, Mars, Adonis, Oedipus, Perseus, Achilles, Triptolemus, Pelops, Hippomenes, and Pollux—represents the Sun. Meanwhile, Isis, Juno, Venus, the mother of Oedipus <note>Jocasta</note>, Danae, Deidamia, Atalanta, Helen, and the Moon—as well as Latona, Semele, Europa, Leda, Antiope, and Thalia—represent the Moon. These are the parts of the compound which, before the operation, is called the "Stone," and by the names of every metal, "Magnesia." After the operation, it is called Horus <note>original: "Orus"</note>, Pyrrhus, Apollo, or Aesculapius. 

The negative forces <note>original: "adjuncts"</note> are Typhon, Python, and the Boar. The "Artists" <note>This term refers to the alchemists practicing the "Great Art."</note> are Hercules, Ulysses, Jason, Theseus, and Pirithous. The labors and perils which were undergone by these artists are innumerable: one may see the labors of Hercules, the wanderings <note>original: "errors"</note> of Ulysses, the perils of Jason, the attempts of Theseus, and the rescue <note>original: "removals"</note> of Pirithous. This is the great volume of matter and doctrine, through which Vulcan, Mercury, and Saturn run backwards and forwards on every page—the first as the father and cause of all, without which nothing is achieved; the second as the matter and form; and the third as the efficient cause. 

Sol <note>The Sun</note> takes his sister Luna <note>The Moon</note> as his wife; just as Jupiter takes Juno, Saturn takes Rhea, and Osiris takes Isis. Dionysus is cut out of his mother Semele while she is being consumed by the lightning of Jove, so that he might be matured in the thigh of Jupiter, his father; likewise, Aesculapius is taken out of his mother Coronis. Dionysus, upon reaching maturity, shows men a new way of drinking wine, undertaking an expedition even to the Indies. Osiris and Triptolemus show the sowing and use of fruits, and Aesculapius shows the administration of me— <note>The text cuts off mid-word "medicine."</note>

<summary>The author argues that Egyptian and Greek myths are actually alchemical allegories, where gods and heroes represent chemical elements, processes, and the alchemists themselves.</summary>
