# Discourse 44.

...divine: Dionysus, so called by the Greeks, is Bacchus to the Romans, Osiris to the Egyptians, and Adonis to the Syrians. Oedipus killed his father and married his mother; Perseus slew his grandfather; Typhon slew his brother Osiris; a boar killed Adonis; and Ceres, the nurse of Triptolemus, killed his father Eleusius. Hippomenes <gloss>overcame</gloss> Atalanta by using a golden apple. Tantalus, the father of Pelops, won Hippodamia through a chariot race <note>original: "dispute of a chariott"</note>.

Osiris was cut into pieces and joined together again by Isis, who was his mother, sister, and wife. Meanwhile, Pelops was boiled and prepared as food, and his shoulder was eaten by Ceres; he was later restored to life with an ivory shoulder added. Achilles and Triptolemus were both placed under hot coals at night and nourished with milk during the day: Achilles by <gloss>Thetis</gloss> his nurse, and Triptolemus by his mother <gloss>Ceres</gloss>.

Achilles and Helen were the causes of the Trojan War—Achilles as the impulsive cause and Helen as the efficient cause. Helen was hatched from an egg. At the wedding of Peleus and Thetis (from whom Achilles descended), the apple of Eris—the primary cause of the kidnapping <note>original: "rape"</note> of Helen—was thrown among the guests. 

Pollux was among the Argonauts, who (if they truly existed) are supposed to have lived nearly fifty years before the start of the Trojan War. Since Helen and Pollux both came from the same egg, Helen would have been an old woman when she was abducted by Paris. Medea was married to Achilles in the Elysian Fields <note>the Greek version of the afterlife</note>, though she was then an old, toothless woman—unless, perhaps, she restored her own youth just as she did for Aeson (the father of Jason) or as Ceres did for Pelops, for which reason he is said to have become young twice.

Perseus received a flying horse from Pallas <note>Athena</note> and, in return, brought her the head of Medusa. Mercury <note>Hermes</note> gave him a scimitar, and other gods gave him other weapons. Ceres gave Triptolemus a chariot drawn by flying dragons. It rained gold at Rhodes when Pallas was born from Jupiter’s brain, and also when Sol <note>the Sun</note> lay with Venus. Jupiter, in the form of a golden shower, had intercourse with Danae; he also forced himself upon <note>original: "compressed"</note> Leda as a swan, his sister Juno as a cuckoo, Europa as a bull, and Antiope as a satyr. Thus, there is a consistency in all these tales.

## Emblem 45. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.
->The work is perfected by Sol and his shade.<- <note>original: *Sol*. In alchemical theory, Sol (the Sun) represents the masculine principle, the spirit, and the metal gold.</note>

---

### Epigram 45.
> The Sun can penetrate no dense bodies,
> And so the opposing side creates a shadow.
> Although this shadow is valued least of all things,
> It is very useful to the study of astronomy;
> But the Sun and shadow are of even more use to the Sages, <note>original: *Sophi* (the wise men or alchemists)</note>
> Because they bring the gold-making craft <note>original: *auriferous trade*</note> to perfection.

<vocab>Sol, shade, Sages, gold-making craft, Astronomy, Secrets of Nature</vocab>

<summary>This emblem explains that just as a shadow is necessary for astronomical calculations, the "shade" or hidden aspect of the Sun (gold) is essential for alchemists to complete their work. It emphasizes the importance of dualities—light and dark—in the process of transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Sol, Sun, shadow, Sages, gold-making, astronomy, transmutation, duality</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<page-num>141</page-num>
# Discourse 45.

Just as in a round or spherical palace, a fire lit in one place illuminates the parts above, below, and all around the walls—except for those spots where a table or something similar standing in the middle might block the light and create a dark shadow—so too the Sun, being placed in that great palace or curved theater of the heavens, illuminates every hollow of heaven and the bodies contained within it that are transparent <note>original: "diaphanous"</note> and receptive to light. This includes all stars, whether wandering <note>planets</note> or fixed, except where the thickness of the Earth in the middle may prevent it.

For there, a black and dark shadow, which is called night, continues until it is driven away by the presence of the Sun, and light is established and made visible in its place. A shadow, and therefore night, is the deprivation or absence of solar light, just as day, on the contrary, is the irradiation and surrounding <note>original: "circumfusion"</note> of that light. A shadow is that which cannot endure the sight of the Sun, and therefore avoids and hides itself now in one part of the Earth and then in another, as the Sun moves into opposition. 

The Sun and shadow never saw each other, though, if nature allowed, they might at every moment; but the Sun, considering her as an enemy to himself, <margin>Buchanan <note>George Buchanan (1506–1582), a Scottish scholar and author of the astronomical poem "De Sphaera."</note></margin> always pursues her as she flies, and can never catch her, however weary she may be, as Buchanan in his *Book of Spheres* has cleverly declared. 

Following the example of that great Sun and its shadow, the philosophers have observed that their "Sun" also has a black, cloudy, and fleeing shadow. Therefore, Hermes says: "My son! Take the shadow from the ray." That is, see that you carry your sun around by the *primum mobile* <term>the "first moved" sphere in the geocentric model, believed to carry all other celestial bodies around the Earth once every 24 hours</term>, of which Vulcan <note>the Roman god of fire and metalworking, often representing the alchemical fire</note> is made overseer. This ensures that even that part of your Earth which is now covered with a shady night may obtain the clear light of the Sun. 

For if the whole firmament of heaven, with all it contains, were not revolved every natural day (that is, in twenty-four hours) by this first motion, and if the Sun moved only by its own motion (called the second and annual motion), it would happen that the Antipodes <note>those living on the opposite side of the globe</note> below us would have night for nearly six months while we had day, and afterwards, on the contrary, they would have day and we a night. Thus, the whole year would consist of only one night and one day, as experience and reason confirm is currently the case at both poles.

But it seemed otherwise to Divine Providence, which therefore ordained two motions of the planets—the first and second—and so distributes the year into many days. Now, that shadow and sun together create day and night, which the sun could not do by itself alone; for its proper nature is to illuminate all opposite places and bodies, and it does not make a shadow except indirectly <note>original: "accidentally"</note> through its absence. So also the philosophical Sun creates a "day" (light) and "night" (darkness) with its shadow—namely, Latona or Magnesia <note>alchemical terms for the base matter or "mother" of the philosopher's stone</note>, whose shadow Democritus teaches about, as appears in the third book of his—

<summary>The author compares the astronomical relationship between the Sun and Earth's shadow to alchemical processes, arguing that just as the Sun's motion creates day and night, the "philosophical Sun" must interact with its "shadow" (base matter) through a specific motion or heat.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, alchemy, Sun, shadow, George Buchanan, primum mobile, Vulcan, Hermes, Latona, Magnesia, Democritus, geocentric model</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical discourse on the relationship between light (the Sun) and darkness (the Shadow) in the Hermetic Work, likely following a discussion of the initial stages of the process.</meta>

# Discourse 45.

The golden table mentioned at the beginning must be extinguished and consumed by a fiery medicine. The use of shadows in astronomical matters is so great that without them, that science could hardly be perfected. Chemists <note>original: "Chymists"; here meaning alchemists</note> also credit their own "shadow" for the fact that their art reaches perfection. For what is the Sun without a shadow? It is like a clapper without a bell: the clapper makes the first movement toward a sound, but the bell actually produces the sound. The Sun is the quill <note>the plectrum or pick used to pluck strings</note>, while the shadows are the instrument; the Sun is the tongue, and the shadows are the great mouth. 

A shadow is a very lowly thing, hardly even considered a "being." Similarly, the "philosopher's shadow" is a substance "blacker than black," as they call it, or held in less esteem than common weeds. This low reputation is not because of the thing itself, but because of human opinion and its commonness. What is more useful than fire? What is more precious than water? What is more lovely than the earth, which produces flowers and all pleasant things? What is more delightful than air? (For if air is restrained or blocked, nothing can remain pleasant.) Yet, because these elements are used for so many common purposes across the globe, people—through a backward <note>original: "praepostrous"</note> imagination—consider them worthless. In the same way, both the common shadow and the "philosophical shadow" are undervalued. 

Just as those who live for a long time in underground shadows lose their eyesight if suddenly brought into the clear light of the Sun, those who work only within the "philosophical shadow" and do not join it with the "Sun" lose their judgment and mental vision, failing in their efforts. When the celestial Sun rises in the south, there is greater heat and less shadow. Similarly, in this work, as the heat is increased, the shadow is diminished, and vice versa. 

We must therefore begin when the Sun is on the southern <note>original: "meridionale"</note> side, returning toward the zenith <note>the point directly above one's head</note> in the sign of Capricorn. The first operation will be finished by the time the Sun reaches Aries. Then the "women's work" <note>an alchemical term for a gentle, low-heat process, often associated with washing or whitening</note> begins and continues until the Sun reaches Leo. Afterward, one stage of labor follows another until the "year" takes its tail in its mouth like a snake <note>referring to the Ouroboros, a symbol of cyclical completion</note>—that is to say, until the cycle is completed.

<summary>This page explains the vital importance of the "shadow" (the dark, material aspect) in alchemical work, arguing that just as shadows are necessary for astronomy, they are essential for the perfection of the philosopher's stone. It outlines a symbolic timeline for the Great Work based on the zodiac, from Capricorn through Leo, ending in the completion of the cycle.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, philosopher's shadow, celestial Sun, zodiac, Capricorn, Aries, Leo, women's work, Ouroboros, four elements</keywords>

## Emblem 46. Of the Secrets of Nature.

### Epigram 46.

> To the Eastern and Western parts, for this purpose:
> That he might find the center of the earth;
> Which, returning there, satisfied his mind.
> But these two eagles are two stones, which hasten <note>original: "hast"</note>
> One from the East, the other from the West.

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical interpretation of classical mythology, specifically focusing on the lineage and myths of Apollo as metaphors for chemical processes.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
# Discourse 46.

Cicero, in his book *On the Nature of the Gods* <note>original: *De Natura Deorum*</note>, declares the most ancient Apollo to be the son of Vulcan and the protector of Athens. If this opinion is transferred to an allegory—as it ought to be—it is indeed most true, because Vulcan <note>The god of fire and metalworking</note> produces the "philosophical Sol" <note>The "Philosophical Sun," often representing gold or the perfected substance in alchemy</note>, which is Apollo. 

However, the common belief has prevailed that he was born of Jupiter, and that Latona, having conceived two children by Jupiter—Apollo and Diana—was persecuted by a jealous Juno. Juno sent Python, a serpent of prodigious magnitude and malice, to torment and follow Latona while she was pregnant. After tedious wanderings, the miserable woman was finally brought by a ship to the island of Ortygia to stay with her sister Asteria, who reigned there. That island, which was almost entirely overflowed by the sea, gave a place to Latona while she was in labor. Because of this, the island was called Delos <note>meaning "manifest" or "visible"</note>, though it was previously called Adyndos. 

While in labor there, Diana was born first, and she performed the office of a midwife to her mother as she labored to bring forth her brother Apollo. From this event, it came to pass that women in pregnancy would invoke her deity during labor and call her either Lucina or Ilithyia, because she shows the light to newborn infants when their eyes are first opened. 

Once Apollo was born and grown up, he slew Python, the tormentor of his mother, with arrows. He also killed the Cyclopes because they had forged the lightning for Jupiter to destroy his son, Aesculapius. Jupiter had condemned Aesculapius to be blasted with lightning and sent to hell because he had restored Hippolytus to life after he had been torn to pieces by horses. 

We have demonstrated in many places that these stories are merely alchemical <note>original: "Chymicall"</note>. Latona, Cynthia <note>Another name for Diana, the Moon</note>, Apollo, and Python are all requirements of the Art, and they have such a relationship to one another as has been declared. However, because these things were published in the writings of the most ancient poets—such as Orpheus, Linus, Musaeus, Homer, and others—it gave the ignorant an occasion to assign religion and veneration to Apollo. 

Consequently, Apollo is worshipped in many places across Europe and Asia, and innumerable temples have been erected to him. He had a temple in Delphi especially intended for worship according to ancient religion; within it, a great number of statues made of solid gold and silver, of great weight and craftsmanship, were deposited by kings and princes, along with other most precious gifts hidden in sacred places for the sake of religion by men of all ranks. Pausanias reports that a brass <note>original: "brazen"</note> skeleton of admirable craftsmanship was hung at the top of the temple by Hippocrates. That famous tripod was also consecrated to Apollo by Pelops at the time when he married Hippodamia, the daughter of Oenomaus, King of Elis. This tripod was made by Mulciber <note>Another name for Vulcan</note>, who gave it to Pelops. It was erected in the middle of the temple, and upon it the Pythia <note>The priestess of Apollo who delivered oracles</note> would sit.

<summary>This page interprets the mythological birth and deeds of Apollo and Diana as alchemical allegories, arguing that ancient poets used these stories to hide chemical secrets which the "ignorant" later turned into literal religion.</summary>

# Discourse 47.

The philosophers have in many places mentioned two stones given to us for free <note>original: "gratis"</note>, as Isaac <note>likely Isaac Hollandus, a 15th-century alchemist</note>, Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova</note>, and others [have done]. Among them, Avicenna <note>a Persian polymath and alchemist (c. 980–1037)</note> says they lie in dung, neglected and rejected by the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note>; if these are joined together, they are able to complete the Magistery <term>the successful completion of the alchemical process to create the Philosopher's Stone</term>. 

Some men extol the Western <note>original: "occidentall"</note> Mercury, which has preferred itself before gold and overcome it. But the author of the *Advice on the Marriage of Sol and Luna* <note>Sol and Luna: Sun and Moon, representing Gold and Silver, or Sulphur and Mercury</note>, taken from the *Epistle of Aristotle* <note>a pseudo-Aristotelian alchemical text</note>, describes the two stones best of all when he says: "There are two principal stones of your art, the white and the red, of a wonderful nature. At the setting of the sun, the white begins to appear upon the surface of the water, hiding itself until midnight and afterwards sinking <note>original: "declining"</note> to the bottom. But the red operates in the opposite way <note>original: "contrariwise"</note>; it begins to ascend upon the water at the rising of the sun until noon, and then descends to the bottom."

These stones, therefore, are the aforementioned Eagles sent by Jupiter from Delphi <note>in Greek mythology, Jupiter released two eagles to find the center of the world; they met at Delphi</note>. These are also the wolf and the dog coming from different or opposite parts of the earth, which bite and harass <note>original: "worry"</note> one another until both become mad, as Rhazes <note>original: "Rhasis"; Persian physician and alchemist (c. 865–925)</note> declares in his epistle. 

These stones are the truest Bezoar <term>a stony mass found in the stomachs of animals, historically believed to be an antidote to poison</term>. The East Indies send the best of these, drawn from the bowels of wild beasts; the West Indies yield another of less efficacy, obtained at Peru <note>original: "peruana"</note> from tame animals. For the East affords a most fierce wolf, and the West a dog familiar to men. That is to say, Sulphur comes from the East and Mercury from the Western region. The Mercury <note>original: "this"</note> is gentle and tractable, while the Sulphur <note>original: "that"</note> is irritable <note>original: "cholericke"</note> and angry. When these two first find one another, they begin to fight <note>original: "fall together by the ears"</note>. The dog, being of notable size, obtains the first victory, knocking the wolf down and bringing him to death's door. But the wolf, recovering his strength afterward, overthrows the dog; once the dog is cast down, the wolf never leaves him until he is utterly killed and dead. In the meantime, the wolf receives from the dog wounds no less mortal than those he gave, until they harass one another to death. 

Regarding the wolf, Rosinus <note>Zosimos of Panopolis, a 3rd-century alchemist</note> says to Euthesia <note>original: "Euthicia"</note>: "He is a fortified soldier, the conqueror of two, held in high esteem and of most intense strength. He penetrates <note>original: "perforating"</note> bodies when he meets them and appears white—a fact proven by experiment. He is the man who took Luna <note>the Moon, representing silver or the feminine principle</note> as his wife, whom some men suppose to be gold of a most precious connection and composition. His congealed part is never dissolved, nor are his footsteps or impressions erased <note>original: "defaced"</note>; God has freely bestowed this upon the pious and chosen <note>original: "elect"</note> philosophers. You must know that nature has found an equal as an enemy." 

These are his words. And a little after he says: "Sulphur is most strong, fighting against the fire which it contains and by which it is contained. A most precious color proceeds from them when they are joined together, and that Sulphur—which is naturally volatile—can never afterward fly away because the soul has..."

<summary>This page describes the dual nature of alchemical principles (Sulphur and Mercury) through metaphors of the wolf, the dog, and celestial movements, citing authorities like Avicenna and Zosimos. It emphasizes the violent but necessary union of these opposing substances to achieve the alchemical Magistery.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Magistery, Sulphur, Mercury, Bezoar, Sol and Luna, Avicenna, Rhazes, Zosimos, chemical allegory</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>The text continues the discussion of alchemical principles, specifically the interaction between the soul (tincture) and the body, and the symbolic relationship between Sulphur and Mercury.</meta>

# Discourse 47.

...perforates it. In the same way, the tincture <note>In alchemy, a "tincture" is a spiritual or transformative essence capable of changing the properties of a substance.</note> of the soul has both perforated and mixed with the body, and the body has contained the soul and restrained the natural part from flying away. And then, to one demanding which of the stones was strongest, he answers: "That stone which is no stone <note>A common alchemical paradox referring to the Philosopher's Stone, which is a physical substance but possesses spiritual properties.</note> is stronger than the other enemy; but the red is stronger than that which strengthens its companion by its fortitude."

The Oriental wolf <note>A symbolic representation of Sulphur.</note> is therefore stronger than the Occidental dog <note>A symbolic representation of Mercury.</note>, though he does not obtain the victory effectively but perishes together with the enemy; however, the stinging poison is produced from both. The difference between a wolf and a dog is small, as a mastiff or great dog represents the form and shape of a wolf, so as to seem like a wolf originally but made tame by long breeding <note>original: "progeneration"</note>. In the same manner, Sulphur and Mercury differ little from one another, because one proceeds from the other: Mercury did indeed beget Sulphur, but Sulphur purges Mercury and made it so.

Concerning these, the same Rosinus <note>likely referring to Zosimos of Panopolis, a 3rd-century alchemist whose name was often Latinized as Rosinus in medieval texts</note>, when asked, "From where comes its color?" answers, "From its most intense bitterness." 

And again: "From where comes its bitterness and intensity?" He answers, "From the impurity of its metal." 

And again: "Is its red color never pre-eminent?" He answers, "Yes." 

And again: "Is it also never hotter than fire?" He answers, "Fire, in respect to it, is as water in respect to fire." 

And again: "Is it not stronger than fire?" He answers, "No." 

And again: "Why, therefore, do you affirm it to be stronger than fire?" He answers, "Because fires meeting together destroy one another." 

It is therefore manifest that one becomes the food and nourishment <note>original: "aliment"</note> of the other, and as much as one decreases, the other increases, until that which increases prevails and the Dragon devours the serpent. In great fights, it often happens that those who undergo the greater slaughter win the spoils and victory. So also, though the dog lay prostrate, yet he was not altogether overcome; he held his enemy so fast that the wolf could not live without him, nor could the dog die without the other.

<summary>This page explores the alchemical union of opposites—Sulphur and Mercury—through the metaphors of a wolf and a dog, explaining how these conflicting elements must consume one another to achieve transformation. The text uses a traditional Q&A format attributed to the alchemist Rosinus to describe the properties of the resulting "red" stone.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Sulphur, Mercury, Rosinus, Zosimos of Panopolis, Oriental wolf, Occidental dog, tincture, Philosopher's Stone, Dragon and serpent</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical commentary on Emblem 48 of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens." The text draws parallels between historical anecdotes of kings and physicians and the alchemical process of "curing" the base matter (the King) to achieve the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

# Discourse 48.

Xerxes, that most powerful King of Persia, leading his army through dry and rugged places in hot weather, being thirsty, did not refuse a draught of muddy water offered by a soldier, but drank most freely and rewarded him that brought it very amply. And indeed, if a man at this very time (as some of the latest historians affirm) travels through the borders of Persia, it is said that fountains of sweet water are very rarely found, standing waters being brackish, and the soil itself on the surface yielding much saltiness. After the same manner the King, whom the philosophers have made mention of, being overwhelmed with thirst, commanded plenty of sweet water to be brought to him, of which he drank his fill; as is manifest to any man by the Allegory of Merlin <note>A famous alchemical parable in which a King drinks an immense amount of water, dissolves, and is eventually reborn in a purified state.</note>.

The cure of this sick and discolored King is undertaken by several physicians. The Egyptians, administering their medicines, stirred the humors while they were still crude—which Hippocrates affirms ought to be purged only after being first "concocted" <note>original: "concocted"; in early medicine, this referred to the "ripening" or "cooking" of bodily fluids by the body's natural heat before they could be safely expelled.</note>, unless they are fluid and unstable. For then they must be immediately expelled so that they might not by chance make an assault upon the more noble parts or bowels. Whereupon dangerous symptoms, such as <term>Lipothymy</term> <note>A sudden sensation of fainting or physical weakness.</note> and <term>Syncope</term> <note>A temporary loss of consciousness caused by a fall in blood pressure.</note> happened to the King. But the physicians of Alexandria coming last of all—and often being more successful in a chronic disease—restored the King to his former health. 

It is well worth the effort to cure so great a King, who, being made healthy, extends a liberal hand and a serene countenance to his physician. We have read that many physicians have been very nobly rewarded by various Kings: such as Democides by Polycrates, the tyrant of Samos, with two talents; or Erasistratus (who, as Pliny writes, was a scholar of Chrysippus, born to the daughter of Aristotle) for curing King Antiochus, who was sick for the love of his stepmother Stratonice—he was rewarded with a hundred talents by Antiochus's son, Ptolemy. Or Jacob Coctier <note>original: "Jacob Cocterius"; the personal physician to King Louis XI of France.</note>, physician to Louis XI, King of France, from whom he received four thousand crowns every month as a stipend. We make no mention of others more recent; but the cure of this King <note>The "King" here refers to the alchemical matter being transformed.</note> is recompensed with an even greater gratuity or reward. 

For so say Hermes and Geber in the <term>Rosary</term> <note>Referring to the "Rosarium Philosophorum" (The Rosary of the Philosophers), a seminal 16th-century alchemical text.</note>: "He that has once completed this art, if he should live ten hundred thousand years and every day feed four thousand men, would not be in want." Senior <note>Zadith ben Hamuel, a 10th-century alchemist often cited as "Senior" in Western texts.</note> confirms this, saying: "He that has that stone, from which the Elixir is made, is so rich that he is like one who has fire; he can give fire to whom he will, and when he will, and as much as he will, without his own detriment or lack."

The father of Democritus was so rich that he gave a feast to the army of Xerxes; and Pythius, an exceedingly wealthy man, offered to the same King pay for his whole army and victuals for five months, provided that the youngest of his five sons—who was the comfort of his old age—might be permitted to stay at home and not be forced to go to war.

<summary>This page uses historical examples of royal physicians and wealthy figures to illustrate the immense rewards of the alchemical "cure" of the King (the matter). It emphasizes that the successful alchemist attains a source of inexhaustible wealth comparable to a flame that can light other fires without being diminished.</summary>
<keywords>Xerxes, King, Physician, Merlin's Allegory, Hippocrates, Lipothymy, Syncope, Erasistratus, Jacob Coctier, Rosarium Philosophorum, Senior, Elixir, Philosopher's Stone</keywords>

<meta>This is the final page of the commentary for Discourse 48 of Atalanta Fugiens. The text concludes the alchemical interpretation of the King's health, using the historical anecdote of Pythius and Xerxes (as recorded by Sabellicus) to contrast earthly wealth with alchemical "riches," and referencing the alchemist Count Bernhard of Trevisan.</meta>

->Discourse 48.<-

into the King's army. But the barbarous King [Xerxes], taking the petition of Pythius most unworthily, commanded his younger son to be cut into two pieces and fastened to stakes on both sides of the highway by which his whole army was to pass, as Sabellicus <note>Marcus Antonius Coccius Sabellicus (1436–1506), a Venetian historian.</note> observes in Book 2, Ennead 3.

But the riches of these men are nothing compared to the riches of our King, which are without dimension and number. Being purged and free from the water, all the kings and potentates of other regions honored and feared him. And when they were willing to see any of his wonderful works, they put one ounce of <term>mercury</term> <note>Quicksilver, which in alchemy must be purified before it can be transformed.</note> (well washed) into a crucible, and cast upon it a tiny amount—about the size of a millet seed—of his nails, his hair, or his blood. Having blown gently on it with coals, they let it cool, and found such a stone as I know. 

This is he whom Count Bernhard <note>Bernhard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a celebrated alchemist whose works often used allegories of a king bathing to represent chemical processes.</note> mentions, who gives to his six servants <note>original: "servitors." In alchemical allegory, these usually represent the six other metals—lead, tin, iron, copper, mercury, and silver—which are "ennobled" into gold.</note> as much of his kingdom as he himself possesses, provided they wait until he recovers his youth in the bath <note>The chemical dissolution and purification of the matter.</note> and is adorned with various garments: namely, a black breastplate, a white shirt, and purple blood. For then he promises to give some of his blood to every one of them and make them partakers of his riches.

<summary>This page concludes Discourse 48 by contrasting the cruelty of worldly kings with the generosity of the alchemical King (the Philosopher's Stone), who shares his wealth with his "servants" after undergoing a transformative "bath." The text uses the colors of the alchemical stages—black, white, and purple—to describe the Stone's completion.</summary>

# Emblem 49. Of the Secrets of Nature.
## The philosophical infant <note>The "philosophical infant" is a recurring alchemical metaphor for the Philosopher's Stone in its developing or nascent state.</note> is, like Orion, the son of three fathers.

---

### Epigram 49.
->Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire, representing the alchemist's furnace and controlled heat.</note> with Hermes <note>The Greek god associated with wisdom and transformation, often identified with the "mercury" of the philosophers.</note> and Apollo <note>The Greek god of the Sun, representing natural celestial heat and gold.</note> did<-
->Inject their seed into an ox's hide,<-
->From which the great Orion was produced:<-
->Three fathers also has the Sophic Son <note>original: "Sophick Sonne"; the "Son of Wisdom," another name for the Philosopher's Stone or the refined alchemical result.</note>;<-
->The Sun is first, Vulcan the next is second,<-
->The artist <note>The alchemist himself, whose labor and intellect guide the transformation.</note> is the third father of this child.<-

<summary>This emblem explains the origins of the alchemical "child" (the Philosopher's Stone) by comparing it to the mythological birth of the giant Orion, who was said to be born from the combined essence of three gods.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Orion, Vulcan, Hermes, Apollo, Philosopher's Stone, Sophic Son, nature, heat, alchemist, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discourse on the generation of life, transitioning from biological observations to the symbolic language of alchemy.</meta>

# Discourse 49.

Those women who prostitute themselves to a variety of men seldom conceive living children because of the confusion of various seeds. For nature, in the generation of humans and most animals, admits no superfetation <term>the formation of a second fetus while one is already present in the uterus</term>, except perhaps very rarely. Therefore, every offspring is produced from only one father and mother, whether there is one child or more. This is also shown by history and the fate of some who supposed otherwise. 

The first example is Margaret, the wife of Herman, Earl of Henneberg, who, in the year 1276, gave birth to 365 children. They were all baptized; the males were named John and the females Elizabeth. Their tomb can still be seen in the Church of Loosduinen <note>original: Lausdune</note>, one mile distant from the Hague toward the sea in Holland, together with the bronze basin in which they were baptized and an inscription of the story. The cause of this was that the Countess, seeing a poor woman carrying twin children in her arms, called her an adulteress, as if it were impossible for more than one child to be born from one conception by one man, but necessarily by several fathers. Thereupon, a curse was made by the poor woman, who knew she was innocent of that accusation. The Countess then conceived in her womb at one time, and by one man, as many children as there are days in the year. This is indeed a miracle, yet a natural work that happened through the vengeance of God. 

But in the "philosophical work" <note>Alchemical practice was often called the "Great Work" or the "philosophical work"</note>, that which is otherwise contrary to nature is safely permitted under the veil of an allegory. For here, one infant is said to have three fathers, or perhaps two, as well as a mother. Regarding this, Raymund <note>likely Ramon Llull, a 13th-century philosopher often associated with alchemical texts</note>, cited by the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note>, says: "Our infant has two fathers and two mothers, and because he and his entire substance is tenderly nourished in fire, he therefore never dies." 

So Dionysus, or Bacchus, is said to have two mothers; he was ripped out of his mother’s belly (she being burned) before he was mature and was then matured in his father’s thigh, so that the father became a mother. But these things are better explained in the conception of Orion, who is said to be produced from the seeds of Apollo, Vulcan, and Mercury mixed together and preserved in the hide of an ox for the space of ten months. These things would be entirely <margin><insert>Well</insert> <note>original: Ben; likely "bene," a Latin marginal mark meaning "well noted"</note></margin> monstrous, and not merely mythical, if a secret of nature were not hidden under these veils, which are not obvious to everyone. 

Lully <note>Ramon Llull</note>, in the *Theory* of his *Testament*, attributes to the same philosophical infant many and almost the same fathers—namely Sol (the Sun), who is Apollo. Or rather, the celestial Sun is the first author of this generation. Through his inexpressible power—both hidden and astral—he operates upon a certain matter known to the philosophers, as if upon the matrix <term>the womb or the place where something is generated</term>.

<summary>This page discusses the biological rarity of multiple conceptions (superfetation), citing the legend of Margaret of Henneberg's 365 children, before explaining how alchemy uses myths of multiple parents (like Bacchus and Orion) as allegories for natural secrets.</summary>
<keywords>superfetation, Margaret of Henneberg, alchemy, philosophical infant, Bacchus, Orion, Ramon Llull, Sol, generation, Loosduinen</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the alchemical "infant" or philosopher's stone, specifically focusing on its education and the roles of various symbolic "fathers" in its creation.</meta>

->Discourse 49.<-

...of a woman, and in her produces a son or infant like himself, to whom he will afterward leave and hand over his arms and insignias of virtue by paternal right. These include the faculty of maturing things that are immature, and the energy of tinging <note>original: "tinging," meaning dyeing or transforming the color and nature of a substance, a key alchemical process</note> and purging things that are neither tinged nor purged. For whatever Sol <note>original: "Sol," the Sun, representing gold or the primary male alchemical principle</note> is able to perfect in a thousand years, his son will perform in half an hour. To ensure that virtue may be made a thousand times stronger in him than in Sol, his father delivers him to Vulcan <note>The god of fire, representing the controlled heat of the alchemist's furnace</note> and the Artist <note>The alchemist or practitioner</note> together for his education, so that his noble disposition may be polished and his virtues multiplied by their means. It is manifest that practicing from childhood is very beneficial.

Thus, Achilles, Jason, and Hercules were committed to Chiron <note>The wise centaur of Greek mythology who mentored many heroes</note> to be instructed for the same purpose. Consider Milo of Croton <note>A famous ancient Greek wrestler celebrated for his legendary strength</note>, who as a boy carried a calf, and by habit carried an ox when he became a man. Furthermore, Sol, Vulcan, and the Artist are not wrongly considered the fathers of this child; for while Sol was the first, the others shaped him into someone so great. No equal compensation can be given to teachers for their instruction, just as no reward is sufficient for parents for the act of generation. Parents compose the body, while teachers compose the mind. If the mind is superior to the body, no less a reward is due to the teachers than to the parents. 

In the production of Orion, Mercury contributes the matter, Apollo the form, and Vulcan the heat (the external efficient cause). Likewise, in the philosophical work <note>The *Magnum Opus* or "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>, it is fitting to see three fathers working together for the procreation of one infant, in whom the philosophers take great delight.

<summary>This page uses the allegory of a son’s education to explain how the alchemist (the Artist) and fire (Vulcan) accelerate the natural processes of the Sun (Sol) to create the Philosopher's Stone. It emphasizes that the refinement of the "infant" through art and heat is as vital as its initial generation.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Sol, Vulcan, Artist, Chiron, Milo of Croton, Orion, Mercury, Apollo, philosophical work, transmutation, education</keywords>

# Emblem 50. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

->The Dragon kills the woman, and she kills him, and they are both covered<-
->in blood.<-

## Epigram 50.

Dig a cavernous <note>original: "cavery"</note> place suitable for the Dragon;
And there, join him with the woman face to face.
While she reaps the joys of marriage <note>original: "wedlocke"</note>, she dies;
Entomb him with her, so that he may not rise.
Thus he is killed and stained <note>original: "ting'd"</note> with blood all over;
Follow only this course, and you need ask for no more.

<summary>This emblem describes a symbolic alchemical process using the metaphor of a lethal union between a dragon and a woman, representing the purification and transformation of base matter through "death" and "staining."</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, emblem, Dragon, symbolic marriage, chemical transformation, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical discourse on the symbolic relationship between various substances, specifically focusing on the interaction between the "Dragon" (earth/fire) and the "Eagle" or "Woman" (air/water).</meta>

# Discourse 50.

The home of dragons is in the caverns of the earth, but the residence of men is above the earth in the open air. These two elements [Earth and Air] are opposites, yet the philosophers have determined they should be joined together so that one may act upon the other. By "the woman," they mean what Basil <note>original: "Basill"; likely referring to Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist</note> says in his second "Key": It is not suitable or convenient for an Eagle to build her nest upon the Alps, because her young ones would die from the coldness of the snow at the top of the mountain.

But if you add a "cold dragon" to the Eagle—one that has lived for a long time in the rocks and crept out of the caves of the earth—and put them both into an <term>infernal cell</term> <note>original: "infernale Cell"; a reference to a furnace or specialized heating vessel in an alchemical laboratory</note>, then Pluto <note>The Roman god of the underworld, here representing subterranean or intense heat</note> will blow a blast. This will draw a volatile, fiery spirit out of the cold dragon, which, with its intense heat, will burn the Eagle's feathers and produce such a <term>sweat-inducing bath</term> <note>original: "sudorificke bath"; a process involving heat and condensation intended to "cleanse" the matter</note> that it melts the snow at the top of the mountain and turns it into water. From this, a wonderful bath may be prepared, bringing fortune and health to the King <note>In alchemy, the "King" often symbolizes the perfected stone or gold</note>.

It is indeed remarkable that a cold dragon should yield a fiery spirit; yet experience proves this to be true in the case of burned serpents, which emit a venomous flame that poisons those standing nearby. It is not for nothing that the guardians of <term>chemical</term> <note>original: "Chymicall"</note> treasures are called <term>fire-breathing</term> <note>original: "flammivomous"</note> dragons—just like the guardians of the Golden Fleece, the Hesperian gardens, Cadmus, and similar legends.

This dragon lives in the narrow crevices of underground rocks. You must take it from there and join it to the Eagle or the "woman"—joining it to her in the "grave" or, if you prefer, in the "nest." For the nature of a dragon is sometimes to lie in wait for the Eagle's eggs and wage deadly war against the Eagle. Some Greek writers even claim that in ancient times, a dragon fell in love with a young woman and slept with her.

Is it any wonder, then, that the philosophers would want their dragon shut up in a cavern with a woman? Greverus <note>Jodocus Greverus, a 16th-century alchemical author</note> joins red and black dragons together in the deep abyss of the mountains and burns them with fire. When the black one perishes, he says that the "keeper of the mountains" searches for them everywhere and brings them back to the mountains. <term>Merlin</term> <note>The legendary wizard, to whom many medieval prophetic and alchemical texts were attributed</note> in his vision (if it is not a forgery) mentions a white and a red dragon.

These dragons, whatever they may be—whether one is a woman or a female dragon—act upon each other until they both die and shed blood from their wounds, becoming soaked in it. This explains how the elements of earth and fire are represented by the dragon, and how the woman represents the elements of air and water. On this subject, the text <term>The Blare of the Trumpet</term> <note>original: "Clangor bucina" (Latin: *Clangor Buccinae*), a famous alchemical treatise</note> says: "The dragon is the matter remaining at the bottom after the water has been distilled from it." And according to <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of the Hermetic arts and alchemy</note>: "The water of the air, being between heaven and earth, is the life of everything; for that water dissolves a body into a..."

<meta>Catchword "a" at bottom right corner indicates the next page begins with this word.</meta>

<summary>This page explains alchemical symbolism where the "Dragon" (representing Earth and Fire) is joined with the "Eagle" or "Woman" (Air and Water) in a heated vessel. This process, supported by references to Basil Valentine and Hermes Trismegistus, describes the dissolution of matter to create a medicinal or transformative "bath."</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, dragon, eagle, Basil Valentine, Hermes Trismegistus, four elements, chemical wedding, Clangor Buccinae, Greverus</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<page-num>157</page-num>
<header>Discourse 50</header>

Spirit, and vivifies <note>original: "vivifyes"; brings to life or animates</note> a dead thing, and creates a marriage <note>original: "matrimony"; in alchemy, this refers to the "chemical wedding" or union of opposing principles like sulfur and mercury</note> between man and woman: for it confers the whole benefit of the art. Regarding the earth, we also speak in this way. Furthermore, understand that the specific earth which we walk upon is not the true element, but is rather composed of its true <term>fifth element</term> <note>also known as the quintessence, the pure essence of a substance</note>. Nor does the fifth elemental substance withdraw from its composed body from which the earth is formed. 

And a little after: 

But the pure and true element, which fire cannot burn, is in the center of the earth. This is the **dragon** of which we speak, working its way even into the center of the earth where, the heat being great, it conceives within itself a flaming heat with which it burns the **woman** or **eagle**. The woman or eagle is indeed an "airy water," which some call the white or celestial eagle; they believe it is made of common mercury or <term>sublimed salt</term>, following the ideas of certain men who are blind in this art while pretending to be sharp-sighted. 

But says **Count Bernhard** <note>Bernhard Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist</note> in his Epistle:

> I tell you truly, that no water dissolves a metallic species by natural reduction, except that which remains with it in matter and form, and which the metals themselves can sustain <note>original: "perlongate"; to extend, endure, or prolong</note>.

And a little after:

> Nor does that water belong to bodies in solution if it does not remain with them when they solidify <note>original: "congelation"</note>.

And a little after:

> I tell you truly, that the oil which naturally attacks and joins natures together, and naturally introduces a medium for the staining <note>original: "tinging"; the process of changing the color or nature of a metal</note> of other bodies, is not composed of any other foreign <note>original: "extraneons"</note> thing, but only of the innermost parts <note>original: "bowells"</note> of the body which is to be dissolved.

Therefore, once this is grasped, the eagle and woman—as well as the dragon and the secrets of almost the entire art—are understood. We have perhaps explained and declared these things too openly to the **Sons of Learning** <note>a traditional term for students or initiates of alchemy</note> so that God may be glorified through this work.

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical "Fifth Element" found in the center of the earth and uses symbolic language (the Dragon and the Eagle) to explain the nature of the universal solvent. It cites the alchemist Bernhard Trevisan to argue that the true solvent must come from the substance of the metal itself rather than external chemicals.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, quintessence, Bernhard Trevisan, the Dragon, the Eagle, chemical marriage, Sons of Learning, metallic solution, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page serves as the introductory epigram for Michael Maier’s *Atalanta Fugiens* (1617), a famous alchemical emblem book. It sets the stage by using the Greek myth of the race between Atalanta and Hippomenes as an allegory for alchemical processes involving "Sulphur" and "Mercury."</meta>

->The Author’s Epigram<-

The Hesperian apples <note>Golden apples from the Garden of the Hesperides, which granted immortality</note> of the Cyprian lady <note>original: "Cyprian dame"; a reference to Venus, the goddess of love, who was associated with the island of Cyprus</note>
Gave the nimble youth a famous victory:
Closely pursued by the swift virgin’s speed,
He throws one into view, so that she might slow her pace.
She is gone; he follows after, swifter than the wind,
While he influences her mind with another lure,
Diverting the maiden's chase once again;
But wait, neither of them has lost the race yet.
On and on they run; the youth lets a third apple fall,
And then Atalanta yields herself and everything else.
Hippomenes represents the strength of <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, solar, or male principle</note>;
She, possessing the wisdom of <term>Hermes</term> <note>original: "wise of Hermes"; referring to Mercury or Quicksilver, the volatile or female principle in alchemy</note>, is overcome by his.
By mixing their loves in Cybele’s <note>An ancient Phrygian Mother Goddess</note> sacred place,
They move her chaste soul to wrath by that disgrace,
Transmuting their physical forms, in revenge,
Into a lion and a lion's mate.
To better portray this through a princely chase,
My Muse describes it in a three-fold way;
Depicting first the fruit that slows their speed—
One runs ahead, the third is in hot pursuit.
Your eyes may behold these <term>Emblems</term> <note>Symbolic illustrations accompanied by text, common in 17th-century philosophy</note> with ease,
But reason must unfold the mysteries.
I have made these things familiar, so that the mind
May seek and find the treasures enclosed here;
Whatever wealth exists in the world, or health in medicine,
These two united lions comprise it all.

## Michael Maier, Doctor of Medicine, President of the Imperial Consistory, Knight, etc., most humbly offers, presents, and dedicates this testimony (such as it is) of his goodwill and gratitude to the most magnificent, famous, and most excellent Senatorial Order of the Imperial Republic of Mülhausen in Thuringia, consisting of men incomparable for virtue, learning, and true nobleness of mind;

->as also<-

->to the most vigilant Syndic <note>A syndic was a high-ranking government official or legal representative.</note> thereof, Christopher<-
->Reinart, a most eminent Doctor of both laws, etc.,<-
->being all his honored and much esteemed<-
->friends and Masters.<-

Most honorable and most prudent Senators! Regarding the Tripod <note>A three-legged stand or stool, often associated with the Pythia at Delphi who delivered oracles.</note> given by Vulcan to Pelops the Bridegroom when he married Hippodamia, the daughter of Oenomaus, King of Elis: they report that, being a work of singular craftsmanship, it was presented again by Pelops to Apollo Pythius at Delphi. Upon this tripod, oracles were sometimes uttered by a virgin through the inspiration of that God. 

So, this Tripod, intricately crafted by Vulcan, having come into my hands, I was moved by the example of Pelops. I resolved to present and dedicate it to the most worthy place and order—indeed, to your Honors and Excellencies above all others. This is not so that Oracles should arise from it (though such things are not to be expected here, but rather <term>chemical</term> <note>original: "Chymicall"; in this era, "chemical" referred to alchemical processes and laboratory work.</note> matters), but to publicly testify to the readiness and goodwill of my mind toward you. 

In your most high and worthy state, you were pleased a few years ago to declare your opinion regarding this matter to me, while I was then serving as physician to his Imperial Majesty, Rudolph II <margin>of blessed memory</margin>. Since that time, I have always, as far as my ability extended, promoted your virtues abroad, not only in words. I have also been careful and diligent in considering how I might more clearly demonstrate and more fully express my devotion through action. 

Realizing this could only be done through a literary gift, and having put some effort into this "running Atalanta" <note>A reference to the mythological Atalanta, known for her speed, and the title of Maier's book, *Atalanta Fugiens*.</note>, I desired to dedicate it entirely to your Honors and Excellencies. In this, I imitate the writers of both our own and former ages, who would never appear in public without the protection of a Scipio <note>A reference to Scipio Africanus, symbolizing a powerful and virtuous patron who provides "conduct" or protection to an author.</note>; for if they had fallen, who could have helped them? I entreat that I...

...may serve you, the patrons of this little work—not because you have the opportunities (either theirs or mine, as far as I know) for this study, for you do not have much leisure from your most important affairs, but because you seem to me able to defend any branch of learning. As things stand, I have not encountered any matter more worthy or more honorable, once any abuse of the subject is excluded and set aside. Whatever it may be, value my effort not according to the poor quality of the writing, but by the sincerity of my mind, of which I am certain; and in the future, receive and record me among the most dutiful of your Honors' servants. Farewell.

## The Preface to the Reader.

Sincere Reader!
It is a truth denied by none that man, in his composition, represents a small summary <note>original: compendium</note> of the universe, and is destined to live three kinds of life. Namely: a vegetative <note>original: vegetable</note> life in his mother’s womb, where he indeed grows and increases like a plant; a sensory <note>original: sensible</note> life in this world, where for the most part he is led by the senses like all other animals (from which he differs by beginning to exercise the understanding, though imperfectly); and lastly an intellectual <note>original: intelligible</note> life in the next world, with God and the Intelligences <note>In Hermetic philosophy, "Intelligences" refer to higher spiritual beings or angels associated with the planets and the divine mind.</note> or good angels who assist Him. In this life, the closer a man comes to the divine nature, the more he rejoices and takes delight in searching out subtle, wonderful, and rare things through the understanding. On the contrary, the more a man declines and degenerates into a beastly state, the less he is moved by these things, being bound to a more physical sense.

<margin>We see examples of both: some—namely the more learned—are adorned with these arts and sciences, but very many others (to use their own words) are slaves to the pleasures of the body: lust, gluttony, sloth, pride, and pomp.</margin>

Now, to instruct and perfect the understanding, God has hidden infinite secrets in nature, which are found out <insert>by</insert> innumerable arts and sciences—like fire struck out of a flint—and put to use. Among these, <term>chemical</term> <note>In the 17th century, "chemical" (Chymicall) referred to the art of alchemy as well as material chemistry.</note> secrets are not the least important; rather, next to the investigation <note>original: indagation</note> of divine things, they are the most principal and precious of all. They are not to be investigated by wandering <note>original: circumforaneous</note> impostors and <term>pseudo-chemical</term> <note>False alchemists, often called "puffers," who lacked the philosophical foundation of the work.</note> drones—who in these matters are like asses at the harp <note>original: asini ad Lyram; a Latin proverb for someone who is unsuited for a task or lacks appreciation for a refined art.</note> because they are altogether strangers to all good learning and intention—but by more lofty wits, who are generously educated and born to greater things. These secrets are very subtle, concise, sacred, rare, and hidden, and therefore are to be comprehended by the understanding sooner than by the senses, rather by profound contemplation—

<summary>This page concludes the dedication to the Senators of Mülhausen and opens the Preface, defining man as a microcosm of the universe who must use his intellect to uncover the hidden chemical and divine secrets of nature.</summary>
<keywords>microcosm, vegetative life, sensory life, intellectual life, chemical secrets, alchemy, Frankfurt am Main, Michael Maier, asini ad Lyram</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface from page 4, completing the word "contemplation." It develops the argument that the "spiritual senses" (sight, hearing, and intellect) should be engaged simultaneously through the combination of emblems, music, and alchemical theory.</meta>

[Contem]plation upon the reading of authors, and comparing them together and with the works of nature, is better than sensory operation or manual experimentation, which is blind without prior theory. Next after such intellectual sciences as these, we welcome those which treat of visible and audible objects, such as optics (or perspective) and painting—which is called "silent poetry" by some poets, just as they call poetry a "speaking picture." Likewise, we welcome music, whether vocal or instrumental. In these arts, the ancient philosophers exercised themselves so much that anyone who refused the harp at a festival—as is said of Themistocles—was reputed to be unlearned and fit only "to sing to the myrtle" <note>A Greek custom where those unable to play the lyre held a branch of myrtle instead while singing.</note>. Socrates was instructed in music, as was Plato himself, who concluded that any person who does not delight in musical harmony is not harmoniously composed in their own soul. Pythagoras was very famous for this; he is said to have used the symphony of music every morning and evening to calm the minds of his disciples.

For there is a peculiar power in music to quicken or refresh the emotions by different musical rhythms. For example, the Greeks termed the Phrygian mode "warlike" because it was sung in war and during engagements, having a singular power to stir up the spirits of the soldiers. In its place, the cornet is sometimes used for the same purpose, though it was formerly considered amorous (just as the Phrygian mode is <insert>now</insert> reputed to be); from this, we see how things change from one to another. It is said that Timotheus of Miletus <note>original: "Timothy Milesio"</note> used the Phrygian mode to make Alexander the Great more spirited and courageous in warlike affairs, which Cicero takes notice of in the second book of *On the Laws* <note>original: "2. de Legibus"</note>. On the other hand, Terpander relied on the cornet; having been sent for by the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans.</note> while civil unrest was raging among them, he moved and affected their minds by the sweetness of his singing so much that they returned to friendship and ceased their rebellion. For this reason, the Lesbian singers were always preferred in the judgment of the Spartans.

<margin>
X Many other things are joined to this, involving not only many senses but very many others.
</margin>

Quintilian <note>original: "Fabius," referring to Marcus Fabius Quintilianus.</note> asks why we should study music, answering that music is an honorable and pleasant recreation most suitable for noble minds. Therefore, so that we might have those three objects of the more spiritual senses—namely seeing, hearing, and the understanding itself—in a single view and embrace, and to introduce them all at once into many minds for better comprehension, behold: we have joined optics with music, and the senses with the understanding. That is, we have combined rare things to be seen and heard with chemical emblems <note>term: alchemical illustrations</note>, which are unique to this science. 

For if all other arts represent emblems of morals or subjects other than the secrets of nature, they seem to deviate from their purpose and goal, as they are intended and required to be understood by all men. Chemistry <note>original: "Chymy," referring here to alchemy.</note> is not like this. Like a chaste virgin, she ought to be seen through a veil; and like Diana <note>The goddess of the hunt, often associated with the moon in alchemy.</note>, she should not be seen without a garment of various colors, for reasons expressed elsewhere. I say, then, that you have all together in this one book these four things: emblematic, poetic, and allegorical pure notions, engraved in "Venus" or copper <note>Venus is the alchemical name for copper; the author also puns on "Venus" meaning elegance.</note>—and not without elegance—alongside the most hidden chemical matters, and finally, rare musical pieces to be explained <note>original: "enucleated"</note> by the understanding. You have them all together in one book and may apply them as designs for your own use. If it be more intel-

<summary>The author explains the philosophical and psychological benefits of music and visual arts, citing classical authorities to justify combining music, emblems, and alchemy in this work. He argues that while most arts should be plain, alchemy ("Chymy") must remain veiled in allegory and "spiritual" sensory experiences.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s preface, transitioning from a discussion of the senses and the intellect into the specific allegorical meaning of the myth of Atalanta as it relates to alchemy and music.</meta>

# The preface to the Reader.

...intellectual, than sensual, the more profitable and delightful will it be in time. But if it is first in bondage to the senses, there is no doubt but there will be a passage from the sense to the understanding as if through a door; for nothing is said to be in the understanding which did not first have admission through some sense. The understanding of a newborn man is supposed to be like a smooth tablet <note>original: "smooth table"; referring to the concept of the *tabula rasa* or blank slate</note> whereon nothing is as yet written, but anything may be written with the help of the senses, acting as a pen. It is commonly said that there is no desire for a thing unknown; because the senses, acting as inquisitors and messengers, must first convey information. Like guards watching at the gates <gloss>the instruments of a city</gloss> <gloss>(the sense organs)</gloss>, they inform and impart everything that is to be known to the understanding, which acts as the dictator and judge.

As to the reason for engraving these emblems—lest even they may seem monstrous and inconsistent to you—I will add a few things. Atalanta is renowned by the poets for running, an exercise in which she surpasses all her suitors. Therefore, a reward for victory being proposed, death follows for those <gloss>that were</gloss> being outrun by the virgin, until she herself was overcome by Hippomenes. He was a <gloss>very</gloss> hardy and cautious youth who cast three golden apples <gloss>at three</gloss> several times in her path. By stopping to pick these up, she was in the meantime outrun by him, as he was now near and reaching the goal. 

As this Atalanta runs, so one musical note always runs before another (like Hippomenes) and the other follows; yet they are established and confirmed in a third note, which is single and of one value, like a golden apple. This same virgin is purely <term>alchemical</term> <note>original: "Chymicall"</note>, namely <term>philosophical mercury</term>. She is fleeing in flight and is restrained by the <term>golden Sulphur</term>. If a man knows how to make her fixed <note>original: "stans"; referring to the alchemical process of "fixing" a volatile substance so it can endure fire</note>, he will have that spouse which he desires; if not, he will face the destruction and ruin of his affairs. 

From Hippomenes and Atalanta lying together in the temple of the mother of the Gods—that is, a vessel—lions are produced, or they acquire a red color <note>The "Red Lion" is a famous alchemical symbol for the final stage of the Great Work</note>. This virgin also obtains a victory before men by killing a certain boar of notable size, for which she was rewarded by Meleager. Striking a rock at Rhetany, the temple of Aesculapius, she brought forth water, <gloss>of which</gloss> being thirsty, she drank. 

Because these things are truly allegorical and emblematic, and in no way historical, I was willing for this intellectual, emblematic treatise to point out <note>original: "indigitate"</note> and take notice of them in commemoration of this noble process. This is especially true of the apples cast before her, which were taken out of the <term>Hesperian garden</term> <note>The garden of the Hesperides, where golden apples grew</note> and given to Hippomenes by Venus, the Goddess of favor and sweetness. In those musical measures or "flights," you see this observed: every <term>distich</term> <note>a pair of poetic lines</note> is conveniently adapted to these three voices and may be of profit in singing. In this way, so many varieties of "flights" are accommodated to one single voice. Every wise and intelligent man will also think well of this kind of emblematic representation in them, as well as the adaptation of the verse to every voice. For if some merchants value and buy a work of painting for a great sum of money where the eyes alone are deceived—judging it to be very near to nature—why should the learned not hold these figures in high estimation?

<summary>Maier explains the philosophical necessity of using sensory emblems to reach the intellect, then decodes the myth of Atalanta as an allegory for the alchemical union of Mercury and Sulfur.</summary>

### The First Epigram

<margin>Because</margin>

<margin>The white stage</margin>

<meta>This page continues Discourse 1. A handwritten signature from a later owner, "A Howell 1821," appears at the top right of the original page.</meta>

# Discourse I.

Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the "Hermetic" alchemical tradition is attributed.</note>, the most diligent researcher of every natural secret, elegantly and concisely describes the work of nature in his Emerald Tablet <note>original: Smaragdine table</note>. Among other things, he says: "The wind carries him in his belly." This is as if he were to say that he—whose father is the Sun <note>original: Sol</note> and mother is the Moon <note>original: Luna</note>—must be carried by windy vapors <note>original: fumes</note> before he can be brought to light, just as a bird is carried by the air when it flies. 

Now, from vapors or winds (which are nothing else but moved air), a solidified <note>original: coagulate</note> water is produced. From this water, when mixed with earth, all minerals and metals proceed. Indeed, it is determined that these very things consist of and are immediately solidified from vapors. Therefore, whether it is attributed to water or to vapors, the result is the same, because both are the substance of wind. The same may be said of minerals and metals, though more distantly. 

Now the question is: who is it that ought to be carried by the wind? I answer that, chemically, it is Sulfur, which is <margin>Lully [mentions this] here.</margin> carried in quicksilver <note>original: Argent vive</note>, as Lully <note>Ramon Llull, a major figure in medieval alchemy and logic.</note> attests in Chapter 32 of his book, along with all the rest. Physically, it is an infant who is soon to be born into the world. I say also, arithmetically, that it is the root of a cube; musically <margin>"The wind carries him in his belly"—this explains what Sulfur is carried in; see Book 1 and Chapter 47.</margin>, it is the octave <note>original: Diapason</note>; geometrically, it is a point <note>original: punctum</note>—the beginning of a moving line; and astronomically, it is the center of the planets Saturn, <margin>The [philosopher's] stone is fire carried in the belly of the air.</margin> Jupiter, and Mars. 

Although these are different subjects, if they are compared carefully, they will easily demonstrate the "offspring of the wind." The understanding of this must be left to each person’s individual effort. But I will point out <note>original: indigitate</note> the matter more plainly: every "Mercury" is composed of vapors. That is, it consists of water lifting earth up with it into an airy state, and of earth forcing air to return into a watery earth or an earthy water. Because the elements are all within it—mixed, subdued, and produced together into a viscous nature—they do not easily separate from one another. Instead, either the volatile elements rise to the top, or they remain below with the fixed elements. 

The first case is evident in common quicksilver <note>Mercury</note>, while the other is seen in the "philosophical" and fixed metals. In these fixed metals, the stable elements predominate over the volatile ones; in common mercury, the volatile ones outweigh the fixed. It is not without reason that Mercury is called and reputed the messenger, interpreter, and intermediate servant of the other gods, depicted with wings on his head and feet. He is windy and flies through the air like the wind itself, a fact often confirmed to the misfortune of many men <note>Likely a reference to the toxic effects of breathing mercury vapors.</note>. Yet, by carrying a white wand wrapped with two crossed serpents <note>The caduceus.</note>—which can draw souls out of bodies and bring them back again, performing many such contradictory things—he perfectly represents "philosophical mercury." 

Therefore, Mercury is the wind. It takes Sulfur—or Dionysus, or Aesculapius <note>Dionysus and Aesculapius are used here as mythological allegories for the transformative stages of the alchemical substance.</note> if you prefer—while it is still an imperfect embryo <margin>Because it is volatile</margin> out of the mother’s body, or even out of the ashes of the mother’s burned body. It carries it to the place where it <margin>Digested and fixed</margin> may be matured. This embryo is Sulfur, which is infused into the body of Boreas <note>The North Wind.</note> by the celestial Sun, so that he may bring it <margin>The white and red light</margin> forth once it has matured. At the end of his term, Boreas produces twins: one with white hair named Calais, and the other with red hair named Zothes. These sons of Boreas were (as Orpheus, the chemical poet, writes)...

<summary>This page interprets the Emerald Tablet of Hermes, specifically the phrase "the wind carries him in his belly," as an alchemical allegory for the volatile and fixed nature of Mercury and Sulfur. The author uses mythological figures like Boreas and his sons to explain the maturation of the "philosophical stone" through various elemental states.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Emerald Tablet, Sulfur, Mercury, Argent vive, Ramon Llull, Boreas, Calais and Zothes, alchemy, four elements, philosophical mercury</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discourse on alchemical symbolism, specifically interpreting Greek mythological figures like Jason and the Argonauts as metaphors for chemical processes involving purification and the "generation" of the philosophical stone.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>Discourse 1.</header>

<margin>Basil Valentine, Book 1.</margin>
<margin>From the east <note>original Latin: ab orient</note></margin>
<margin>Noeman comes from the north</margin>
...wish Jason among the Argonauts to carry away the Golden Fleece from Colchis: and Phineus the blind prophet, infested by the Harpies, could not be freed except by the aforementioned sons of Boreas <note>The North Wind.</note>. For this benefit obtained from them, he, being grateful, declared the whole manner of the way to the Argonauts. But the Harpies are nothing other than corrupting <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemical theory, sulphur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of matter.</note>, which is driven away by the sons of Boreas once they have reached full age. Thus, a thing that was imperfect or molested by noxious birds is made perfect and no longer subject to that evil, which then shows Jason the "physician" <note>Jason's name was often etymologically linked by alchemists to the Greek word 'iasis' (healing), casting him as a healer or alchemist.</note> the way to obtain the Golden Fleece. 

These winds our countryman <term>Basil</term> <note>Likely referring to Basil Valentine, the legendary author of alchemical treatises.</note>, among others, also takes notice of, and plainly says: <gloss>A double</gloss> wind ought to <gloss>arise</gloss>, <gloss>then a single wind</gloss> called Vulturnus <note>The East or South-East wind.</note>, and <gloss>next</gloss>, one called Notus <note>The South wind.</note>, which <gloss>will</gloss> blow from the East and South impetuously. When their motion ceases—for just as water is to be made from air—you <gloss>will</gloss> confidently believe that a physical <note>original: "corporall"</note> thing will be made from a spiritual one. 

And <term>Ripley</term> <note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist and author of 'The Compound of Alchymy'.</note> in Gate 8 says that our "Infant" <note>A term for the philosophical stone in its developing stages.</note> ought to be born again in the air—that is, in the "belly of the wind." The <term>Scala Philosophorum</term> <note>Latin: "The Ladder of Philosophers," a popular alchemical text.</note> says the same in degree 6: "You must know that the son of the wise is born in the air." And in degree 8: "Airy spirits ascending together into the air love <gloss>one another</gloss>." As <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of the Hermetic tradition.</note> says, "the wind carries him in its belly" <note>A famous line from the Emerald Tablet, a foundational text of alchemy.</note> because the generation of our "son" is made in the air; and being born in the air, he is born wisely. For he ascends from earth to heaven, and again descends to earth, acquiring both the superior and inferior power <note>original: "virtue".</note>.

It cites alchemical authorities like Basil Valentine and George Ripley to explain the Hermetic concept of the "philosophical child" being nurtured by the wind and air.</summary>

### The earth is his nurse.
