<summary>This page presents the second emblem of an alchemical series, using the mythological examples of Romulus and Jove to explain how the "philosophical birth" is nurtured by the Earth. It emphasizes the greatness of the alchemical result by comparing its nourishment to that of legendary figures and deities.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Romulus, Jupiter, Philosopher's Stone, Mother Earth, nourishment, nature's secrets</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural philosophy and the alchemical process, drawing parallels between biological reproduction, nutrition, and the "philosophical work."</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Discourse 2.</header>

It is concluded by the Peripatetics <note>original: "peripateticks"; followers of Aristotle</note> and mainstream philosophers that the substance providing nourishment is converted into the substance of the thing being nourished, and is assimilated into it. This does not happen before, but after its transformation, and this is considered an infallible axiom. For why would the thing providing nourishment (which is already, beforehand, like and the same as the thing being nourished) need to have its form changed? If that were to happen, it would not remain the same or even similar. Furthermore, how could things that cannot be assimilated into the nourisher—such as wood, stones, and the like—be taken as nutriment? Therefore, just as the first scenario is pointless, the other is contrary to nature.

However, for a newborn infant to be nourished by the milk of animals is not contrary to nature, because assimilation can occur; yet, it happens far more easily with the mother's milk than with another’s. For this reason, physicians conclude that it contributes to the health, physical resemblance, and strength of an infant if it is always nourished by the milk of its own mother, rather than by another's. This is the harmony of all nature: that like delights in its like and follows its footsteps as far as it is able, in all things, with a certain quiet consent and cooperation.

The same thing usually happens in the natural work of the philosophers <note>meaning the alchemical process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>, which is governed by nature in its formation just as an infant is in its mother’s womb. And though a father and mother—and more than ~~these~~—are ascribed to it by way of metaphor, yet it is no more artificial than the generation of any animal. Two seeds are joined together by animals through a certain pleasant ingenuity—both of them human ~~seeds~~—which, being united, produce an embryo through successive changes. This embryo grows and increases, acquiring life and motion, and is then nourished by milk. 

But it is necessary for the woman, during the time of conception and pregnancy, to conduct herself temperately regarding heat, food and drink, motion and rest, and other circumstances; otherwise, a miscarriage will follow, and the destruction of the conceived embryo. This observation of the <term>six things not natural</term> <note>a traditional medical concept referring to external factors that affect health: air, food/drink, sleep/waking, motion/rest, excretion/retention, and mental passions</note>, because it is prescribed by physicians according to their art, is also considered "artificial" or skillful. 

In the same way, if the seeds are not already joined together in the philosophical work, they ought to be joined. But if they should be found already joined together somewhere—just as the nutrients for the rooster and hen are contained together in one egg—then the philosophical work would be even more natural than the generation of animals. But let us grant, as the philosophers assert, that one "seed" comes from the east and another from the west and they are made one; what more is administered to them than <insert>union</insert> ~~mixing~~ in their own vessel, temperate heat, and nourishment? The vessel is indeed artificial, but in this, there is no difference whether the nest is made by the <insert>hen</insert> ~~countrywoman~~ herself, or prepared by <insert>someone</insert> ~~a peasant~~ in a certain place; as is common, the generation of the eggs and the hatching of chickens from them will be the same. 

Next, it is a natural thing regardless of whether the process proceeds from the temperate fire of a furnace, the putrefaction of dung, the sun and air, the bowels of the mother, or elsewhere. Thus the Egyptian does, by art, administer...

<summary>The author argues that alchemical processes (the "philosophical work") follow the same natural laws as biological nutrition and reproduction, comparing the use of vessels and artificial heat to a mother's womb or an egg's shell.</summary>
<keywords>Peripatetics, nutrition, assimilation, breastfeeding, alchemy, philosophical work, six non-naturals, embryology, generation, artificial vs. natural heat</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural and artificial processes, likely part of a series of discourses on alchemical or natural philosophy. It transitions from the concept of incubation to the specific role of "Earth" in the alchemical process.</meta>

# Discourse 2.

[Natural heat] provides for the hatching of eggs by using furnaces. The seeds of silkworms, and even hen's eggs, are said to be hatched by the warmth of a virgin's breast. Therefore, Art and Nature mutually help one another, so that they may perform the duties of the other. Nevertheless, Nature is always the Mistress, and Art is the servant. 

But to say the earth is the nurse of the <term>philosophical babe</term> <note>The "philosophical babe" or child refers to the nascent "Philosopher's Stone" or the central matter of the alchemical work in its early, developing stages.</note> might raise an objection from anyone, as the earth is the driest of all elements and is devoid of juice; indeed, <term>siccity</term> <note>original: "siccity"; dryness.</note> is its proper quality. However, it may be answered that "elemented earth" is understood here, not the raw Element itself, which we noted sufficiently during the first day of the <term>philosophical work</term> <note>The "philosophical work" refers to the "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) of alchemy.</note> and which explains nature. 

This earth is the nurse of <term>Heaven</term> <note>original: "Cælum"; in alchemy, this often refers to the volatile or "spiritual" aspect of the matter that must be grounded.</note>, not by opening, washing, or watering the babe, but by coagulating, fixing, and coloring it, and converting it into mere juice and blood. Nutrition implies augmentation in length, breadth, and depth—that is, extending itself through all dimensions of the body. Since this is performed by earth alone for the <term>philosophical babe</term>, the name of "Nurse" is ascribed to the earth with good reason. 

Yet, something happens in this admirable terrestrial juice that is contrary to other kinds of milk (which are converted by the body they nourish, rather than converting it). By reason of its most efficacious virtue, this juice greatly alters the nature of the thing being nourished. This is much like how the milk of the wolf was believed to have disposed the body of Romulus <note>The legendary founder of Rome, who, according to myth, was suckled by a she-wolf.</note> toward a magnanimous nature and a tendency toward war.

<summary>This page discusses the symbiotic relationship between Art (human skill) and Nature, specifically exploring the alchemical metaphor of Earth as a "nurse" that stabilizes and nourishes the developing "philosophical babe."</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Art and Nature, philosophical babe, siccity, Cælum, coagulation, nutrition, Romulus, incubation</keywords>

### Emblem 3: Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Go to the woman washing clothes, and do in the same manner.<-

### Epigram 3.

> If you delight in diving into abstruse <term>abstruse: hidden, complex, or difficult to understand</term> things;
> Be vigilant and observe what may be conducive <note>original: "conduce"; meaning to lead to a specific result</note> to your goal.
> Do you not see how women wash and boil
> Their clothes in hot water if they are soiled?
> Follow their steps and you cannot go astray,
> For water washes any filth away.

--- Page 123 ---
### Discourse 3.

If <insert>any</insert> impurities affect linen clothes, causing them to be spotted and blackened as if with earthy waste <note>original: "faces," meaning dregs or solid impurities</note>, they are washed away by the moist element, namely water. The clothes are then exposed to the air so that the moisture, together with the dregs, may be drawn out by the heat of the sun—acting like fire, the fourth element. If this is done often, the clothes, which before were foul and unclean, become pure and <insert>free</insert> from spots. This is the art of women, which they learned from Nature herself. For we see that if the bones of animals, which at first are black and filthy, are exposed to the air, often washed with rain, and dried again by the frequent heat of the sun, they will, as Isaac <note>likely Isaac Hollandus, a 15th-century alchemist</note> notes, be reduced to perfect whiteness. We observe the same in the philosophical subject: whatever rawness <note>original: "crudityes"</note> and dregs appear, they are purged and taken away by water poured on them, and the body is brought to great clarity and perfection.

All chemical operations—such as calcination, sublimation, solution, distillation, descension, coagulation, separation, and the rest—are perfected by washing <note>original: "ablution"</note> alone. For he who washes an impure thing with water performs the same task as is accomplished by so many forms of operating. As the *Philosopher's Rosary* <note>original: "Rosarium Philosophorum," a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> says, "the linen of initial origin <note>original: "prime descent"</note> being defiled by sweat, must be washed by fire and burned by water." By this, fire and water seem to have given one another mutual qualities, or it suggests that philosophical fire is not of the same kind as common fire, and the same must be supposed of the water.

As for quicklime <note>original: "Calx vive"</note> and Greek fire <note>original: "ignis Graecus," a combustible weapon used in medieval warfare</note>, we know that they are ignited by water and in no way extinguished, contrary to the nature of other things that catch fire. For they affirm that camphor, once ignited, burns in water; and the stone called jet <note>original: "Gagates"</note> (as Anselm de Boodt <note>Anselmus de Boodt (1550–1632), a Flemish mineralogist and physician</note> attests), once ignited, is more easily extinguished by oil than by water. This is because oil does not mix with it and suffocates the fire's body, whereas water, being unable to mix itself with fatness, gives way to the burning body unless it altogether covers and overwhelms it. But that cannot easily be done because it is a stone and swims upon the top of the water like all oil. Naphtha, petroleum, and the like, once ignited, do not much fear water.

Some write of the subterranean coals in the territory <note>original: "dominion"</note> of Liège that, being ignited in the earth, they cannot be extinguished by water, but only by throwing and heaping the dust of the earth upon them. Tacitus <note>a Roman historian</note> declares the same of a similar kind of fire, which cannot be quenched by water but only by clubs and garments taken off from the body. There is, therefore, a great diversity of fires, and <gloss>1</gloss> no <gloss>2 both 4</gloss> <gloss>3</gloss> <gloss>4</gloss> less of liquids in igniting <gloss>5</gloss> and in <gloss>6</gloss> <gloss>7</gloss> extinguishing <gloss>13</gloss>. Milk, vinegar, spirit of wine, *Aqua fortis* <note>original: "strong water," refers to nitric acid</note>, *Aqua regia* <note>original: "royal water," a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving gold</note>, and common water differ much from one another in comparison with fire. Not only this, but the matter itself sometimes sustains fire, such as those fine Byssin clothes <note>likely referring to asbestos cloth, which was historically confused with Byssus (sea silk) and was famously cleaned by being placed in fire</note> formerly used and esteemed by rich men, which were washed, or restored to their former cleanness, by fire and not by water, the dregs being burned away. Whatever some men vainly say of the hairs of the reptile salamander, as if they were incombustible—

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical principle of purification through the metaphorical and literal use of water and fire, comparing chemical "washing" to the cleaning of linen and the whitening of bones. The author lists various substances that defy common nature, such as fires that burn in water or materials like asbestos that are cleaned by flame.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, purification, ablution, quicklime, Greek fire, Anselm de Boodt, chemical operations, Byssinian cloth, salamander, Rosarium Philosophorum</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical substances and their purification, specifically focusing on the non-combustible nature of the "philosophical subject."</meta>

# Discourse 3.

Lamp wicks <insert>or lamp-wicks</insert> <note>original: Elychnia</note> made from it are not to be considered. There are some who truly state that in the past, a garment was prepared of <term>talc</term> <note>original: Talke; often used historically to refer to heat-resistant minerals like mica or asbestos</note>, <term>plumose alum</term> <note>original: plumous Alum; a fibrous mineral, likely asbestos</note>, and other such materials. This garment was frequently cleaned by fire, but that the art was lost and died with the artist (a woman from Antwerp) because of envy, and the same formula could not be found afterward. 

We do not speak here of combustible materials. The <term>philosophical subject</term> <note>the primary material worked upon by alchemists to create the Philosopher's Stone</note> must be considered according to all these differences whenever it is prepared; for here, the fire, water, and the matter itself are not ordinary. For the philosophers' fire is water, and their water is fire. Their water both washes and <term>calcines</term> <note>to reduce a substance to a powder or ash through heat</note> at the same time, and so does their fire. 

The clothes which are to be washed have the nature of the <term>byssine garment</term> <note>original: byssinean vesture; traditionally made of "byssus" or fine linen, here used metaphorically for a purified alchemical substance</note>, or prepared talc, the composition and art of preparation of which is also not obvious to everyone. To wash them, they make a <term>lixivium</term> <note>original: Lixivium; an alkaline solution or lye used for cleaning</note>, or lye, not with the ashes or salt of oak, but a metallic one, which is more durable than the other. They do not use common water, but water that has been frozen into ice and snow under the zodiac sign of <term>Aquarius</term> <note>original: Aquary</note>. This is because it is composed of finer parts than the stagnant waters of ditches or marshes, and therefore it can better penetrate into the internal parts of the black and unclean philosophical body in order to wash and cleanse it.

<summary>This page describes the specialized, metaphorical nature of alchemical fire and water, comparing the purification of the "philosophical subject" to the cleaning of fire-resistant minerals using a metallic lye and pure snow-water gathered under Aquarius.</summary>

### Epigram 4.

To multiply the world with the human race;
The brother <gloss>did</gloss> must<note>original: 'must'; the word is struck through in the manuscript with a horizontal line.</note> his sister closely embrace:
Therefore, let the one wed the other in friendship,
That they may act as husband and wife in bed:
To invite them first, prepare a cup of the best <note>The "cup" often symbolizes a solvent or the "philosopher's wine" used to facilitate the union of substances.</note>,
And then they both will freely do the rest.

<meta>This page continues the "Discourse" for Emblem 4 of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens," which explores the alchemical allegory of the marriage between a brother and sister. This concept is justified through both natural philosophy and historical-theological precedents, such as the children of Adam.</meta>

# Discourse 4

Both divine and political laws forbid people to marry who are joined by nature in too near a degree <gloss>directly</gloss> of consanguinity <term>consanguinity: being descended from the same ancestor; blood relation</term>—specifically those in a direct genealogical line of ascent or descent—and this is for very good reasons. However, when the philosophers <note>original: "philosophers"—here referring specifically to alchemists</note> speak of the mother being joined with the son, or the father with the daughter, or the brother with the sister, they say or do nothing contrary to these laws. This is because their subjects distinguish between attributes, and their causes distinguish between effects. The persons described by the philosophers are <gloss>said to be</gloss> free from those distinctions <gloss>among</gloss><note>The word 'among' is struck through with a horizontal line in the original manuscript.</note> the sons and daughters of Adam, who married one another without any accusation of crime. 

The principal reason for these human laws seems to be that mankind might be more firmly united and linked in affinity and friendship, rather than being divided into hostile and hereditary family factions. This same reasoning, remaining firm and entire for the first sons and daughters of Adam, did not at all bar them from the <gloss>rites</gloss> <note>The word 'right' is struck through with a horizontal line and replaced with 'rites'.</note> of matrimony. Since mankind then existed in them alone, and no others were living besides them and their parents, they were necessarily joined in marriage as they were allies in blood. 

But as the population of men increased and was distributed into innumerable families, it was found to be a true and just cause that brothers should not marry sisters. The philosophers, however, have another reason why sisters may marry brothers: namely, the "similarity of substance," so that like may be joined to like. In this alchemical work, there are only two things like one another in species but different in sex, one of which is called "brother" and the other "sister." They may therefore—by the same liberty, condition, and inevitable necessity as the first humans—be lawfully committed to one marriage. 

The brother is hot and dry, and therefore very choleric <term>choleric: associated with yellow bile and a fiery, irritable temperament</term>; the sister is cold and moist, having much phlegmatic <term>phlegmatic: associated with phlegm and a cool, calm, or sluggish temperament</term> matter in her. These natures, though differing in temperament, best agree when joined in love and fruitfulness for the propagation of offspring. 

Just as fire, which can be spread, is not easily struck out of two pieces of hard steel, nor out of two pieces of brittle flint, but rather out of the "hard and the brittle"—that is, the steel and the flint—so a lively offspring is not produced from a hot man and a fiery woman, nor from two cold people (for the coldness of a man causes barrenness). Instead, it comes from a hot man and a colder woman. Within the range of human temperament, even the hottest woman is colder than the coldest man of his sex, provided he is healthy, as Levinus Lemnius proves in his book *On the Secret Miracles of Nature* <note>original: "Lev. Lemnius in lib. de occ. nat. mirat." referring to 'De Occultis Naturae Miraculis'</note>. 

The sister and brother are therefore rightly joined together by the philosophers. If a man desires offspring from a hen, bitch, female goat, ewe, or other animals, he joins a <gloss>rooster</gloss> <note>The word 'dunghill' is struck through with a horizontal line and replaced with 'cocke'.</note>, dog, male goat, or ram—every animal to its own species, to which it is most like—and so he is not disappointed in his desire. In doing so, he does not worry about the "blood relation" of the animals, but only the quality <note>original: "generosity"—referring to the excellence of the breed or lineage</note> of each one and the compatibility of their natures. The same may be said of the body of a tree and the branch to be grafted into it, and even of the metals to—

<summary>This page explains the alchemical concept of 'philosophical incest' (the marriage of brother and sister) by comparing it to the children of Adam and the breeding of animals, arguing that 'like must join with like' to produce the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>consanguinity, philosophers, Adam, affinity, matrimony, species, choleric, phlegmatic, Levinus Lemnius, alchemy, tempering of natures, animal breeding</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse for Emblem 4 of Michael Maier's "Atalanta Fugiens." It elaborates on the necessity of a "philothesium" (cup of love) to facilitate the union between the alchemical brother and sister, using medical and biblical analogies (specifically Lot) to explain how natural barriers to such a union are overcome to ensure fruitfulness.</meta>

->### Discourse 4.<-

Nature, which nevertheless has the greatest similarity and homogeneity <note>original: "homogeneity of substance"; meaning having the same nature or being composed of like parts</note> of substance in all things, requires this when it desires a thing to be joined with it. But when a brother and sister are joined together, they do not become fruitful nor remain long in love, unless a *philothesium* <term>philothesium: a Greek-derived term meaning a "loving-cup" or a cup given as a mark of friendship</term>, or cup of love, is administered to them like a love potion. For their minds are so calmed and united by this that (setting aside modesty) they go together as if drunk—like Lot <note>A biblical reference to Genesis 19:30-38, where Lot’s daughters, seeking to preserve their lineage, had children by their father after he was intoxicated.</note>—and produce offspring that are legitimate rather than spurious. 

For who does not know that humanity is greatly indebted to medicine? Many thousands of people are now in the world who would not have been here if their fathers or ancestors had not been freed and delivered from the defect of sterility by its means and benefits, or if their mothers had been <gloss>preserved</gloss> from miscarriage <note>original: "abortion"; in the 17th century, this term was commonly used for any premature birth or miscarriage, not just intentional ones.</note> by removing the cause or the immediate and distant obstacles. This cup of love is therefore given to this newly married couple for the same reasons, which any person can clearly understand are threefold: the constancy of love, the removal <note>original: "remotion"</note> of sterility, and the prevention of miscarriage.

<summary>Maier explains that the alchemical union of "brother and sister" requires a special medicinal potion (the cup of love) to overcome natural modesty and ensure fertility. He likens this to how medicine assists human procreation by curing sterility and preventing miscarriages.</summary>
<keywords>philothesium, cup of love, alchemy, Lot, sterility, medicine, procreation, homogeneity, Atalanta Fugiens</keywords>

### Emblem 5. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->Place a toad <gloss>to</gloss> ~~in~~ the woman's breasts, so that she may give it suck,<-
->and the ~~woman~~ <gloss>herself</gloss> dies, and the toad <gloss>grows fat</gloss> ~~is fed~~ with milk.<-

### Epigram 5.

> Lay the vile <note>original: "vild"</note> toad at the woman’s milky breast,
> So that he may feast himself like an infant,
> And thereby grow in size and strength,
> Until he has killed his dry-sucked nurse at last.
> From this, prepare an antidote to cure the
> Poisoned <note>original: "intoxicated," meaning filled with toxin or venom</note> heart, and purge it with art.

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Discourse 5 from Michael Maier’s *Atalanta Fugiens*. The discourse provides an alchemical commentary on Emblem 5, which depicts a woman nursing a toad. It explores the symbolic relationship between the "husband" and "wife" (sulfur and mercury) and the nourishment required for the alchemical "offspring."</meta>

# Discourse 5

The entire assembly of philosophers agree in this: that their work is nothing else but man and wife. The man's part is indeed to generate and govern; the wife's part is to conceive, be impregnated, bring forth, nurse, and educate the offspring, and be subject to the man's commands. For just as she sustains and nourishes the conceived embryo with blood before it is brought to light, so once it is brought forth, she nourishes it with milk. To this end, nature has prepared a digestible and balanced food in the mother's breast for the tender infant, which awaits his coming as the first provision and viaticum <note>original: "viaticum"; a Latin term for a provision or travel-allowance for a journey</note> in <gloss>the</gloss> race of the world. By milk, therefore, he is nourished, grows, and is increased, until he is furnished with the means to chew bread—that is, when <gloss>he</gloss> is rightfully weaned because nature has provided him other, more solid nutriment. 

But here the philosophers say that a toad must be put to the woman's breasts, so that she may nourish it like an infant with her milk. This is a miserable and horrible spectacle, and a wicked thing: that milk designed for an infant should be given to a toad, which is a poisonous beast and contrary to the nature of man. We have heard and read of serpents and dragons sucking the udders of cows; toads might perhaps desire the same thing if the opportunity were offered with beasts. 

There is a well-known story of a toad that seated himself upon the mouth and the inside of the lips of a certain countryman while he was asleep, so that he could not be driven off by any method except by violence. Because violence would have brought the danger of death from the spitting of poison (which the toad is said to use as a defensive or offensive weapon), it was not to be attempted. However, a remedy was found for the miserable man through antipathy <note>original: "antipathy"; a natural or instinctive biological opposition between two species</note>—namely, that of a very large spider and the toad, who hate one another mortally. The man was therefore carried, with the toad still attached, directly to the place where a <gloss>grown</gloss> spider had made its web. Upon seeing the toad, the spider immediately came down onto the toad's back and pricked him with its sting. When the toad received no harm from this, the spider descended a second time and struck him again more strongly. At this, the toad began immediately to swell and fell down dead from the man's mouth without any hurt to him. 

But here the opposite happens: because the toad seizes not the mouth but the breast of the woman, by whose milk he increases so as to reach great size and strength, while the woman wastes away and dies as her spirits are taken from her. Poison is easily communicated through the pectoral veins to the heart, and it corrupts and destroys it. This is evident in the case of Cleopatra, who applied vipers to her breasts, choosing to prevent her capture through voluntary death rather than be led alive in the triumph <note>A "triumph" was a formal procession in ancient Rome celebrating a victorious general and parading their captives.</note> of the conqueror. 

<margin>Theophilus in the *Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers) is one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts.</note> mentions a dragon joined to a woman.</margin>

But lest any man should think the philosophers so cruel as to command a poisonous reptile to be put to a woman, he must know that this toad is the offspring or son of the same woman, produced by a monstrous birth. Therefore, by natural right, it ought to be fed and nourished by the mother's milk. Furthermore, it is not the son's will that the mother should die; for he could not infect his mother, as he was formed <note>original: "coagulated"</note> within her womb <note>original: "bowells"</note> and increased by her blood until his birth. It is indeed an ominous thing for a toad to be born of a woman, which to our knowledge has happened elsewhere.

<summary>This page explains the alchemical allegory of a woman nursing a toad, framing it as a natural, though monstrous, relationship where the mother (matter/mercury) must nourish the offspring (the stone/sulfur) despite its perceived toxicity.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens, symbology, toad, Cleopatra, Turba Philosophorum, antipathy, spider and toad, mercury and sulfur</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of alchemical symbols and natural wonders, specifically focusing on the legend of the toadstone and the credibility of medieval historians.</meta>

### Discourse 5.

William of Newburgh <note>original: Guilielmus Neobrigensis; a 12th-century English historian</note>, an English writer, says in his commentaries (how truly, let others judge) that in a certain stone quarry in the Diocese of the Bishop of Winchester, a large stone was split open. Inside, a living toad with a golden chain was found. By the Bishop's command, it was hidden in the same place and buried in perpetual darkness, for fear it might be an ill omen. This "philosophical" toad is similar: for it is dressed in gold, though not on the outside with the man-made work of a chain, but on the inside with a natural one—specifically, a stone. Some call this stone <term>Borax</term>, <term>Chelonitis</term>, <term>Batrachites</term>, <term>Crapaudina</term>, or <term>Garatronium</term> <note>various names for the "toadstone," a mythical gem thought to be found in the heads of old toads</note>. This stone far exceeds gold in its power against every kind of animal poison, and it is commonly set in gold, as a case or cover, so that it may not be damaged or lost.

But it ought to be genuine, <gloss>being</gloss> obtained from <strike>the</strike> <insert>an</insert> animal. However, if it is picked out of underground trenches, as it commonly is, the stone taken up may be shaped neatly to resemble the original and used in its place with the best minerals, relieving and comforting the heart. For in this <insert>the philosophical</insert> toad, gold is <gloss>really</gloss> found—not in a stone quarry (as that storyteller claims)—and he has gold in him, not outwardly for show. For to what end should a toad adorn himself while lurking in darkness and secret places? Perhaps so that he might be grandly greeted by a beetle if they met in the twilight! What underground goldsmith would make him a golden chain? Perhaps it was the father of the green children <note>original: "green boyes"; a reference to the medieval legend of the Green Children of Woolpit</note>, who came out of the land of Saint Martin—indeed, out of the earth itself—as well as the two dogs from the stone quarry mentioned by the same author.

<summary>The author critiques a legend by William of Newburgh about a toad found with a golden chain, instead arguing for the alchemical and medicinal reality of the "toadstone" found within the "philosophical toad."</summary>
<keywords>William of Newburgh, toadstone, Crapaudina, alchemy, Green Children of Woolpit, poison remedies, Diocese of Winchester</keywords>

### Emblem 6. Of the secrets of nature.
->Sow your gold in the white foliated earth. <note>original: "foliated earth" (Latin: *terra foliata*) refers to a specifically prepared, purified substance in alchemy, often described as having a flaky or layered appearance like the leaves of a book.</note><-

### Epigram 6.
->Peasants <note>original: "Rusticks"</note> their seed to the fertile earth commit,<-
->When with their harrows they have made it fit:<-
->The Sages <note>original: "Sophi"; referring to the "Philosophers" or masters of alchemy</note> thus their golden seed do sow<-
->In foliated earth as white as snow:<-
->Observe your mother well, and you'll behold,<-
->As in a glass, by wheat, your budding gold.<-

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of agriculture to explain alchemical processes, comparing the planting of wheat by farmers to the planting of "golden seed" in purified "foliated earth" by alchemists.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, gold, foliated earth, agriculture metaphor, Sophi, secrets of nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Discourse 6," focusing on the philosophical parallels between different arts of nature.</meta>

### Discourse 6.

<margin>Alchemy <note>original: "Chymistry"</note> is exactly parallel to agriculture and acts as its deputy. In all things, it carries out its office, but under a more hidden <note>original: "abstruse"</note> allegory.</margin>

Plato says a city does not subsist through physician and physician, but through physician and farmer <note>original: "husbandman"</note>—that is, men of different functions. However, he mentions the farmer and physician first because their works are very visible in the imitation, improvement, and perfection of nature. Both take a natural subject and, according to their art, add certain necessary things that are missing or remove those things which are superfluous. Thus, the art of both may be defined (as Hippocrates defined medicine) by the addition of what is lacking and the subtraction of what is extra. 

For what does the farmer do more than add plowing, ridging, furrowing, harrowing, manuring, and sowing to the land provided by nature? He then commits the increase and success to nature, which provides the heat of the sun and rain, multiplying the seeds and promoting them to standing corn soon to be reaped. In the meantime, while the herb is springing up, the farmer weeds out thistles and all obstacles. Once the corn is ripe, he gathers it and removes the superfluous parts—namely the straw, chaff, and other such things. 

In the same way, a physician (as well as a <term>Chymist</term> <note>In this context, an alchemist.</note> in a different respect) labors to preserve the body's present health. If health is missing, he works to recover it through various remedies. He removes the disease-causing <note>original: "morbificke"</note> cause, cures the illness, and eases symptoms. He reduces superfluous blood by opening a vein; if blood is lacking, he restores it by prescribing a good diet. He clears out harmful <note>original: "noxious"</note> humors through purgation. In this way, he imitates, supplies, and improves nature in a thousand ways through the works of understanding and art.

Since these things are well known, we should rather consider alchemical matters. Chemistry first explains the operations of agriculture through its secret boundaries and methods. Farmers have land in which to sow seed, and so do the alchemists. Farmers have dung with which to manure their ground, and so do alchemists; without this, nothing could be done, nor could any success be expected. They have seed, the multiplication of which they desire. If alchemists did not have the same, they would (as Cicero <note>original: "Tully"</note> says) be like a painter who tries to capture the face of a man he has never seen, nor even seen an image of. 

Farmers expect rain and the heat of the sun, and alchemists likewise truly administer convenient heat and "rain" <note>likely referring to distillation or liquid solvents</note> to their works. I need say no more. Because of this connection, the ancients produced Ceres, Triptolemus, Osiris, and Dionysus—the "golden gods"—as figures relating to alchemy. These myths teach men how to cast the seeds of fruits into the earth, showing tillage along with the propagation of vines and the use of wine. The ignorant have interpreted these stories only as rural operations, but they are mistaken. Those things which are hidden from the common people and declared to the learned under these veils of agriculture are the most hidden mysteries of nature. 

To this end, the philosophers say that their gold must be sowed upon <term>white foliated earth</term> <note>A specific stage in the alchemical process where the material becomes white and flaky like leaves.</note>. They mean that the sowing of corn should be observed and imitated as an example. The author of the *Treatise of Corn* and Jodocus Greverus have performed this most excellently in their descriptions; they have both most elegantly matched every operation of agriculture in producing corn to the "semination" <note>planting or seeding</note> of gold, or the generation of <unclear>it</unclear>.

<summary>This page argues that alchemy and agriculture are sister arts that both seek to perfect nature by adding what is missing and removing what is redundant. It suggests that ancient agricultural myths are actually secret allegories for the alchemical process of "sowing" gold.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, agriculture, Plato, Hippocrates, Cicero, Ceres, Osiris, Dionysus, Jodocus Greverus, white foliated earth, semination of gold</keywords>

# Discourse 6.

<margin>For this reason, Ovid says that Chiron <note>original: "Philyrides," a name for the centaur Chiron, son of Philyra</note> made the youth Achilles an excellent harper . . .</margin>

the tincture: White earth, because it is sandy, yields little fruit to the country people, by whom that which is black and rich <note>original: "fatt"</note> is more desired; but the white principally contributes to philosophy <note>This refers to the "Great Work" of alchemy, specifically the stage of the "White Stone" or *albedo*.</note>, if it be foliated—that is, well prepared—because they know how to enrich it with their manure <note>original: "dung"</note>, whereas the other they do not. Now sowing is the propagation of the world, whereby that which cannot long endure in an individual is caused to persist in the species. This occurs in men, animals, and plants in their hermaphroditism <term>hermaphroditicey: the state of having both male and female characteristics</term>, and in others under two distinct sexes. 

But in metals, it is far otherwise: for in them a line is made from the flow <term>flux: in this context, the continuous movement or extension from one geometric state to another</term> of a point, a surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> from the flow of a line, and a body from the flow of a surface. But the starry <note>Refers to the influence of the celestial bodies or the "Starry Heaven."</note> produces that point before the line, surface, and body, because it is the principle of them all. Nature adds the flow a long time after; that is, the celestial sun generated a son in the earth, whom Mercury commits to Vulcan and Chiron to be educated. This means a manual craftsman <note>original: "manuale artifice"</note> acts as the instructor, just as they write of Achilles, who was made whole and hardened in fire by his mother Thetis. He, among other things, learned music and the art of playing the harp from Chiron. 

But Achilles is none other than the "philosophical subject" <note>The primary material or "Prime Matter" used in the alchemical process.</note> whose son was Pyrrhus with red hair; without these two, Troy could not be taken and subdued, as we have abundantly demonstrated in our *Hieroglyphic*, Book 6. Therefore, we do not without reason touch (though briefly) upon music in this work of ours, where we describe Achilles and his heroic virtues and enterprises. For if music adorns so great a hero, why may it not also make this work of ours more complete and acceptable? For the angels sing (as the sacred Scriptures attest), the heavens sing (as Pythagoras affirmed, and as the Psalmist says, "declare the glory of God"), the Muses and Apollo sing (as the poets say), men and even infants sing, birds sing, and even sheep and geese "sing" through musical instruments <note>A clever reference to the materials used for instruments: gut strings from sheep and quill plectrums or pens from geese.</note>; if, therefore, we also sing, there is a reason for it . . .

<summary>The author explains the alchemical significance of "White Earth" and geometric generation, identifying the hero Achilles as a symbol for the "philosophical subject" in alchemy. He concludes by defending the inclusion of music in his work, citing its universal presence in nature, divinity, and myth.</summary>

->To the abandoned nest. You will hit the nail on the head,<-

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical discussion of the composition of matter, moving from the basic principles of elements to their practical and symbolic manifestations in the "philosophical subject" (the alchemical work).</meta>

### Discourse 7.

What Hippocrates, the defender and champion <note>original: "propugnator"</note> of physicians, affirms of humors—that they are different and many in man, and not one only, otherwise various diseases could not arise—was perceived to be true of the elements of the world. For if there were but one only element, there could be no mutation of it into another, no generation nor corruption; indeed, all things would be one immutable thing, and no metals, minerals, plants, and animals could be naturally produced from it. For this reason, the supreme Creator composed the whole system of the world of diverse and contrary natures, namely of light and heavy, hot and cold, moist and dry, so that one might by affinity pass into the other, and so a composition be made of bodies which might be very different from one another in essence, qualities, virtues, and effects. 

For in things perfectly mixed are the light elements, such as fire and air, and also the heavy, such as earth and water, which are so equally tempered together that one does not recede from the other, but suffers itself to be easily taken and held by the other, as neighbor by neighbor. Earth and air are contrary to one another, as are fire and water; yet, notwithstanding, fire maintains friendship with air by the heat common to both, and with earth by dryness <note>original: "siccity"</note>. Likewise, air maintains friendship with water, and water with earth. By these means, they are all joined by bonds of affinity, or rather consanguinity, and remain together in one composition. If it abounds with the light element, it elevates the heavy with it; if with the heavy, it weighs down the light with it. 

This is declared by the two Eagles, one with wings and the other without, of which that which endeavors to fly is restrained by the <gloss>other</gloss>. An example of this is evident in the fight of the Falcon and Heron; for the Falcon, soaring higher in the air by his speedy flying, takes and bears the Heron with his talons, by whose weight both fall to the ground. The contrary appears in the artificial dove or automaton <note>original: "Automa"</note> of Archytas <note>Archytas of Tarentum (4th century BCE) was a Greek philosopher and mathematician credited with building the first autonomous flying machine, a steam-powered wooden pigeon.</note>, where heavy things were lifted up by light—that is, its wooden body was carried into the air by the spirit <note>the air or steam</note> included within. 

In the philosophical subject, the light things do first predominate over the heavy in terms of quantity, but they are overcome by the virtue of the heavy things. In the process of time, the Eagle's wings are cut off, and one very great bird (namely an Ostrich) is made of two. This bird can consume iron and, being impeded by weight, runs more upon the earth than flies in the air, though it has goodly wings. Concerning this or the like, Hermes (as the Author of *Aurora*, chapter 5, affirms) writes thus:

> I have considered a bird worthy to be held in estimation by the wise, which flies when it is in Aries, Cancer, Libra, or Capricorn <note>These represent the four cardinal signs of the zodiac, often corresponding to the four seasons or elements in alchemy.</note>, and you will never fail to find it in pure mines and rocky mountains.

Concerning the same, Senior <note>Refers to Senior Zadith (Ibn Umail), a 10th-century Arabian alchemist.</note> in *Tabula* <note>The *Tabula Chimica*.</note>, where two are seen—one volatile, the other without wings—whereof one holds the other fast by its beak, so that they cannot easily be separated. For this is the device and machination of universal nature: always to raise heavy things by light, and likewise to depress light things by heavy, as the author of the *Perfect Magistery* declares, who constitutes seven minerals as spirit, as it were created to stay, and...

<summary>The author explains that the world is composed of contrary elements (hot/cold, light/heavy) that must be balanced and bound together to create life and matter. He uses alchemical symbols like the winged and wingless eagles and the ostrich to describe the process of fixing volatile substances into a stable form.</summary>
<keywords>elements, humors, Hippocrates, alchemy, four elements, Archytas's dove, symbolism of birds, Hermes Trismegistus, Senior Zadith, volatile and fixed</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical principles regarding the combination of volatile and fixed substances. The catchword "a" at the end of the previous page's transcription refers to "and."</meta>

### Discourse 7.

So many metallic bodies, categorized as fixed or "starry," and enjoying those qualities, are to be wedded to these [other bodies]. From this, Aristotle the chemist <note>original: "Aristotle the chymist"; likely referring to the pseudo-Aristotelian alchemical tradition rather than the Greek philosopher himself</note> says that the spirit, once it has dissolved the body and soul so they exist in their elemental forms, does not remain a fixed thing unless you perform an operation upon it.

Now, this "operation" involves joining the spirit with the body from which you originally prepared it. This prevents the spirit from escaping <note>original: "flight"</note> because it becomes mixed with the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the body. In camphor <note>original: "Camphire"</note>, as a learned man declares, the light elements—namely air and fire—overpower the heavy elements. Therefore, it is said to exhale and evaporate completely into the air. 

In quicksilver <note>original: "Argent vive"</note>, flowers of sulfur <note>a powdered form of sulfur produced by sublimation</note>, antimony, the salt of hart's <note>deer's</note> blood, sal ammoniac <note>original: "Sal Armoniacke"; ammonium chloride</note>, and other such substances, the "earth" component flies up with the air into the alembic <note>a distilling apparatus</note> and is not separated from it. 

In gold, glass, diamonds, the stone emery <note>original: "Smyris"</note>, garnets <note>original: "Granats"</note>, and similar materials, the elements remain joined for a long time without damage, despite the fire. The "earth" in these substances retains those things which are left within itself. 

In other combustible materials, a division and separation of one thing from another occurs, so that the ashes are left at the bottom, while the water, air, and fire fly upwards. We must not, therefore, focus on the unequal composition of these last items (which are not strongly mixed), nor on the mixture <note>original: "commixtion"</note> of the first group (which, though more durable, are still volatile). Instead, we should look to the solidity, constancy, and fixity of the "middle" substances. In this way, the "bird without wings" <note>an alchemical metaphor for a fixed or non-volatile substance</note> will restrain the bird that has wings, and the fixed substance will stabilize the volatile one, as must necessarily be done.

<summary>This discourse explains the alchemical principles of "fixing" volatile spirits by recombining them with solid bodies, using examples of various minerals and elements to illustrate degrees of stability.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, volatility, fixity, Aristotle the chemist, camphor, quicksilver, alembic, elemental theory, metallic bodies</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Discourse 8, expanding on the metaphorical and physical nature of the "Philosophical Egg" by comparing it to the natural generation of birds and crocodiles.</meta>

# Discourse 8.

There are many and various sorts of birds which, just as they are infinite in number, are also unknown to us by name. They tell us of a very great bird appearing at a certain time of the year in a small island of the sea called Rut <note>original: "Rut"; likely referring to the Roc, a legendary giant bird of prey from Middle Eastern mythology capable of carrying off elephants.</note>, because it can lift an elephant with it into the air. India and America yield parrots of various colors, crows, and the like; but it is not the philosophers' intention to inquire after the eggs of these birds. The Egyptians annually attack and, as if in a public war, denounce and destroy the eggs of crocodiles with weapons of iron. The philosophers, however, "burn" their eggs with fire—not to kill or destroy them, but so that they may receive life and growth. 

For when a chicken is produced from that egg, animated and living, it cannot be said to be the destruction of the egg, but rather its generation. It ceases, indeed, to be an egg by the loss of the <term>oval</term> form, and begins to be a two-footed, flying animal by the introduction of a more noble form. In an egg, the seeds of both male and female are joined together under one cover or shell. The yolk constitutes the chicken and its fundamental parts and bowels—the seed of the male being the primary and internal active force. The white yields the matter, providing the "thread" and growth for the initial structure, acting as the "warp" <note>The set of longitudinal threads in a loom; here used to mean the structural foundation of the embryo.</note> of the chicken. 

External heat is the first mover. Through a certain circulation of the elements, combined with the instinct and guidance of nature, this heat introduces a new form by changing one thing into another. Water turns into air, air into fire, and fire into earth. Upon the conjunction of these elements, a specific form is transmitted from the starry realm, and an individual is made of a certain kind of bird—namely, the kind whose egg it was and whose seed was infused in it.

It is indeed said to be struck with a fiery sword, while <term>Vulcan</term> <note>The Roman god of fire and smithing, often representing the alchemist's furnace.</note> performing the duty of a midwife, gives passage or exit to the chicken with his axe, just as he did for <term>Pallas</term> <note>Pallas Athene (Minerva), who was said to have been born fully grown from the forehead of her father Jupiter.</note> out of the brain of <term>Jupiter</term>. This is what <term>Basil Valentine</term> <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist; his name is likely a pseudonym for Johann Thölde.</note> affirms: that <term>Mercury</term> was imprisoned by <term>Vulcan</term> at the command of <term>Mars</term>, and was not released until he was wholly putrefied and dead. But this death is for him the beginning of a new life. Just as the corruption or death of an egg brings new generation and life to the chicken, so when an embryo is freed from that "human vegetative life" (which it alone enjoys in its mother's womb), a more perfect life is obtained by coming into the light of this world, as if by a second birth. 

You also, who are deprived of this present life (which was bad), have another most perfect and eternal life at hand. Thus <term>Lully</term> <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and logician whose name was attached to many later alchemical texts.</note> in many places calls this fiery sword a "sharp spear." He does this because fire, like a sharp spear or sword, pierces bodies and makes them porous and permeable, so that water can penetrate to dissolve them and reduce them from hardness to softness and flexibility. 

In the stomach of a cormorant <note>A large, dark-colored water bird known for its greediness.</note>, which is the most voracious of all birds, long and round worms are found alive. These act as the instruments of heat. They immediately attack and—as we have sometimes observed—pierce like very sharp needles, thereby consuming eels and other fish taken by the bird in a very short time through a wonderful operation of nature. Therefore, just as heat pricks, so a pricking thing sometimes supplies the...

<summary>This page uses the biological development of a bird from an egg as an allegory for alchemical transformation, arguing that the "death" of the egg's form is necessary for the "generation" of new life.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, embryology, generation, Basil Valentine, Ramon Llull, Vulcan, Mercury, Pallas Athene, cormorant digestion, Philosophical Egg</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical heat and the metaphorical "philosopher's egg." There is a note regarding large ink smudges on the lower half of the original page.</meta>

### Discourse 8.

...place of heat. In consideration of this, the instrument with which the philosopher's egg <term>Philosopher's egg: The hermetically sealed vessel, or the alchemical matter itself, which resembles an egg in its potential for life and transformation</term> ought to be pierced and struck may not undeservedly be called a "fiery sword." However, the philosopher would rather have it understood as a temperate heat by which the egg is kept warm. As <term>Morsolous</term> <note>A speaker in the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note> declares in the *Turba*, saying: "Wise men ought to burn away moisture first with a slow fire, as is shown to us by the example of the generation of a chicken." And when the fire is increased, the vessel must be closed on all sides, so that the body of the brass <note>original: "brasse"; in alchemy, this often refers to the base metallic matter or "aes" undergoing change</note> and its fugitive spirit <term>Fugitive spirit: A volatile substance that easily evaporates or escapes when heated</term> may not exhale.

But from what bird did the egg come? <term>Muséty</term> <note>Another figure from the *Turba Philosophorum*</note> says in the same passage: "Now I say," says he, "that no instruments are made, except of our white, starry, splendid powder, and of the white Stone <term>White Stone: A stage of perfection in the alchemical process, representing purity and the ability to transmute metals into silver</term>. From this powder, fit instruments for the egg are made." Yet they have not named the egg, nor specified what bird the egg was.

<summary>This page explores the metaphorical "philosopher's egg" and the specific application of heat required to transform alchemical matter, drawing parallels to the incubation of a chicken.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, philosopher's egg, fiery sword, Turba Philosophorum, Morsolous, Muséty, fugitive spirit, white stone, temperate heat</keywords>

### Epigram 9.

The wise men <term>Sophi</term> <note>original: "Sophi"; refers to the "Sages" or masters of alchemical knowledge</note> have a tree with golden fruit,
And an old man <term>Senior</term> <note>original: "Senior"; in alchemical texts, this often represents the "Old Man" or the base metal (like lead) that must undergo transformation</note> of no small reputation;
For these, you must provide a house of glass
Filled with dew, to spend some time within:
So that he, comforting his nature with that food,
May shake off old age and become as young men are.

<gloss>Sophi: Sages or wise philosophers. Senior: An ancient man, often symbolizing the prime matter. Golden fruit: The goal of the alchemical process. House of glass: An alchemical vessel or retort. Dew: A symbolic solvent or the "spirit" used in the work. Solacing nature: Comforting or strengthening the physical constitution. Alchemy: The medieval forerunner of chemistry. Emblem: A symbolic picture with a moral or philosophical lesson. Epigram: A short, pithy poem explaining the emblem.</gloss>

<summary>This page presents an alchemical allegory of rejuvenation, where an "old man" (symbolizing base matter) is restored to youth by consuming the fruit of a golden tree within a glass vessel filled with dew.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Discourse 9, focusing on the biological and philosophical nature of aging and the potential for rejuvenation.</meta>

# Discourse 9.

All things whatsoever grow in length, breadth, and depth—that is, they are propagated, nourished, and augmented to maturity—these same things also decrease. That is, they are diminished in their faculties, die, and are completely demolished, as is clearly evident in all vegetables and animals. Therefore, man also, having arrived at full growth, undergoes decline <note>original: "admits of decrement"</note>, which is old age, whereby his strength is gradually diminished even until the approach of death. 

Now, the cause of old age is the same as that of a lamp decaying and shining obscurely when the oil is almost consumed. For just as there are three things in a lamp—the wick <note>original: "match"</note>, the fatness, and the flame—so in man, the vital parts, bowels, and ligaments are the wick; the fatness is the radical moisture <note>The "radical moisture" was a concept in medieval and early modern medicine referring to the fundamental life-fluid or "oil" that sustains the body's natural heat; its exhaustion led to old age and death.</note>; and the flame is the natural heat. The only difference is that the flame of a lamp shines, while the natural heat does not shine at all, as it is not fire but only heat; additionally, the lamp's fatness is oily, while the radical moisture is viscous, being derived from a seminal principle. Just as a lamp is extinguished through a lack of oil, so man, through old age and without any other disease, falls into a marasmus <note>original: "marasme," a medical term for a wasting away or withered state of the body.</note>, a consumption of age, and finally death. 

They tell of the Eagle that in old age, being hindered by a crooked beak, it would starve to death if it were not restored by nature to a state of youth, as it were, by shedding its beak. So stags seem to grow young again by shedding their horns, serpents their skins, and crabs their shells—not indeed in reality, because the consumed radical moisture is not restored to them, but only in appearance. 

That which can truly make a man grow young again is nothing but death itself and the beginning of the eternal life that follows. Regarding the external form and the restoration of strength to some degree, along with the removal of wrinkles and gray hairs, some say a remedy may be found. Lully <note>Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1316), a famous polymath and alchemist.</note> affirms this of the "Quintessence," and Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a physician and alchemist who wrote about "prepared gold" as a medicine.</note> of "prepared gold." 

But here the philosophers say that for an old man to become young, he must be shut up with a certain tree in a "house of dew," and then he will eat of the fruit of the tree and so recover his youth. It is scarcely believed by the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note> that such trees now exist in the nature of things. Physicians write wonders of Myrobalans <note>The fruit of various tropical trees, used in traditional medicine as a purgative and restorative.</note>, the fruit of a tree, as if they could perform such a thing: take away gray hairs, purify the blood, and prolong life. 

But these things are doubted by many men, unless they are said to achieve it by accident—just as other things purge the mass of blood from the dregs <note>original: "faeces"</note> mixed with it, or dye gray hairs black (as Myrobalans are reported to color the hair and the pupils of the eyes). Marsilio Ficino <note>original: "Marsilius Ficinus" (1433–1499), an influential Italian scholar and physician.</note>, in his book on preserving the life of students, writes that in order to attain long life, it is beneficial for a man daily to suck the milk of a certain beautiful and young woman. In place of this, others recommend the eating of viper's flesh; but indeed, these means are themselves...

<summary>The author explains the process of aging using the analogy of a lamp running out of oil (radical moisture) and explores various alchemical and medicinal theories for rejuvenation, ranging from "prepared gold" to sucking the milk of young women.</summary>
<keywords>aging, radical moisture, natural heat, rejuvenation, alchemy, Raymond Lully, Arnold of Villanova, Marsilio Ficino, Myrobalans, longevity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on rejuvenation and the alchemical transformation of the body, specifically referencing methods for restoring youth to the elderly.</meta>

### Discourse 9.

Arnoldus <note>original: Arnoldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), an influential alchemist and physician.</note> is more rigid than old age itself, and his methods are scarcely to be used by one in a thousand, even though they would not be without a certain effect. It is written by Paracelsus <note>original: Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist.</note> in his book *Of Long Life*, that a sick man may attract health to himself by imagination alone, just as an old man might attract youth. However, this author seems in this instance to use only his imagination, not experience. It is a certain thing regarding the Psylli <note>A tribe mentioned by ancient writers like Pliny, rumored to have unique powers over snakes and the "evil eye."</note>—who have two pupils in each eye—and of witches, that they bewitch children and cattle by sight alone; from which comes that line of Virgil: *I do not know what eye bewitches my tender lambs* <note>original: "Nescio quis teneros oculus mihi fascinat agnos," from Virgil's Eclogues.</note>. 

But these things are done without physical contact, whereas the tree restores the old man to youth through contact: for this tree has sweet fruit, ripe and red, which easily turns into pure blood. It is easy to digest and provides excellent nourishment, so as to leave nothing superfluous or feculent <note>original: "feculent," meaning foul or full of dregs/waste.</note> in the body. The old man abounds with white phlegm and is of a white color and hairy; these humors <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to govern health and temperament.</note>, colors, and hair are changed by eating this fruit and become red, as is the color of young men. For this reason, the philosophers say the Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> is first "old" (that is, white) and then "young" (that is, red), because red is the color of youth just as white is the color of old age. 

It is added that the old man ought to be shut up with the tree—not in the open air, but in a house; not in a dry place, but one moist with dew. It is accounted a miracle for trees to grow or flourish in a closed place, but if it is moist, there is no doubt of their long duration. For the nourishment of a tree is moisture and "airy earth"—that is, rich soil—which can ascend into the trunk and boughs to produce leaves, flowers, and fruit. In these natural works, all the elements concur: Fire gives the first motion as the efficient cause; Air gives thinness and the ability to penetrate; Water provides fluidity; and Earth provides coagulation <note>The process of becoming solid or semi-solid.</note>. For Air returns into Water, and Water into Earth, if any of their excess ascends. 

By Fire, I mean the natural heat which, being spread through the food, acts by the power of the stars like a blacksmith to fabricate and form fruit similar <gloss>only</gloss> to those things from which the food arises. But a dewy evaporation is not only good and useful for moistening the tree so that it may more easily yield fruit, but also for the old man, so that by these fruits he may become young again. That is to say, the dewy evaporation softens, fills up, and restores his wrinkles and dry skin with temperate heat and moisture. For physicians advisedly and with great usefulness enjoin and prescribe warm baths for marasmus <note>original: "marasme," a medical term for the wasting away of the body due to old age or malnutrition.</note> and the consumption of old age. But if the matter is well considered, that tree is the "daughter" of the old man, who (like Daphne <note>In Greek mythology, the nymph Daphne was transformed into a laurel tree to escape Apollo.</note>) is transmuted into a plant of the same sort; therefore, the old man does not without reason expect youth from it, which causes the effect to be realized.

<summary>This page discusses alchemical and philosophical methods for rejuvenation, using the allegory of a red-fruited tree to explain how the "white" qualities of old age are transformed into the "red" vitality of youth.</summary>
<keywords>Arnoldus de Villa Nova, Paracelsus, rejuvenation, humors, elements, alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Daphne, transmutation, marasmus</keywords>

## Emblem 10. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

->Give fire to fire, Mercury to Mercury, and you have enough.<-

## Epigram 10.

> The world's entire structure depends upon this chain:
> Like to its like, as a shepherdess to a swain <note>original: "swaine"; a country youth or lover in a pastoral setting</note>.
> These are the limits of art; proceed no further:
> Join Hermes <note>original: "Hermes"; the Greek name for Mercury, here representing the alchemical principle of volatility or the "Mercury of the Philosophers"</note> to Hermes, and fire to fire.
> Vulcan <note>original: "Vulcan"; the Roman god of fire, representing the heat of the furnace</note> makes Hermes fly, but Cynthia <note>original: "Cynthia"; the moon goddess, representing the principle of cooling, fixation, or silver</note> must
> Make him stay, then she yields to her brother's <note>The moon’s brother is the Sun (Sol or Apollo), representing gold or the final perfected state</note> desire.

<note>This emblem describes the alchemical concept of "like curing like" or "like attracting like." It uses the mythological marriage of the Sun and Moon to symbolize the chemical union of opposites, achieved through the application of heat (Vulcan) and the stabilization of volatile elements.</note>

<summary>This page presents an alchemical emblem centered on the principle of harmony and the union of similar substances, using mythological allegories to explain chemical processes. <keywords>Alchemy, Hermes, Vulcan, Cynthia, Mercury, chemical union, secrets of nature, pastoral imagery</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
### Discourse 10.

If this saying is taken literally, it only increases the quantity of fire and Mercury <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the fluidic, volatile, and transformative principle of matter.</note>, and introduces no new quality into the subject: for every like being added to its like makes a similar augmentation. Therefore, physicians affirm that contrary diseases are cured and taken away by contrary medicines. For we see that fire is extinguished by water, but sustained by adding fire. The poet is of the same opinion, saying: Venus rages in wine, and fire in fire. 

However, it may be answered that fire and fire, or Mercury and Mercury, are very different from one another; for the philosophers recognize many kinds of fire, and many kinds of Mercury. Moreover, the same heat and cold, being distant only in place or situation, differ from others of their own kind so as to attract that which is like themselves. Heat fixed in any part is attracted and drawn forth by the same heat; similarly, joints being numbed <note>original: "stupefied"</note> with the cold of winter and almost dead are restored if they are put into cold water, rather than having external heat immediately applied. 

Just as a greater light obscures the lesser, so also a greater heat obscures the less, and a greater cold the less. Nevertheless, it is necessary for the external heat and cold to be less than that which was before fixed or imprinted on the part or joint; otherwise, the same impression would be made as before, and the condition would be increased rather than drawn out by its like. The drawing out of cold by cold water, and of fiery heat by heat, is agreeable to nature, because every sudden change into a contrary state is dangerous and less acceptable to it; but that which happens little by little, and as it were by degrees, is tolerable. 

So we say there is one internal fire that is already impressed and essential in the philosophical subject <note>The "philosophical subject" refers to the primary material being worked upon in the alchemical process.</note>, and another external fire, which may also be understood regarding Mercury. One is an internal fire "equivocally" <note>Used here to mean something that has the qualities of fire but is not literally a flame.</note> because of its fiery qualities, virtues, and operations; the other is external "univocally" <note>Used here to mean something that is literally and plainly fire.</note>. External fire must therefore be given to the internal, and the like Mercury to Mercury, so that the goal of the art may be completed. 

To soften or mature <note>original: "mature or mollify"</note> any hard or raw thing by coction <term>The process of "cooking" or heating a substance to bring it to a state of ripeness or purity.</term>, we use fire and water. Water dissolves hardness and penetrates into the compact parts, while heat adds strength and motion to it. This appears, for example, in the cooking of peas, which are themselves solid and hard, but swell, break, and are reduced to a pulp by water; the heat of the fire thins the water by ebullition <term>The state of boiling or bubbling up.</term> and makes it into a thinner, almost airy substance. Similarly, the heat of fire resolves the raw parts of fruit or flesh into water and causes them to evaporate with it into the air. 

In the same manner, fire and Mercury here are fire and water, and are the same; fire and Mercury are the mature and raw parts, of which the raw are to be matured by cooking, or the mature parts are to be purged from superfluities by the ministry of fire and water. Now, to briefly demonstrate that these two fires and two Mercuries are principally and solely necessary to the art: Empedocles <note>A Greek pre-Socratic philosopher (c. 490–430 BC) who proposed that all matter is composed of four elements: earth, air, fire, and water.</note> determines two principles of all things: love and variance (or friendship and discord)—

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical principle of "like attracting like," arguing that internal qualities of a substance are best drawn out or matured by applying similar external forces gradually. It uses the analogy of cooking and medical treatments for frostbite to explain the interaction between internal and external fire and Mercury.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Mercury, internal fire, coction, ebullition, Empedocles, maturation, philosophical subject, medicinal theory</keywords>

# Discourse 10.

...tors. [The author notes] that through variance <note>conflict or opposition</note>, corruptions were made, while generation occurs through love. Such variance is clearly observed between fire and water: fire causes water to evaporate if it can; water, on the contrary, extinguishes fire if it reaches it. Despite this, it is obvious that generations may be created from these same elements through a certain friendship. For through heat, a new creation of air is made from water, and by that same heat, the hardening <note>original: "induration"</note> of water into a stone occurs. In this way, from these two elements—acting as the primary ones—the other two are also made, leading consequently to the production of all things. 

Water was the substance <note>original: "matter"</note> of heaven and of all physical <note>original: "corporale"</note> things. Fire, acting as the "form," moves and gives shape <note>original: "informeth"</note> to this substance. Thus, this water (or Mercury) provides the matter, and fire <margin>NB</margin> (or Sulphur) provides the form. In order for these two to begin their work and interact through solution, coagulation, alteration, dyeing <note>original: "tinction"</note>, and perfection, they require external aids or instruments. Without these, no result could follow. For just as a blacksmith achieves nothing without a hammer and fire, neither can the philosopher work without his instruments, which are water and fire. 

This water is called by some the "water of clouds," just as this fire sparks further fire. It is undoubtedly called the water of clouds because it is distilled, like the dew of May, from the finest parts. For just as the dew of that month, when sealed in an eggshell, is said to raise the egg (or its container) high by the heat of the sun, so too does this water of the clouds—or dew—cause the <term>philosopher's egg</term> <note>A sealed vessel used in alchemical experiments, symbolizing the container of transformation</note> to ascend; that is, to <term>sublime</term>, <term>exalt</term>, and perfect it. 

This same water is also a very sharp vinegar, which has turned a physical body into a pure spirit. For just as vinegar has different qualities—namely, to penetrate to the bottom and to constrict <note>original: "astringe"</note>—so this water dissolves and solidifies <note>original: "coagulates"</note>. Yet, it is not solidified itself, because it does not belong to a physical subject. Now, this water is obtained from the fountain of <term>Parnassus</term> <note>A mountain in Greece sacred to the Muses; here used as an alchemical metaphor</note>, which, contrary to the nature of other springs, is at the top <margin>+ the...</margin> of a mountain, created by the hoof of the flying horse <term>Pegasus</term>. 

There must also be actual fire, which nevertheless must be governed and adjusted by its intensity <note>original: "degrees"</note>, as if through stages of growth <note>original: "birthes"</note>. For just as the sun, moving from <term>Aries</term> into <term>Leo</term>, gradually increases the heat for growing things by always drawing closer, so must it be done here. For the philosopher’s "infant" must be nourished by fire as if by milk; the more it grows, the more productive it becomes.

<summary>This page explains alchemical generation as the result of the interaction between Mercury (water/matter) and Sulphur (fire/form), using metaphors like May dew and the fountain of Parnassus. It emphasizes that the alchemist must use controlled heat, modeled on the sun's progression through the zodiac, to mature the "philosopher's egg."</summary>
