<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the challenges of alchemical study, specifically addressing the balance between theoretical study and practical experimentation.</meta>

# Discourse 11.

So great is the diversity among authors in their writing that seekers of truth almost despair of finding the end of art. Allegorical speeches are inherently difficult to understand and are the cause of many errors—especially when the same words are applied to different things, and different words to the same things. If a man wishes to extricate himself from this confusion, he must either possess a divine genius to perceive the truth involved in such great darkness, or exert endless effort and expense in proving what is true and what is not. 

But the philosophers say that one is not sufficient without the other: an acute genius is nothing without hand-labor, and labor is nothing without genius—just as theory is nothing without practice, and vice versa. No man is of such discrete understanding as to avoid a hundred thousand detours <note>original: "circumvagations"</note>, errors, and falsehoods regarding the word, or digressions and ambiguities, and still persist in the true path of nature. Therefore, the philosophers say that he who has not yet erred has not yet begun, and that errors are the informers of those things which ought to be done or not. 

So they also emphasize that a man may spend his whole life—even if he should live a thousand years—in distilling and redistilling before he can attain truth by experimentation alone. That little benefit can be had without the study and reading of authors, the "corrector of fools" signifies, saying: "Study removes ignorance and restores a man's genius to true understanding and the knowledge of everything." It is therefore principally necessary to acquire the knowledge of this delectable work by study and to sharpen the wit <term>wit: intellect or mental sharpness</term> by philosophical sayings, for the knowledge of truth is contained within them. 

If, therefore, operators do not despise study, they will sweetly relish the fruit proceeding from it. But for those who abhor study and yet are willing to operate, let them see whether their art is truly an imitation of nature herself (which the artist desires to amend); for it is impossible for such a person to prepare the philosophers' secrets to a perfect end. Of those men, the *Sophi* <term>Sophi: wise men or masters of alchemy</term> say that they run to practice as a donkey runs to hay, not knowing what to put its nose to, guided only as far as the outward senses lead it to see by sight and taste. These are his words. 

But lest a man should exhaust <note>original: "macerate"</note> himself with overmuch study—which is an inexhaustible sea of immense depth and effort—and endeavor (though in vain) to put every letter into action (meanwhile consuming or diminishing his strength, time, reputation, and wealth), the philosophers use emblematic speech. They say that "**Latona** <note>Latona: In alchemy, Latona often represents the "black" stage of matter or the impure substance that must be purified or "whitened" to reach the next stage of the Great Work</note> **must be whitened**," and that those books should be torn lest the readers' hearts be broken. 

For there are many books written so obscurely as to be understood by their own authors only; not a few were left out of envy to mislead <note>original: "seduce"</note> others, or rather to delay them so that they might not attain their end without difficulty, or to obscure those things the authors had written before. But what is the meaning of "whitening Latona"? This is the work; this is the labor to investigate. *Clangor Buccina* <note>original: *Clangor bucina*, referring to the "Clangor Buccinae" or "The Sound of the Trumpet," a famous alchemical treatise</note> affirms Latona to be an...
<margin>!!</margin>

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of balancing deep philosophical study with practical laboratory work, warning that either alone leads to failure or exhaustion. It introduces the cryptic alchemical goal of "whitening Latona" while cautioning against intentionally misleading texts.</summary>

Discourse 11.

imperfect body, a composer of Sol <note>original: "Sol," referring to the Sun or Gold</note> and Luna <note>original: "Luna," referring to the Moon or Silver</note>. The most ancient poets and writers affirm Latona to be the mother of the Sun and Moon, or Apollo and Diana; others call her the nurse. They say that Diana was born first (for the Moon and whiteness appear first) and then performed the office of a midwife on the same day during the birth of her brother Apollo. Latona was indeed one

<margin>Latona was one</margin> of the twelve divine hieroglyphs <note>original: "hieroglyphicks"</note> of the Egyptians, by whom these and other allegories were spread among other nations. Only a very few Egyptian priests understood their true meaning and sense; all the rest referred them to other subjects which did not exist in nature, namely various gods, goddesses, and the like. Because of this, Latona had the most sumptuous temple there after Vulcan's, towered and adorned with gold, as she was the mother of the philosophical Apollo and Diana. But this Latona is brown and blackish, and has many moles and blemishes on her face, which must be removed by art—specifically by dealbation <term>the process of whitening or bleaching</term>. Some perform whitening with ceruse <term>white lead pigment</term>, mercury sublimate, and talc reduced into oil, and the like, with which they coat, cover, and so whiten the outside of the skin. But these whitening crusts fall off with any wine or liquid because they do not penetrate to the inner parts.

But the philosophers do not care for such colorings, which are used more to deceive the eyes than to dye <note>original: "tinge"</note> the internal substance of the body. For they want the face of Latona to be whitened thoroughly and the slime <note>the base matter or "prima materia"</note> itself altered—that is, truly and not superficially. A question might be asked: how can it be done? I answer: Latona must first be sought out and known; though she may be taken from a vile place, she must nonetheless be sublimed <term>purified or elevated through vaporization</term> to a more worthy state. But if she is taken from a more worthy place, she must be thrown into a viler place—namely, into dung <note>A reference to "putrefaction," a stage of chemical decay in the alchemical process often achieved using the heat of decomposing manure.</note>. For there she will become truly white and be made into white lead; once that is achieved, there is no need to doubt success, or the attainment of red lead, which is the beginning and the end of the work <note>The "Great Work" or "Magnum Opus" of alchemy.</note>.

<summary>This page explains the alchemical allegory of Latona as the mother of Gold (Apollo) and Silver (Diana), emphasizing that true chemical "whitening" must be an internal transformation rather than a superficial coating.</summary>
<keywords>Latona, Apollo, Diana, Alchemy, Egypt, Hieroglyphs, Dealbation, White Lead, Red Lead, Putrefaction</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Discourse 12, focusing on the alchemical interpretation of the myth of Saturn and the first stage of the "Great Work."</meta>

# Discourse 12.

The allegory of Saturn was interpreted in diverse ways. Astronomers consider him the highest of the planets in order; the young students of chemistry <note>original: "Chymistry"; here referring to alchemy</note> consider him the lowliest of metals, namely lead. The heathen poets conceive him to be the father of Jupiter and the son of Caelus <note>original: "Coelum"; the personification of the Sky</note>; the mythologists consider him to be Time <term>Chronos</term>.

But though all these may seem to have thought rightly in their own sense, they still cannot explain those other things spoken elsewhere of Saturn: why he devours and vomits up his son and a stone instead of Jupiter; why he is the discoverer of truth; and why he appears with a scythe <note>original: "sieth"</note>, a serpent, blackness, gloominess <note>original: "morosity"</note>, and a distorted foot. Regarding these details, the mythologists think they interpret best by saying that Time reveals and manifests truth, glides away like a serpent, and cuts down all things with death as if by a scythe. They say Time devours its children—that is, all those things which it has generated—and digests hard stones, or, if it cannot consume them, vomits them up again. These interpretations do indeed agree in part, but they are not consistent with the full truth of the matter and all its circumstances.

But the chemical philosophers say that Saturn is first in their works; if he is truly present, they cannot err, for truth is discovered in darkness. In this stage, there is nothing besides blackness. Therefore, it is said in the *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba philosophorum," a famous early alchemical text</note>, "Whatever color comes after blackness is praiseworthy; and when you see your matter turn black, rejoice, because it is the beginning of the work." And, "When it is first black, we say it is the key of the work," because the process is not successful without blackness, as the *Rosary* <note>the "Rosarium Philosophorum"</note> says, along with the works of Arnold <note>likely Arnold of Villanova</note> and the *Mirror* <note>original: "Speculum"</note>.

When you are operating, endeavor to obtain a black color first, and then you will be certain that you are achieving putrefaction <term>the breaking down of matter in the alchemical process</term> and proceeding the right way. And a little later, it is said that this blackness is called "earth," which is created by light decoction <term>boiling or heating</term> repeated so often until the blackness becomes most prominent. For this reason, the same philosophers say that Saturn possesses the earth, Mercury the water, Jupiter the air, and Sol <note>the Sun/Gold</note> the fire.

Saturn, therefore, is blackness, the discoverer of truth, who follows a stone instead of Jupiter. For blackness—that is, a dark cloud—covers the stone at first so that it cannot be seen. Therefore Morienus says, "Every body without a soul is dark and obscure." And Hermes says: "Take his body and dissolve it with the sharpest vinegar (or the urine of children) <note>alchemical code for specific acidic or ammonia-rich reagents</note> until it is made dark." By being kept too long in putrefaction, the dark clouds that were upon him and in his body before he died are returned.

The stone is again disgorged <note>vomited up</note> by Saturn when it becomes white; it is then set upon the top of Mount Helicon as a monument to men, as Hesiod writes. This is because whiteness is actually contained within the blackness, drawn out of its bowels—that is, out of the stomach of Saturn. Therefore Democritus says: "Purify him with a peculiar washing <note>original: "ablution"</note>, draw its blackness and obscurity from it, and its whiteness will appear." And in the *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>the "Turba"</note> it is said: "Join the dry with the moist—that is, the black earth with its water—and boil it until it is whitened." 

Arnold, in the fourth chapter of *New Light* <note>original: "Novum lumen"</note>, declares this excellently, saying: "That moisture which causes blackness during boiling shows itself to be dried out when the white color begins to appear." And a little later: "And my Master said to me, that purity ascends because the whiteness..."

<summary>This page explains the alchemical allegory of Saturn, identifying him with lead and the "nigredo" (blackness) stage of the Great Work, which must precede the "albedo" (whiteness).</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Alchemy, Nigredo, Putrefaction, Turba Philosophorum, Arnold of Villanova, Hermes, Lead, Blackness, Whiteness</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the alchemical stages of transformation, specifically focusing on the transition from the black stage (nigredo) to whiteness and eventually redness.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>Discourse 12.</header>

...way drawn out of the belly of its blackness, as it says in the *Turba* <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note>: For when you see it black, know that its whiteness is hidden in the depths of the blackness that first appears. Just as this blackness is called Saturn, it is also called Lead.

On this point, Agadimon <note>A legendary alchemical figure, likely a corruption of Agathodaimon</note> says in the *Turba*: "Let your brass be worked until the blackness comes forth, which they call money; mix the matter of your art, and you will find blackness presently." This is the "philosophers' lead" <note>The "philosophers' lead" refers to the raw, primary matter of the alchemical work, rather than the common metal lead.</note>, which they have discussed extensively in their books.

To this relates the saying of Enigany <note>Likely a corruption of a name such as Anaxagoras or another philosopher cited in the *Turba*.</note>, that the splendor of Saturn ascending into the air appears as nothing other than darkness. And according to Plato in the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rosary of the Philosophers), a 16th-century collection of alchemical citations.</note>: the first way of governing Saturn is to putrefy it and place it upon Sol <note>original: Sol (the Sun, representing gold or the masculine principle)</note>. By this, it is evident that the meaning used by philosophers when speaking of Saturn is far different from the common understanding.

From Saturn is generated Jupiter (that is, a cloudy whiteness); from Jupiter comes Latona and Diana (that is, perfect whiteness); and then Apollo (that is, redness). This is the successive change <note>original: mutation</note> of all perfect colors. The stone cast out by Saturn <note>A reference to the Greek myth where Saturn (Cronus) swallowed a stone instead of his son Jupiter (Zeus). The stone was later placed at Delphi as a sacred monument.</note> is reported to be placed at the top of the mountains as a monument to men, which is a most refined thing.

<summary>This page explains the alchemical symbolism of Saturn (lead/blackness) and the progression of colors from black to white and finally red. It cites authorities like the Turba Philosophorum and the Rosarium to show that these terms represent chemical transformations rather than literal metals.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Saturn, nigredo, Turba Philosophorum, Rosarium Philosophorum, Jupiter, Diana, Apollo, philosophers' lead, color stages, transformation</keywords>

## Emblem 13. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->The philosopher's brass <term>brass</term> <note>In alchemy, "brass" (or *aes*) refers to a specific state of the metallic matter, often associated with the early stages of the Great Work when the substance is considered "diseased" or impure.</note> is hydropic <term>hydropic</term> <note>original: "hydropicall"; suffering from dropsy (edema), an excess of watery fluid. In alchemy, this represents an excess of unwanted moisture or "phlegm" in the metal.</note>, and desires to be washed seven times in a river, just as Naaman the leper was in the Jordan.<-

---

## Epigram 13.

->Hydropic humors <term>humors</term> <note>In traditional medicine, "humors" were the fluids that governed health; here, they represent the watery impurities infecting the alchemical metal.</note> infesting the Sophic <note>original: "Sophian"; meaning "of the philosophers" or "of the wise."</note> brass,<-
->Have no cure but one, alas:<-
->Naaman the leper restored himself,<-
->Bathing his limbs in the Jordan over and over;<-
->So if your brass is washed in pure water,<-
->Its current dropsy <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "hydropsy"; the accumulation of fluid, symbolizing the "leprosy" or corruption of the base metal that must be cleansed.</note> will not endure.<-

<summary>Emblem 13 uses the Biblical account of Naaman the leper's healing in the Jordan River as an allegory for the alchemical purification of "brass." It explains that the metal must be cleansed of its watery impurities (metaphorically called dropsy) through repeated washing.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, purification, Naaman, Jordan River, dropsy, Sophic brass, humors, Great Work, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical and philosophical discourse, specifically focusing on the purification and healing of substances as analogous to biblical and medical cures.</meta>

### Discourse 13

That Naaman the Syrian traveled to Judea at the advice of the prophet <note>The prophet Elisha; see 2 Kings 5.</note> and washed himself seven times in the river Jordan is attributed to his confidence in the prophet’s words. However, that he was cured of leprosy by that washing is a miracle of divine omnipotence. For leprosy—which, being seated in the blood and fundamental parts of a person's body, is like a <term>universal cancer</term>—cannot be cured or removed by any external washing, much less by cold water such as that of the Jordan. 

In the same manner, it is next to a miracle that the <term>philosophers' brass</term> <note>original: "philosophers brasse"; a term for the base matter or an early stage of the alchemical work.</note>, diseased with <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "Hydropsy"; an abnormal accumulation of fluid, now known as edema.</note>, should be freed from it by the washing of water, and that—being imperfect—it should be brought to perfection, and being sick, to health, so as to be able to communicate that same health to other sick bodies. For such an example does not exist elsewhere in nature, nor is it the ordinary way of nature to produce the philosophers' most perfect tincture unless it is governed by art and suitable materials are provided to it by an external cause. 

In the same way, the repairing of a dislocation is not the work of nature alone, but of art. Nevertheless, the <term>Os Sacrum</term> <note>Latin: "sacred bone." The large triangular bone at the base of the spine; the author suggests it moves or opens to facilitate childbirth.</note> opens itself miraculously during the birth of a child so that the infant may have an exit, as if through a door; in this, the most great and merciful God operates through nature above nature. So, that the stone should be perfected may seem to be a supernatural thing, though it is actually natural. Therefore, a philosopher says in the <term>Rosary</term> <note>original: "Rosary"; likely the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note>:

> You must know that our stone is airy and volatile, externally cold and moist, but internally hot and dry. That redness and moisture which is external is a watery vapor—corrupting, blasting, and destroying itself and all things—and it flees from fire. That heat and <term>dryness</term> <note>original: "siccity".</note> which is internal is hot and dry gold; it is a most pure oil, penetrating bodies, and is not fleeting, because the heat and dryness of the <term>phlegmy</term> <note>original: "phlegmy"; likely referring to the moist, "phlegmatic" substance that must be transformed to provide the "tinge" or color.</note> substance colors it, and nothing else. Therefore, cause the external redness and watery moisture to become like the internal heat and dryness, so that they may agree and be united together, and all at once be made into one penetrating, coloring, and fixing body. But these moistures must be destroyed by fire, using a degree of fire with a soft temperament and a suitable and moderate digestion.

<margin>Note Well</margin> Regarding these words: if this is true, how can the substance be cleared of water by using water? It may be answered that certain waters are hot and dry in quality—such as many mineral baths—in which the substance must be "philosophically washed." For this is what the philosophers say: "Wash with fire, and burn with water." For that fire which washes and that water which burns differ from one another in name only, but agree in their effect and operation. 

That "brass" must therefore be washed—that is, dried of its excessive moisture—by this "water" or this "fire." We have known through experience of bodies suffering from dropsy being cured by six months of abstinence from any drink, by being buried in hot sand or horse dung, by being put in a hot furnace to sweat, and by innumerable other helps. You may also use drying baths, such as those at <term>Karlovy Vary</term> <note>original: "Carolina"; a famous spa town in the modern-day Czech Republic.</note> and <term>Wiesbaden</term> <note>original: "Wisbadenses"; a spa town in Germany.</note> not far from Mainz. By the same ways must your patient be cured: sometimes by waters, or...

<meta>catchword: or</meta>

<summary>This page compares the biblical healing of Naaman's leprosy to the alchemical purification of "philosophers' brass," arguing that both require a "miraculous" transition from sickness to health through specific washings. The text explains the paradoxical alchemical process of "washing with fire" and "burning with water" to remove excess moisture and achieve a perfected state.</summary>

<keywords>Naaman, Jordan, leprosy, dropsy, philosophers' brass, Os Sacrum, Rosarium Philosophorum, alchemy, Karlovy Vary, Wiesbaden, Mainz, transmutation, tincture</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of medical treatments involving heat and fluid regulation, transitioning from general physiological observations to the specific challenges of treating "Quartan" fevers and their alchemical parallels.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>Discourse 13.</header>

Sometimes through the hot air of furnaces, sometimes using dung or sand, and by abstaining from drink; these are most effective remedies in both cases, depending on how and where they are used. In all these treatments, heat is the active agent. It draws out and consumes superfluous water through the emunctories <note>original: "Emunctoryes"; organs or canals that carry off waste, such as sweat glands or kidneys</note> and the pores of the body. External heat quickens the internal heat—that is, the vital spirit—to expel the moisture that is harmful to it as a useless waste product, which had previously suppressed the natural heat like an enemy.

In this cure, great diligence and precaution are needed, lest while one organ is helped, another is hurt. In a <term>Quartan</term> <note>a fever that returns every fourth day, often associated with malaria</note>—which was the <term>Platonists'</term> test of a <term>physician</term>—we find that thick and viscous humors, like the gum or glue of trees, gather from all the veins and the mass of blood. They descend through the hollow or great veins to the base of the back. There, they obstruct the <term>emulgent veins</term> <note>the renal veins, which carry blood away from the kidneys</note> which filter the <term>serous humor</term> <note>watery fluid</note> out of the blood. When these passages are blocked, they become less able to function, and more watery liquid remains in the body; thus, in a short time, if care is not taken, <term>dropsy</term> <note>edema; a condition characterized by an excess of watery fluid collecting in the cavities or tissues of the body</note> may occur, even if the other internal organs were initially healthy.

<term>Diuretics</term> <note>medications that increase urine production</note> do little or no good here, and <term>purgatives</term> <note>laxatives</note> even less, unless the humors are reduced over a specific period of time. <term>Sudorifics</term> <note>medicines that induce sweating</note> are also clearly harmful because they draw out the thinner, more subtle fluids while leaving the thicker ones behind; if continued, they weaken the body. Nature’s habit is to find a way to evacuate these <term>serosities</term> <note>excess watery fluids</note> through the pores when the path to the bladder is blocked. This situation is like being caught between <term>Scylla</term> and <term>Charybdis</term> <note>a Greek idiom meaning to be caught between two equally dangerous choices; here, the risk of dehydration versus the risk of fluid buildup</note>; anyone who wishes to preserve their health must avoid both extremes.

However, a <term>dropsy</term> that results from a damaged liver or spleen is extremely difficult to cure once established. Yet, in the case of <term>philosophical brass</term> <note>an alchemical term referring to a specific stage of material transformation, often associated with the early stages of the Great Work</note>, a cure is not impossible. In this context, the imbalance is accidental and secondary rather than essential or primary, provided the treatment is started cautiously, as we mentioned regarding the excess <term>serum</term> <note>watery fluid</note> in a Quartan fever. One must be careful that the body does not fall into a "consumption" <note>a wasting disease</note> through excessive <term>exiccation</term> <note>drying out</note>, or into a difficult-to-cure <term>dropsy</term> through excessive <term>humectation</term> <note>moistening</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the use of heat to expel excess fluids from the body and the specific difficulty of treating Quartan fevers, which can lead to dropsy if the kidneys are obstructed. The author warns against improper use of diuretics and sweating agents, eventually drawing a parallel between these physiological cures and the refinement of "philosophical brass" in alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>Quartan fever, dropsy, humors, emulgent veins, philosophical brass, diuretics, sudorifics, medical theory, alchemy</keywords>

->This is the Dragon which devours its tail.<-

## Epigram 14.

Hunger taught the octopus <note>original: "polypus"; in ancient natural history, the octopus was believed to eat its own tentacles when starving, becoming a symbol of self-consumption</note> to eat its own flesh,
And man to satisfy his appetite with his fellow man;
The hungry Dragon, feeding on its own tail,
Revives its fainting soul, which otherwise would fail.
Make him such tough meat <note>original: "hard meat"; this likely refers to the alchemical process of "digestion" where the substance must be broken down through intense heat or chemical action</note> until he
Eats and devours himself, kills himself, and is revived anew.

<summary>This page illustrates the Ouroboros, the dragon eating its own tail, as a symbol of the cyclical nature of the alchemical Great Work. It describes a process of self-destruction and rebirth necessary for the purification and "revival" of the alchemical spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Ouroboros, Dragon, alchemy, transformation, rebirth, polypus, Secrets of Nature, self-consumption</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse 14, moving from physical descriptions of serpents and dragons in natural history to their symbolic meaning in alchemy.</meta>

## Discourse 14.

It is an ancient saying that a serpent that has devoured another serpent becomes a dragon, for it is as cruel to its own kind as a thief or a murderer is to others. It is evident that such creatures exist in Africa in such great size and number that they destroyed a large part of Alexander the Great’s army. Among the Asachaei, a people of Ethiopia, there are very large serpents; these wrap themselves together like woven hurdles and, with their heads lifted up, travel toward better pastures. Reports say that the kings of India keep two dragons, one eighty and the other ninety cubits <note>original: "cubits"; an ancient unit of measurement roughly 18 inches long</note> in size. According to the observations of more recent writers, it is known today that creatures as large as the masts of ships are found near Angola. 

They also report that in certain mountains of India and Africa, there are great stores of gold guarded by dragons to prevent anyone from taking it. Because dragons frequent the springs or rivers that flow from the mountains, they are said to guard the gold by coincidence. For this reason, the philosophers <note>The author refers here to "natural philosophers" or alchemists</note> link many dragons or serpents to their treasures—such as the Golden Fleece, the Garden of the Hesperides, and other mythological figures or <term>chemical subjects</term>. These include Cadmus, Saturn, Aesculapius, and Mercury, whose wand <note>The caduceus</note> is entwined with two serpents, one male and one female. 

But do they mean anything by "dragons" other than chemical substances? In their study, they say that dragons yield mountains and the earth yields fountains. They also describe the extreme hunger of the dragon that devours its own tail. Although others may interpret this as the year returning into itself like a circle <note>the Ouroboros symbol</note>, the alchemists adapted it to their own work. By this dragon, they mean a serpent that devours another of its own kind. This is properly called <term>Sulphur</term>, as they all testify in countless writings. 

Lully <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a major figure in medieval philosophy and alchemy</note> says in Chapter 31 of his small book: "This, my son, is Sulphur! This is the serpent and dragon devouring its tail, the roaring lion, and the sharp sword that cuts, mortifies <note>original: "mortifying"; in alchemy, this refers to the chemical breakdown or 'death' of a substance</note>, and overcomes all things." 

And the <term>Rosary</term> <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note> states: "The Dragon does not die unless he is killed with his brother and sister." And a little later: "The Dragon is <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argent vive"; literally "living silver" or mercury</note> extracted from bodies, containing within it a body, soul, and spirit." In the same text, it is called by another name: <term>fetid water</term> <note>original: "ffatid water"; foul-smelling liquid</note>, which is produced after the separation of the elements. 

The Dragon is said to devour its tail by consuming its own volatile, poisonous, and moist parts. Afterward, without a tail, it appears more solid and moves more slowly, for its motion and volatility <note>original: "volubility"</note> depend chiefly on its tail. Other animals rely on feet, but dragons, serpents, and similar worms use the tightening and stretching of their bodies instead. Like poured water, they divide into specific paths, leaning now this way and now that. You can see this in almost all rivers, which wind their courses like serpents and run indirectly in loops. Therefore, it was not without reason that they called <term>quicksilver</term> a serpent and associated serpents with the god Mercury. Like a serpent, it seems to pull a tail behind it, flowing now here and now there with an unstable balance. For as a serpent creeps, so does Mercury, who therefore has wings upon his feet and head.

<summary>This page explores the symbolic transition of the dragon from a legendary beast of Africa and India to a central alchemical metaphor for Sulphur and Mercury (quicksilver). It explains the Ouroboros—the dragon devouring its tail—as a process of chemical purification and stabilization.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Ouroboros, Sulphur, Mercury, quicksilver, Ramon Lully, Rosarium Philosophorum, symbolism, Alexander the Great, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on alchemical symbolism, specifically focusing on the regenerative properties of the serpent and the dragon as metaphors for spiritual and physical transformation.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
# Discourse 14.

In Africa, it is said there are flying serpents which would devastate and depopulate everything if they were not destroyed by the bird called the Ibis. For this reason, the Ibis is placed among the sacred Egyptian images, both for the clear usefulness it provides to the entire country and for a hidden <note>original: occult</note> reason that very few people have understood. 

The dragon biting its tail and shedding its old skin is said to acquire a new one along with youth, just as nature has granted a longer life not only to crows, ravens, eagles, and stags <note>original: hartes</note>, but also to the serpent species. When an ant grows old, it acquires wings, and the same is true for many worms. When a man grows old, he is put into the earth, but being revived from the earth, he will be consecrated to eternal life. 

A powder is made from every burned serpent, which can be safely taken with great effect against all poisons. Such an <term>Alexipharmacum</term> <note>A medicinal remedy or preservative used to ward off poison or infection.</note> should also be made from this dragon—which has already devoured its tail, a part usually cast off by vipers—as it is a most effective remedy against the adversities of fortune and the body.

<summary>This text discusses the symbolic and medicinal properties of the Ibis, the dragon, and the serpent, linking their natural cycles of shedding and regeneration to alchemical remedies and the promise of eternal life.</summary>
<keywords>Ibis, Egypt, dragon, Ouroboros, regeneration, Alexipharmacum, alchemy, serpent, immortality</keywords>

### Emblem 15. On the Secrets of Nature.

->Let the work of the potter, which consists of dryness <note>original: "siccity"</note> and moisture,<-
->instruct you.<-

### Epigram 15.

->By mixing water with his clay, the potter<-
->Quickly gives shape to vessels on his wheel:<-
->Show this skill through your own purpose and art;<-
->Refresh the thirsty earth with liquid <note>original: "liquor"</note>.<-
->Your path is safe; proceed, but do not scorch the work—<-
->Do not drown your earth, nor allow it to burn.<-

<keywords>alchemy, potter's wheel, siccity, moisture, elements, Great Work, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the principles of alchemy, specifically focusing on the comparison between the growth of biological life and the formation of metals.</meta>

### Discourse 15.

As for the transmutation <note>original: "transmutation"; the alchemical process of changing one substance into another, such as turning base metals into gold.</note> of gems by it, and achieving the malleability of glass <note>The creation of "flexible glass" (vitrum flexile) was a legendary feat often attributed to ancient or alchemical skill.</note>: anyone may perceive by this whether these things are possible and consistent with the nature of things, or not. Geber <note>original: "Geber"; the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a 13th-century writer and influential figure in Western alchemy.</note> affirms that the philosopher speaks many things allegorically; he says of himself that where he spoke clearly and plainly, he said nothing, but where he spoke figuratively, he hid the truth like wheat under chaff.

A man reaps what he sows, which happens in plants and animals—though different species may sometimes come from the things sown. Whether these principles ought to be applied to metals (which are not propagated by seed as plants and animals are) must be considered. In metals, the parts are only homogeneous <note>original: "homogeneouy" (likely an OCR error for homogeneous); meaning the parts are of the same kind throughout.</note>—Sulphur and Argent vive <note>original: "Argent vive"; literally "living silver," the archaic term for quicksilver or mercury.</note>—whereas in living things, the parts are heterogeneous <note>Composed of different or diverse parts.</note> or organic. Metals have no receptacles for food, but living things do. In metals, one does not find nutrition, growth, or expansion in all dimensions, but in living things, these are very much present. Indeed, metals are elemental and heavy, allowing only for mixing; living things, besides mixing, possess either a vegetative or a sensitive soul <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, a "vegetative soul" governs growth and reproduction, while a "sensitive soul" governs perception and movement.</note>.

Nevertheless, it is undoubtedly true that there are substances in places under the earth which are not gold now, but will, after a thousand years, become gold by nature. Who will deny this to be the analogous "food" of gold? Both gold and the gold-making <note>original: "aurifick"</note> nature have the same origin and root <note>original: "radix"</note>, though gold has the more noble form. Therefore, once the food of gold is known, this process will also be manifest. The philosopher affirms that purity and moisture—or Sulphur and Argent vive—are the substances which, being most pure, must be extracted from two mountains.

<summary>This discourse explores the distinction between biological reproduction and the formation of metals, arguing that while metals lack organic "souls," they still possess a natural growth process that can be understood and replicated by finding the "food" of gold.</summary>
<keywords>transmutation, Geber, alchemy, Sulphur, Argent vive, mercury, vegetative soul, aurific nature, maturation of metals</keywords>

# Emblem 16. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.
## One lion has wings, the other none.

### Epigram 16.

The lion, prince of beasts in power and dominion <note>original: "sway"</note>,
Fights without fear, and scorns to run away;
Give him a spouse with wings of his own kind <note>original: "race"</note>,
To carry him up with her from place to place.
But he stands immovable and cancels her force;
Using emblems to demonstrate the course of Nature <note>In alchemical symbolism, the winged lion often represents the "volatile" principle (spirit or gas), while the wingless lion represents the "fixed" principle (solid body). Their union represents the process of stabilization.</note>.

<summary>This emblem uses the imagery of a winged and a wingless lion to illustrate the alchemical principle of uniting the volatile and the fixed elements of nature.</summary>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical symbolism, specifically focusing on the lion and lioness as allegories for philosophical substances and their interactions.</meta>

### Discourse 16.

It is a thing known by experience that a lion excels other animals not so much in size and strength of body as in generosity <note>original: "generosity"; here meaning nobility of spirit, courage, or high breeding</note>. If he is seen while hunting, being ashamed to run away, he withdraws by degrees if he is overwhelmed by a multitude. Once removed from the view of the hunters, he makes haste away, thinking it a dishonor to be driven out by hiding. He seeks a wood in flight, which he uses when anything pursues him. His bones are solid and not at all hollow; they are said to be so hard that when struck together, they yield fire as if from a steel and flint. He fears fire above all things. He seems to derive his substance from the nature of the sun, for in force and heat he excels other animals just as the sun excels the stars. Furthermore, he is always beheld with fiery and open eyes, just as the sun beholds the earth with an open and fiery eye. A lioness fighting for her cubs fixes her eyes upon the ground so that she may not be afraid of the hunters' staffs and spears. 

When a lion perceives the mating <note>original: "coition"</note> of a leopard with his mate, he takes revenge upon the lioness for adultery and inflicts severe punishment. The lioness, therefore, washes away the offense and the scent in a river; or, being conscious of the trespass, she also accompanies the adulterer, fleeing for fear of the male.

The philosophers, contemplating this wonderful nature of the beast, have produced several allegories of the lion—acting as hieroglyphic <note>original: "hieroglyphicall"; here meaning symbolic or emblematic</note> writings testifying to their secret works. Observing the lion to be a constant, firm animal without deceit or suspicion, they compared the better part of their philosophical composition to him. For as he does not flee, neither do they; as he has solid bones, so are they <term>fixed</term> <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is stable, non-volatile, and resistant to the transformative power of fire</note> and not to be overcome. And as the lioness is not always innocent and free from adultery, so neither are **Luna** <note>The Moon, representing the element Silver or the feminine principle in alchemy</note> nor **Mercury** <note>The element Quicksilver or the principle of volatility</note> without some blemish. Instead, they are joined by the ignorant sometimes to this matter and sometimes to that, whereby an adulterous conjunction of things mismatched and different in nature—rather than true matrimony <note>The "Alchemical Marriage," or the perfect union of opposites</note>—is established. 

For the offspring of a leopard and a lioness have no beautiful and handsome manes about the neck and shoulders, with which insignias <note>original: "ensignes"</note> those generated by the lion alone are adorned. The philosophical lioness must, therefore, be joined to her proper mate, and a genuine and noble cub will spring from them, which may easily be known by his paw <note>Likely a reference to the proverb "ex ungue leonem"—judging the whole of a thing by its part, or recognizing the "mark" of true gold/philosophy</note>. But the lioness must not be just any creature, but a winged one, who may be able to fight and contend with the lion, depending upon the swiftness of her feathers <note>original: "plumes"</note>. This is so that she is not overwhelmed by his extraordinary wrath, but can think of escape if he ever falls into a fury without cause; for when he stops her flight...

<summary>This page uses the natural lore of the lion and lioness—including their supposed solar nature and the "adultery" of the lioness with a leopard—as an alchemical allegory for the proper and improper union of substances.</summary>
<keywords>lion, lioness, alchemy, Luna, Mercury, allegory, fixed, philosophical marriage, solar symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical instructions for the union of chemical or symbolic principles, likely representing the "Fixed" and the "Volatile" through the imagery of lions.</meta>

### Discourse 16.

...is united with greater love toward her, friendship being established after discord. But you will say, "Who has ever seen a winged lioness, or what use are the feathers of a lioness?" There is a deep valley near Mount Cithaeron <note>original: Cytheron; a mountain range in Greece famous in mythology.</note> where nothing but flying lionesses are seen. At the top of these same mountains dwells a red lion of the kind that kills ravenously. 

Therefore, the lion must be captured and brought into the valley to be immediately paired with the winged lioness. She will easily allow herself to be overcome, as like attracts like. Afterward, they must both be moved out of that valley to the mountain peaks; from then on, they will never leave each other but will always remain together in unbreakable friendship. 

I admit that capturing lions is not easy; it is subject <note>original: obnoxious; in this period, it meant "vulnerable" or "exposed to."</note> to many dangers. Nevertheless, it must be attempted. A lion does not feed with the lioness but wanders alone, as stories tell us; therefore, they must be found and hunted separately. However, if the lion's cubs <term>whelps: the young offspring of a lion.</term> are obtained in their infancy when they first begin to walk—which is at two months old—and are joined together as mentioned before, the task will be free of any danger once they reach full growth and stature. 

Furthermore, they breed in the springtime, which must be carefully noted. After giving birth, lions use crossing and winding paths to prevent their dens from being discovered. Therefore, they must be sought out, and their cubs taken away with great diligence and care.

<summary>This page describes an alchemical allegory involving the capture and union of a terrestrial red lion and a winged lioness from Mount Cithaeron to create a lasting bond. It advises that these symbolic creatures are best captured as cubs during the spring to avoid the dangers of hunting them as adults.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, red lion, winged lioness, Mount Cithaeron, allegory, animal symbolism, chemical union</keywords>

### Emblem 17. On the Secrets of Nature.

<meta>This page continues Discourse 17, discussing the alchemical theory of the four types of fire. The text ends on a partial sentence with the catchword "not" indicating the beginning of the next page.</meta>

# Discourse 17.

Philosophers have frequently mentioned four types of fire necessary for natural works—specifically Ramon Lully, the author of *The Ladder* <note>original: *Scala*, likely referring to the *Scala Philosophorum*</note>, George Ripley, and many others. Regarding these fires, Ramon says in the *Scala*: "Take note that these operations are opposites. The 'fire against nature' dissolves the spirit of a fixed body into a watery cloud and binds the body of a volatile spirit into a congealed earth. Conversely, the 'fire of nature' solidifies the dissolved spirit of a fixed body into a spherical earth and—through the 'fire against nature'—transforms the fixed body of a volatile spirit not into a watery cloud, but into philosophical water."

George Ripley explains these fires more clearly in his third gate <note>Referring to *The Compound of Alchemy*, which is divided into twelve "gates."</note> as follows: "There are four kinds of fires which you ought to know: the natural, the unnatural, the fire against nature, and the elemental fire which ignites wood. These fires were used, and no more. The 'fire against nature' is meant to break down <note>original: "excruciate"</note> bodies; it is the 'dragon' I mentioned, burning violently like the fires of hell. The 'fire of nature' is the third menstruum <term>Menstruum: A solvent or substance used in alchemy to dissolve or extract others, often compared to blood in its life-giving or transformative role.</term>; that fire exists naturally in everything. We call 'unnatural fire' an 'occasioned fire'—such as the heat of ashes or baths used to cause putrefaction <term>Putrefaction: The stage of alchemical decay or decomposition, necessary for the transformation of matter.</term>. Without these fires, you can bring nothing to putrefaction, which allows your matter to be separated so that it can be properly proportioned for a new union. Therefore, create a fire within your glass vessel that can burn bodies more effectively than elemental fire."

These are their teachings. They are indeed called "fires" because they possess a fiery power: the natural fire in solidifying <note>original: "coagulating"</note>, the unnatural fire in dissolving, the fire against nature in corrupting, and the elemental fire in providing the initial heat and motion. There is a linked <note>original: "concatenat"</note> order observed among them, so that the second is prompted by the first, the third by the second, the fourth by the third, and the first acts together with them all. In this way, one is the "agent" (the doer) and the other is the "patient" (the receiver), and the same fire can be both agent and patient in different respects.

The same phenomenon observed in iron rings or pillars held together by a magnet and joined by mutual contact is found in these fires. The elemental fire, like a magnet, transmits its power through the second and third fires even to the fourth, joining them to one another through mutual operations. It causes them to stick together until the internal action is completed among the highest elements. The first is elemental fire in both name and nature; the second is airy; the third is watery or of a Lunar nature; the fourth is earthy.

It is unnecessary to say anything about the first fire, because it is known to every man’s eye and touch. The other three are called "dragons," "menstruums," "waters," "sulfurs," and "mercuries." They are called "dragons" because they possess a toxic <note>original: "venonosity"</note> quality and devour "serpents" of their own kind; they soften and alter—that is, they dissolve and solidify—the bodies incorporated and mixed with them. They are called "menstruums" because the "philosophical infant" <note>A common alchemical metaphor for the developing Philosopher's Stone or the "Great Work."</note> is produced from them and nourished by them until its birth. Lully, in the third distinction of his *Book of the Quintessence*, has two

<summary>This page defines and distinguishes four types of alchemical "fire"—natural, unnatural, fire against nature, and elemental—explaining their roles in dissolving and solidifying matter. It uses metaphors of dragons and magnets to describe the interactive forces required to produce the "philosophical infant."</summary>

# Discourse 17.

...<term>menstruum</term> <note>original: "menstruum"; a solvent liquid used in alchemy to dissolve solids</note>, the vegetable, and mineral. <term>Ripley</term> <note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist</note>, in the preface of his *The Gates*, mentions three types which are really one and are in agreement. This is because the generation of the "infant" <note>a metaphorical term for the Philosopher's Stone or the resulting alchemical product</note> is made of them all together. A "white water" precedes its birth; this water is not part of the substance of the infant but is a superfluity, and therefore it must be separated. 

These substances are called "watery" because they reveal a watery nature when placed in fire—that is, they show a flowing liquidity that resembles water. It is clear that the properties of these "waters" are diverse and wonderful: while some undergo putrefaction, others are coagulated into very hard stones fit for building. Very similar to these are the "mineral waters" of the philosophers, which condense and turn into a stony consistency. They are also called <term>Sulphurs</term> because of the power of <term>Sulphur</term> <note>in alchemy, Sulphur often represents the active, combustible, or "masculine" principle</note> which they contain. In this process, the "sulfur of nature" is mixed and unified with the other sulfur. Thus, two <term>Sulphurs</term> are dissolved by a single one; one is separated from two, and the <term>Sulphurs</term> are preserved and contained by <term>Sulphur</term>, as <term>Eximiding</term> says in the *<term>Turba</term>* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts</note>. 

<term>Dardary</term> <note>likely Dardaris, a character or authority cited in the *Turba*</note> declares what these <term>Sulphurs</term> are in these words: "<term>Sulphurs</term> are souls hidden in the four elements. When extracted by art, they naturally contain one another and are joined together. If you can use water to govern and purify that which is hidden in the 'belly of Sulphur,' that hidden thing will rejoice upon meeting its own nature, just as water does with its like." 

<term>Mosing</term> <note>another authority cited in the *Turba*</note> also says: "I will now tell you what it is: the first is fiery <term>Argent vive</term> <note>original: "Argent vive"; literally "living silver," or quicksilver/mercury</note>; the second is the body compounded within it; and the third is the 'water of Sulphur,' by which the first is washed, expanded, and governed until the work is accomplished." 

What is said of <term>Sulphurs</term> may also be understood regarding the various <term>Mercuries</term>. For as the same <term>Mosing</term> later says: "<term>Argent vive</term> <term>Lambar</term> <note>an archaic alchemical term for a specific state of mercury</note> is the <term>Magnesia</term> <note>in alchemy, this refers not to the modern element, but to the "maternal" raw material of the Stone</note>, but <term>Argent vive</term> or <term>Auripigment</term> <note>original: "Auripigment"; orpiment, a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral</note> is that <term>Sulphur</term> which rises from a mixed compound." 

I will omit the inclusion of further testimonies, as they are infinite and obvious to everyone. These "four fires" are included, as it were, in spheres and orbs; that is, every one has a particular center toward which or from which its motion tends. Nevertheless, they are kept so bound together—partly by nature and partly by art—that one can operate little or nothing without the others. Indeed, the action of one is the passion <note>here meaning "the state of being acted upon"</note> of another, and on the contrary—

## Emblem 18. On the Secrets of Nature.

->Fire loves to burn, not to turn things into gold; only gold <note>original: "aurify"</note> can do that.<-

### Epigram 18.

> All agents of nature spread their own seed,
> So that they may produce their own kind <note>original: "species"</note>;
> Thus, fire turns all things into fire, and there never was
> A true effect that did not come from its own proper cause:
> Gold does not create fire, and fire certainly does not create gold;
> All things are reproduced <note>original: "propagated"</note> through their own seed.

<vocab>Nature, Fire, Gold, Seed, Species, Turn into gold <note>original: "Aurify"</note>, Reproduced <note>original: "propagated"</note></vocab>

<summary>This emblem explains the alchemical and natural principle that every substance can only reproduce its own kind through its "seed," meaning fire only creates fire and gold only creates gold.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Generation, Seed, Gold, Fire, Causality, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<page-num>60</page-num>

# Discourse 18.

Nature's way of operating in all individuals of the universe is to use one single process to complete and perfect one single motion, as appears in the anatomy of the human body. In the body, one muscle serves only for one motion—that is, the attractive (contraction)—while for the expansive (extension), there is another muscle opposite to the first. Thus, if any part or limb is to be moved in a circular form, it must be accomplished by various muscles positioned in a circle.

So, the operation of fire is one and single: that is to say, to heat and ignite—even to assimilate things to itself—and it burns all things to which it is applied, if they are combustible. On this subject, <term>Avicenna</term> <note>original: Ibn Sīnā (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and philosophy were foundational in Europe</note> says in his book *On the Congelation of Stones* <note>original: *De Congelatione et Conglutinatione Lapidum*, a medieval mineralogical text often attributed to Avicenna</note>: "That which falls into a salt pit becomes salt, and that which falls into fire becomes fire; however, some things change sooner and some slower, according to the power of the active agent and the resistance of the passive subject." And there is a place in Arabia that colors all bodies within it with its own color. 

In this way, every natural thing possesses a "virtue" <note>In this context, 'virtue' refers to an inherent power or specific natural property</note> infused into it by nature, by which it acts upon things mixed with or applied to it, assimilating and altering their nature and form. What is called "generation" in plants and animals through the propagation of seed is actually the infusion of this virtue and the process of assimilation in individual or mixed bodies. 

The sun, the light of heaven, projects its rays upon the earth; when these rays are gathered together in concave or burning glasses, they demonstrate their origin by appearing as the projected forms of the sun itself. From this it is manifest that the rays of the sun are nothing else but a fiery flame extended and dispersed across a wide area. When these rays are dilated and then contracted again by concave, transparent, and circular or precisely shaped instruments, they shine like a flame in concave steel mirrors and burn objects placed in their path. 

In the same manner, a certain virtue exists as a vapor dispersed within a certain elementary body; if this vapor is gathered together and drawn into one point, it turns into water, and that water turns into earth. As <term>Avicenna</term> explains in the book cited above, "Earth is made of water when the qualities of earth overcome it, and likewise the reverse." 

However, there is a certain substance that some ingenious men use when they wish to solidify a dry substance. This substance is compounded of two watery liquids and is called <term>Lac Virginis</term> <note>literally "Virgin's Milk"; in alchemy, this usually refers to a white chemical precipitate or a specific mercurial water</note>, which produces this effect most reliably. Thus he says: some <unclear>philosophers</unclear> exist who are certain they can increase or multiply the virtues in a <term>loadstone</term> <note>a naturally magnetized piece of the mineral magnetite</note>. We have seen a similar effect in a piece of silver, which, weighing scarcely one pound, attracts and holds up an iron anchor of twenty-eight pounds. This would have been impossible if the virtue had not been increased and strengthened within it, which was undoubtedly achieved by drawing the dispersed virtues together into a single point or pole, or by attracting the power out of a large body into a smaller one. 

Others are found who say that a <term>plumbifying stone</term> <note>a "lead-making" stone believed to facilitate transmutation</note> may be made by the <term>sulfurous breath of Saturn</term> <note>In alchemy, "Saturn" is the planetary symbol for lead</note>, infused and retained until it is converted into <term>common mercury</term>, which then continually converts common mercury into lead. Some boast that they can, by using <term>antimony</term> or its <term>stellated regulus</term> <note>the metallic form of antimony; "stellated" refers to the star-like crystalline pattern that forms on its surface when purified</note>, create copper from the fumes of copper in the time it takes a man to eat an egg—indeed, they claim to have made all metals. But I will not detract from their reputation, though to me it seems not

<summary>This page discusses the natural principles of "virtue" and assimilation, explaining how substances like fire, salt, and light transform other bodies into their own likeness. It also explores alchemical and physical phenomena, including the intensification of magnetic power and the purported transmutation of metals using antimony and lead.</summary>

# Discourse 18.

...likely to be true. I do not know whether those who endeavor to derive gold from gold are more confident or more successful, according to the saying of the "golden poet":

> He who desires barley sows only barley; within gold is the seed of gold.

Every natural thing indeed has a certain power of multiplying itself; however, this capacity is only activated in plants and animals, and not at all in metals, minerals, things dug out of the earth, or atmospheric phenomena <note>original: "meteors"</note>. Some plants grown from a single small grain of seed often yield a thousand or more seeds, and so multiply and propagate themselves every year. Animals also have their offspring in greater or lesser numbers, according to the nature of each species. But gold, silver, lead, tin, iron, copper, or <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argent vive"</note> are never found to have multiplied themselves or their species in that manner, even though one metal may very often be found transformed <note>original: "commuted"</note> and improved <note>original: "nobilitated"</note> into another. 

Nevertheless, the philosophers <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers to alchemists.</note> affirm that the principle of "igniting" <note>original: "ignifying"</note> is in fire, and likewise the principle of <term>making gold</term> <note>original: "aurifying"</note> is in gold. But where is the <term>tincture</term> <note>an elixir or agent believed to be able to transform base metals into gold</note> by which gold must be made? This must be sought in gold's own principles and creation <note>original: "generation"</note>, not in things of another nature. For if fire produces fire, a pear produces a pear, and a horse produces a horse, then lead must generate lead and not silver, and gold must generate gold and not the tincture. 

Moreover, the alchemists possess a peculiar gold. They not only do not deny that it should be added to the <term>gold-making stone</term> <note>original: "aurifick Stone"</note> as a <term>ferment</term> <note>a substance used to catalyze or "ripen" the alchemical work</note> at the end of the process, but they also affirm that it is absolutely necessary. This ferment converts the substance being fermented into its own nature; without it, the whole composition would never reach perfection.

<summary>This page explores the alchemical argument that gold contains its own "seed" for multiplication, while noting the difference between biological reproduction in plants and animals and the chemical transmutation of minerals.</summary>

## Emblem 19. On the secrets of Nature.

->If you kill one of the four, they will all suddenly die.<-

> And they will all immediately surrender <note>original: "resigne"</note> to death,
> Their lives depending jointly on a single breath. <note>These "four brothers" represent the four classical elements—earth, water, air, and fire. <meta>This page continues the alchemical interpretation of mythological figures, specifically focusing on Geryon as an allegory for the unity of the elements.</meta>

# Discourse 19.

The poets feign Geryon, King of Spain, to have been three-bodied <note>original: "tricorporall"</note>, who had oxen of a purple color, which a dog with two heads and a dragon with seven were charged with as wardens. And the same Geryon is reported to be the son of Chrysaor, sprung from the blood of Medusa, and that dragon the son of Typhon and Echidna. Because these things do not agree with any history or truth of the matter as they sound, and yet make a noise in all <term>Chymicall Allegoryes</term> <note>Alchemical allegories: symbolic stories used to describe chemical processes</note>, they are not without cause used by us here.

But by the three bodies of Geryon, we mean three faces beheld in one father, according to the sense of <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note>, or, as others would have it, four—having regard to so many <term>Elements</term>. For from a quadrangle a triangle should be made, as that is made from a circle; so this returns into a circle. For the consanguinity <note>blood relationship or kinship</note> and natural conjunction of the bodies of Geryon, or the elements, is so great that one being overcome or killed, the rest also die of themselves and putrefy without the administration of any manual force. 

As to things with two bodies, it is known that one being dead, the other also wastes and consumes. As in Italy, we have seen a boy four years of age with two bodies, who <unclear>absteeded</unclear> <note>likely "bore" or "supported"</note> the head of his brother, bearing the other's body within his own, which hung down to the navel where they were bound or joined together. But if he that hung down, being much smaller and carried by the other, should be touched about the hands or feet too hard, the larger boy thereby felt pain—and even hunger, if the smaller wanted food. And this is the combination and sympathy of nature, whereby the members and parts of one body, or those joined with another, are mutually moved and affected together. If one part is sound and unhurt, the other does not necessarily continue sound and unhurt; but if one is grievously hurt, the rest also sympathize and perish by the same malady. 

So, if a neighbor gains much, no profit thereby accrues to his neighbor; but if he suffers loss by fire, his neighbor receives much damage. For your affairs are in danger when your neighbor's house has taken fire. It is not, therefore, a thing contradictory to truth for the other three to die by the destruction of one of these four brethren, seeing that it may happen in various ways: either by finding the same ends as beginnings of life (being born at one birth by both father and mother, as we have read has happened to some, perhaps by the inclination of the stars); or by being joined together not only in souls but also ligaments of the body; or by confederation of mind; by strong imagination in time of pestilence; or by the vow of a league. 

A certain heathenish people live by the name of <term>pythagoreans</term> <note>The author refers to Hindus in India, so named by Europeans because their belief in reincarnation mirrored the teachings of Pythagoras</note> in the Indies under the government of the Great <term>Mogulle</term> <note>The Mughal Emperor</note> (who now reigns there as the <unclear>minorit</unclear> <note>descendant or successor</note> of the successor of <term>Tamberlan</term> <note>Tamerlane, the Mongol conqueror</note>). Among them, this ancient custom is hitherto observed: that if the husband dies, his wife is burnt with fire or, as now, lives perpetually in the highest infamy, deserted by all and esteemed as a dead woman. This was done so that wives might not kill their husbands by poison, unless also...

<summary>The author uses the myth of the three-bodied Geryon to illustrate the alchemical unity of the four elements, comparing their interconnectedness to conjoined twins and the social "sympathy" of shared disasters.</summary>
<keywords>Geryon, alchemy, four elements, conjoined twins, sympathy of nature, Mughal Empire, Sati, Pythagoras, Hermes Trismegistus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "brethren" (the elements or chemical components) and their symbolic death and rebirth within the alchemical process, likely following a description of Geryon or a similar mythological figure.</meta>

# Discourse 19.

...they were willing to die. So in the philosophical work <note>The "philosophical work" refers to the practice of alchemy, often called the "Great Work."</note>, when one of the brethren is dead, the others also perish by fire—not by compulsion, but voluntarily—so that they may not survive in sorrow and infamy. Or, if one is assaulted with a club, sword, or stone, he will commence a civil war with his brethren. This is similar to the giants who sprang from the teeth of a dragon and opposed Jason, and elsewhere also Cadmus; they will all fall by mutual destruction. 

For if you touch or hurt the one that carries air, he will rise up against the two nearest to him—namely, the one that carries water and the one that carries fire. These two will, from both sides, oppose the one who carries earth and the one who first started the quarrel, until they have given and received mutual wounds from which they die. For it is decreed among these brethren that the more earnestly and vehemently they love one another, the more impossible they are to reconcile if they once begin to hate. Instead, they pursue one another to the death, just as the most bitter vinegar is produced from the sweetest honey in a stomach that is too hot, or when the liver is corrupted. 

Therefore, kill the one who is alive, but do so in such a way that, being dead, you may bring him to life again <note>original: "quicken him again"</note>; otherwise, the death of the one killed avails you nothing. For death will reveal him—that is to say, after his resurrection. Then death, darkness, and the sea will flee from him, as <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the founder of alchemy.</note> asserts; and the dragon which watches the crevices will avoid the rays of the sun. Then our dead Son lives, and the King arrives through fire. 

<margin>Chapter 9.</margin> <term>Bolinus</term> <note>Likely referring to Balinas, the Arabic name for Apollonius of Tyana, a philosopher to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note> signifies the same thing in his metaphor in the *Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (Rosary of the Philosophers), a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note>, saying: "And let this be done: when you have drawn me partly from my nature, and my wife partly from her nature, you must then also kill those natures. Then we are brought to life by a new and spiritual resurrection, so that afterwards we cannot die."

## Emblem 20. Of the secrets of Nature.

### Nature teaches nature how to subdue fire.

## Epigram 20.

Fire, that insulting <note>original: "insulting"; here meaning aggressively assaulting or triumphing over</note> dragon, is prepared
To scorch the virgin's beauty without regard:
Distressed and seeking help, she <insert>spies</insert> <unclear>looks for</unclear> a man-of-war <note>original: "man of warre"; here referring to a soldier or armed man</note>
Equipped, all, from head to foot <note>original: "cap a pe"; from the French "cap-à-pie," meaning head to toe</note>, as such men are;
Who, shielding her from harm, assaults the flames,
And by this precedent <note>original: "president"; used in the sense of an example or previous instance</note> her fear proclaims.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the relationship between different "natures," moving from biological metaphors of education to the practical application of these principles in metallurgy and alchemy.</meta>

### Discourse 20.

The common sign and symbol by which the philosophers recognize one another is that nature is guided, taught, governed, subdued, and overcome by nature—just as a young scholar is by a mistress, a waiting maid by her lady, a subject by a queen, or even a daughter by her mother and a kinswoman by her kinswoman. How true this is appears through daily experience in the education of youth among men and in other actions, such as literary discipline or instruction, government, and the like.

Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (AD 23–79), a Roman author and natural philosopher.</note> writes of the nightingale that one teaches, attends, observes, imitates, and overcomes another in singing. Or, being overcome, the bird grieves; sometimes, being outdone and vanquished in the conflict, its throat breaks and it perishes, falling down dead in the midst of singing. We also see how all sorts of birds begin to accustom and instruct their young while they are still unfeathered and too tender for flying; thus, not only nature, but also art or practice has produced in them the habit of flying—though nature alone gave them the power and the organs to perform that action, without which neither instruction nor art can find a foundation or place. 

So colts are taught to run by the mare, puppies to bark by the bitch, and young foxes to be crafty by their dam. Nor is there any animate and sensitive nature or species found which does not guide, instruct, and govern another nature as its inferior <note>original: "minor"</note>, or allows itself to be overcome by another nature, as a parent. We do not perceive such discipline in vegetables, yet nevertheless, practice is observed in them, and many hands are of much effectiveness. For while grain is still in the blade, it may be cleansed of inconvenient thistles and tares; while a tree is still a twig, it may be bent and made to grow as you please.

So in metals and philosophical subjects, one nature keeps, preserves, and defends another nature in fire, as is known to founders and testers <note>original: "lesters," likely a transcription error for "testers" or "assay masters."</note>, but especially to the masters of natural things. Iron is very helpful and acts as a midwife to silver and gold when they are still very tender and "spiritual" (as they call it), mixed in their ore with <term>Cadmia</term> <note>an oxide of zinc or a cobalt-rich ore found on the walls of furnaces.</note>, arsenic, or <term>Antimony</term> (the "thistle"), if it is cast into mines to be burned in the fire of furnaces. 

In the same manner, iron itself—if it is intended to be changed into steel—is saved from burning by white stones found at the seashores. Some cast the powder of crystal glass, or the <term>gall of glass</term> <note>a scum or dross that forms on the surface of molten glass, also known as sandiver.</note> upon metallic powders which are to be dissolved, so that they do not perish by excessive fire. Instead of this "gall of glass," the philosophers use their **Eudica**, which <term>Morienus</term> <note>Morienus Romanus, a legendary 7th-century alchemist and hermit.</note> says is also the gall of glass and is to be found in glassy vessels. For the heat of fire consumes the body with hasty burning, but Eudica, when applied, will free bodies and changes into earth to prevent any burning. For bodies, when they no longer retain their souls, are quickly burned. Eudica (the dregs <note>original: "fax," from the Latin *faex* meaning dregs or sediment.</note> of glass) is indeed very compatible with all bodies, for it vivifies and prepares them, and defends them from all burning. These are the sayings of Morienus the Roman. This, therefore, is the nature which teaches another nature to resist fire and be accustomed to it, which is the Mistress instructing the...

<summary>This discourse uses metaphors of education and animal behavior to explain the alchemical principle that "nature overcomes nature," specifically regarding how certain substances (like Eudica or iron) protect precious metals from being destroyed by fire.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, nature, Pliny the Elder, nightingales, metallurgy, Eudica, Morienus the Roman, gall of glass, antimony, transmutation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical discourse, using mythological and historical allegories to describe the relationship between the various stages of the "philosophical work" or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>67</page-num>

### Discourse 20.

...young scholar, and if you consider well which is the Queen governing the subject, and which is the daughter ennobling <note>original: "nobilitating"</note> and dignifying the mother. This is that "red servant," who is joined in matrimony with his fragrant <note>original: "odoriferous"</note> mother, and from her begets an offspring <note>original: "progeny"</note> far more noble than the parents themselves. This is Pyrrhus, the son of Achilles, and the young man with red hair, golden garments <note>original: "vestment"</note>, black eyes, and white feet. This is the knight with a collar or chain, armed with a sword and shield <note>original: "buckler"</note> against the Dragon, to rescue from its jaws the pure and unviolated virgin Albifica <note>From the Latin *albico*, meaning "to make white."</note>, surnamed Roja <note>Red</note> or Blanca <note>White</note>.

This is the monster-killing Hercules, who delivers Hesione, the daughter of Laomedon, from the monster after she was exposed to a terrible and cruel whale <note>In Greek mythology, Hercules rescued Hesione from a sea monster sent by Poseidon to plague Troy.</note>. This is that Perseus, who defends, rescues, and marries Andromeda—the daughter of Cassiopeia and Cepheus, King of Ethiopia—from the sea monster by showing the head of Medusa. 

He may also be compared with those ancient Romans who were the preservers and rescuers of the people: Marcus Curtius, Mucius <note>original: "Scevius"</note> Scaevola, Horatius Cocles, Manlius Capitolinus, and others like them, who rescued the city (acting as the mother) from dangers. For this is the way and method of nature tending toward the perfection of any work: to bring one thing out of another, a more perfect thing out of an imperfect one, and to turn potential into reality <note>original: "reduce power into action"</note>. Nature does not perfect all things in a single moment, but one thing after another. 

Not only this, but she also establishes her deputy in the first place, to whom she leaves the power of life and death—that is, the power of forming other things. For example, in the creation <note>original: "generation"</note> of a human, she uses a long process of ten months. According to the opinion of Aristotle, she first builds the heart as the representative <note>original: "vicar"</note>, deputy, or principal organ <note>original: "bowel"</note>. Then, the heart outlines, forms, and perfects the other parts necessary for nutrition, life, the senses, and the power of reproduction. It communicates life and life-giving <note>original: "vivific"</note> spirits to them through systole and diastole—the expansion <note>original: "dilatation"</note> and compression of the arteries—as long as it is not hindered by disease or violence. And thus one nature gives birth to another, which you must observe and follow as an example for the alchemical process <note>original: "philosophical work"</note>, as it is a most clear and evident thing.

<summary>The author uses classical mythology and Roman history as allegories for alchemical transformations, specifically describing how one stage of the process "rescues" or perfects another. He further explains that the alchemist should imitate nature's gradual methods, citing Aristotle's theory of how the heart forms first to create the rest of the human body.</summary>

# Emblem 21. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

->Create from <gloss>for</gloss> the man and the woman a circle; from that, a square; from that,<-
->a triangle; from that, a circle; and you will have the Philosopher's <term>Stone</term>.<-
<note>The Philosopher's Stone was the legendary substance of alchemy said to transform base metals into gold and provide the Elixir of Life.</note>

## Epigram 21.

->Provide a circle for the man and wife;<-
->Make it into a square with equal sides;<-
->Turn that into a triangle, resulting in a sphere:<-
->And then the blessed Stone will appear to you.<-
->If you find this too difficult <note>original: "high"</note> and too obscure <note>original: "abstruse"</note>,<-
->Geometry will soon enlighten <note>original: "inform"</note> your mind.<-

<summary>This page illustrates the "Squaring of the Circle," a central alchemical metaphor that uses geometric transformations—moving from a circle to a square to a triangle and back to a circle—to represent the creation of the Philosopher's Stone. It suggests that if the spiritual concepts are too difficult to grasp, one should look to the principles of geometry for clarity.</summary>
