--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical inquiry into the nature of human knowledge, contrasting Platonic and Aristotelian views on whether learning is a process of "remembering" innate truths or developing inherent "sparks" of potential.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>

<header>Discourse 21</header>

It is the doctrine of that most famous philosopher, Plato, that knowledge is, as it were, actually engraved and imprinted in the mind of man. He believed this serves as the foundation of all arts and sciences, and that by the remembrance and repetition of these innate ideas, all learning may be known and understood by anyone. 

To prove this, he introduces a youth who is still very young, ignorant, and illiterate, whom he first instructs through geometrical questions <note>This refers to a famous scene in Plato’s dialogue, the *Meno*, where Socrates questions a slave boy to demonstrate that mathematical truths are innate.</note>. The youth was observed to answer all questions directly and—not by rote <note>original: "usery"</note> or without thought—attained the very heart of such a complex science. From this, Plato concludes that no doctrine or discipline is truly learned or understood for the first time; rather, it is only recalled and brought back into the mind by children through remembrance and repetition. 

He alludes to his *Annus Magnus* <term>The "Great Year," a vast cosmic cycle after which the stars and planets return to their original positions</term>, in which he claimed that before a solar cycle of 48,000 years, the same people, things, and actions existed before the revolution of the heavens, just as they do now. However, every sensible man understands that such ideas are like dreams, without any foundation in truth. 

We do not deny that there are certain sparks of knowledge naturally rooted <note>original: "radicated"</note> in us, with the potential to be put into action through use and instruction. But we deny that these sparks are so great or of such a quality that they are the primary sources <note>original: "seminaryes," meaning seed-beds or nurseries</note> of arts and sciences without any previous preparation. 

From where, then, did the sciences and arts come, if men did not invent them? Others may ask whether they were first taught by heaven, or by the gods of the heathens. I answer that it is one thing to say that burning coals are covered under ashes in such great plenty that if they are revealed by simply removing the ashes, they are sufficient to boil meat or warm our cold limbs. It is quite another thing to claim that only small sparks lie hidden there, which—before they can be useful for boiling or warming—must be stirred up, quickened, and nourished with fuel through the industry, art, and care of man. Otherwise, they may easily be extinguished and completely reduced to cold ashes. 

The latter is the assertion of the Aristotelians; the former is that of the Platonists. Reason and experience agree with the Aristotelians, while only fantasy or imagination agrees with the Platonists. Consequently, one might ask why Plato wrote above the door of his school that he who was ignorant of geometry should not be admitted, even though he also claimed that children actually know it! Are men more unlearned than boys? Or have those of full age forgotten the things that boys know? That cannot be assumed. 

For we see that a brute animal, by natural instinct, shrinks from and avoids the danger of fire, water, sudden accidents, and the like, even when newly born. Yet a human infant neither knows nor avoids such things until he is hurt—such as when his finger is slightly burnt by the flame of a candle, like a moth <note>original: "Fire-flye"</note> that burns its wings and falls down. Why do the young bee, the fly, and the gnat not fly directly into the fire, even though they do not know the danger from experience? They do so because nature has taught them; but she has not taught a child...

<summary>The author critiques Plato's theory of reminiscence, arguing that while humans have innate "sparks" of potential, true knowledge requires education and effort, unlike the natural instincts seen in animals.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Aristotle, geometry, epistemology, innate ideas, Annus Magnus, Meno, instinct, education, philosophy of mind</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the philosophical and physical applications of geometry, specifically the "squaring of the circle" as an alchemical process.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>

# Discourse 21

If geometry is so natural and easy for children, how does it happen that the squaring of a circle was not known to Plato himself? Aristotle, the student of Plato, affirms it is possible to be known, though it was not yet known in his time. However, it is evident that this was not unknown to the natural philosophers <note>natural philosophers: early scientists or alchemists who studied the physical world</note>, because they command that a circle be converted into a quadrangle; and this, through a triangle, be turned back into a circle. By "circle," they mean a most simple body without angles; by "quadrangle," they mean the four elements. It is as if they were saying that one must take the most simple physical figure that can be found, which then divides into four elemental colors and becomes an equilateral quadrangle. 

Now, everyone understands that this squaring is physical and agreeable to nature. From this, far more benefit comes to a Commonwealth, and more enlightenment to the human mind, than from that mathematical and merely theoretical squaring derived from abstract matter. To learn this perfectly, a geometrician working with solid bodies must inquire into what depth of a solid figure—for example, a sphere or a cube—can be known, and transfer that knowledge to manual use or practice. For instance: if the capacity or circumference of a sphere is 32 feet, how long must one side of a cube be to equal the capacity of that sphere? Or, conversely, if a sphere contains 32 measures within its circumference, how long will one side of a cube be to contain the same amount? One must be able to look back from the measures contained within the sphere or cube to the actual feet of every circumference. 

In the same way, the philosophers would have the quadrangle reduced into a triangle—that is, into body, spirit, and soul. These three appear in three colors that precede the final "redness." For example: 
* The body (or earth) appears in the blackness of Saturn; 
* The spirit appears in a lunar whiteness, like water; 
* The soul (or air) appears in a solar citrinity <note>citrinity: "yellowing," the stage in alchemical transformation between whiteness (albedo) and redness (rubedo)</note>. 

Then the triangle will be perfect. But this triangle must likewise be changed into a circle—that is, into an unchanging redness. Through this operation, the woman is converted into the man and made one with him. Thus the senary <note>senary: relating to the number six, considered a "perfect number" because its divisors (1, 2, and 3) add up to itself</note>—the first perfect number completed by one, two, and three—returns again to a single unit, in which there is eternal rest and peace.

<summary>This page explains the "squaring of the circle" not as a mathematical problem, but as an alchemical allegory for the refinement of matter through the four elements and the three stages of soul, spirit, and body.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, squaring the circle, Plato, Aristotle, four elements, alchemical colors, Saturn, citrinity, senary, unity, Great Work</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical interpretation of mythological allegories, specifically focusing on the preparation of metals like lead (Saturn) and tin (Jupiter) through "philosophical" processes rather than common ones.</meta>

# Discourse 22.

Just as statues of Mercury <note>original: "Mercuriall Statues"; also known as Herms, these were stone pillars used as milestones and boundary markers in ancient Greece and Rome.</note> are commonly placed at crossroads where three ways meet, together with a title and inscription to guide doubtful travelers onto the right way, so too are certain sharp insights inserted into many of the books and volumes of the philosophers. These are scattered here and there, allegorical and ambiguous, so that the seeker of truth may be warned and led, as if by the hand, into the right path. 

One of these emblematic inscriptions is this one, which is understood to mean that lead must be made from <term>philosophical brass</term> <note>original: "philosophicall brasse"; a symbolic substance used in the "Great Work" of alchemy, not ordinary brass.</note> and tin from lead. This is what Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, an influential 8th-century polymath and alchemist.</note> calls "white lead." He also teaches how Saturn <note>The alchemical name for lead.</note> may be converted into Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for tin.</note> by washing it with Mercury <note>Quicksilver.</note>. Therefore, this indicator—though it is the speech of Battus <note>In mythology, Battus was a shepherd who witnessed Mercury stealing Apollo’s cattle; he was turned to stone for breaking a promise of silence.</note>—must be believed if he ever reveals the philosophers' secret places, saying they were in the mountains and under those mountains. For many men, as Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova, a medieval physician and alchemist.</note> affirms in **Novum lumen** <note>original: "New Light"</note> Chapter 1, wander in the mountains without knowing these animals <note>Likely a metaphor for alchemical substances or "spirits."</note>, yet they are sold openly at a very low price. 

In the summer, snow is sometimes observed on the highest mountains, as are clouds most commonly. By these—acting like vapor and water—black lead is washed and turned white. However, their crystals are found frozen and hardened by ice in the lowest valleys and mines, along with <term>Lapis specularis</term> <note>original: "Lapis specularis"; a transparent mineral like mica or selenite used historically for windows and mirrors.</note> and talc, which is wonderful for making the face white and beautiful if an oil is made from it.

But this soft, clear, and running Mercury, when well-prepared, corrects the defects of Saturn and raises him to the throne of Jupiter. However, these should not be understood as "vulgar" <note>In alchemy, "vulgar" refers to ordinary, impure metals found in common commerce, as opposed to "philosophical" metals.</note> Saturn and Jupiter, because common metals do not enter into the "physical" <note>In this context, "physical" refers to the "Great Work" of alchemy or the "Physic" of the philosophers.</note> work except when they are purged and made "physical" through long preparation. 

Saturn is the father of all "gentile" <note>meaning "heathen" or mythological</note> or rather golden things, and the first gate of secrecy. With this, says Rhazes <note>original: "Rhasy"; Abu Bakr al-Razi, a famous Persian polymath and alchemist.</note> in his Epistle, the gates of science are opened. His son Jupiter succeeds him and expels his father from his kingdom, even dismembering him so that he might not father more sons; yet Venus, the most beautiful of women, sprang from his testicles being cast into the sea. From Jupiter (who is prepared "hot lead") the other planets are produced: Mars from Juno, Mercury from Maia (the daughter of Atlas, a mountain of Mauritania), and the Moon and Sun (Luna and Sol) from Latona. 

These four are brought into the world by <term>coction</term> <note>original: "coction"; the process of slow heating, boiling, or "cooking" a substance to mature it.</note> alone, or "women's work." Through coction, maturation and the scattering of the raw parts are achieved, which is performed by Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire, representing the alchemical furnace.</note> in philosophical vessels. It should not be supposed that this is common cooking in its method, though it agrees with it in its goal. For just as a woman matures fish in water—that is, softens, boils, and seethes them until all the excess moisture is released from them into the water and air—so the philosopher handles his subject. He soaks, melts, dissolves, solidifies, and fixes it in a special water that is stronger than the sharpest vinegar, within the "vessels of Hermes" <note>Hermetically sealed glass vessels.</note>. The joints of these vessels must be closed most perfectly, as is required, so that the water does not evaporate and the contents of the vessel do not burn. This is 

<meta>catchword: is</meta>

<summary>This page uses Greek and Roman mythology as an allegory for alchemical processes, specifically describing how "vulgar" lead (Saturn) is purified into "philosophical" tin (Jupiter) through washing and controlled heating (coction).</summary>

<meta>This page continues a description of alchemical heating apparatuses and the philosophical principles of maturation through vapor rather than direct contact with water.</meta>

# Discourse 22.

...that hole above the vessel, and the philosopher's pot, the Laconian bath <note>original: "Laconian bath" — a reference to a dry heat or steam bath used by the ancient Spartans, here used as an alchemical metaphor for heating</note>, in which the old man sweats: some people boil fish, lobsters, crabs, or green peas in a double boiler <note>original: "in a double"</note>, so that the said things are in the upper pot and only water is in the lower. The pots are placed one above the other with rings <note>original: "orbes"</note> so that the steam <note>original: "vapour"</note> may not escape. By this means, only the rising steam penetrates and matures the contents, making them much more tender and soft than if they had been boiled directly in water. 

This is the most praiseworthy method of the philosophers <note>Referring to alchemists, often called "chemical philosophers"</note>, by which they soften <note>original: "mollify"</note> that which is hard, dissolve that which is compact, and thin out <note>original: "rarefy"</note> that which is thick. For it is the air, or invisible <note>original: "insensible"</note> vapor, that matures, cooks, and perfectly ripens the seed—not raw and cold water itself. 

The air is also that which tints and colors the golden apples in the Orchards of the Hesperides <note>In Greek mythology, a garden producing golden apples; in alchemy, this often symbolizes the goal of the Great Work, such as the creation of gold</note>. For, if it is carefully considered, the boiling <note>original: "ebullition"</note> of water—by which raw meat is cooked <note>original: "sod"</note> until it is fit to eat—is nothing else but a thinning <note>original: "rarefaction"</note> and transformation <note>original: "transmutation"</note> of the water into an airy vapor. The bubbles are air contained within the water, which easily vanish away as the air leaves the water for its own natural place <note>original: "sphere"</note> and the water settles back to its own center.

<summary>The author explains how alchemists use steam and "invisible vapors" rather than direct boiling to soften and mature substances, using the analogy of a double boiler and the mythological golden apples.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Laconian bath, philosopher's pot, maturation, transmutation, Hesperides, ebullition, steam, double boiler</keywords>

## Emblem 23. On the Secrets of Nature.

->When Pallas was born, and Sol lay with Venus, it rained gold at Rhodes.<-

### Epigram 23.

> Your prodigy <note>a marvelous or prophetic sign</note> seems more than strange to us,
> Yet a grave authority <note>original: "Grave"; likely referring to a serious or respected historical author, such as Pindar or Strabo</note> has affirmed it as truth:
> The clouds twice declared their joy at Rhodes,
> Showering down gold through the yielding air;
> When Sol <note>the Sun god</note> pays the dues of love to Venus <note>the goddess of beauty and love</note>,
> And Pallas <note>Athena, the goddess of wisdom</note> sprang out of the brain of Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note>.

<summary>This emblem interprets the mythological event of golden rain falling on Rhodes as an allegory for the simultaneous birth of wisdom (Pallas) and the union of solar and venereal forces.</summary>
<keywords>Pallas Athena, Sol, Venus, Rhodes, golden rain, alchemy, mythology, Jove, Secrets of Nature</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical discourse on the symbolic "golden rain" at Rhodes, moving from a literal critique to an allegorical interpretation involving the Colossus and the chemical marriage of the Sun and Venus.</meta>

# Discourse 23.

It would be madness to affirm that gold ever rained upon the earth, unless it was understood allegorically. For there are no golden rivers or veins of gold in the clouds where it could be said to be generated, nor is gold so light that it could be thought to be lifted up by vapors. But a trope <term>A figurative or metaphorical use of a word or expression.</term> admits and explains all these things. For just as surely as Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, who according to myth was born fully armed from the forehead of Zeus (Jupiter).</note> actually sprang from **Jupiter’s** brain, and *Sol* <note>The Sun.</note> committed adultery with *Venus*, so also surely fell the golden rain. We do not doubt that both these things happened in a sense, but we must remove the common and literal meaning from things spoken allegorically. For if we follow the bare words of this emblem, nothing is more absurd than they are; yet if we follow the meaning, nothing is more true.

Now Rhodes is an island, first called *Ophiusa* <note>From the Greek 'ophis', meaning serpent.</note> because of its multitude of serpents. After that, it was called Rhodes from the garden of roses flourishing there, and finally it was called *Colossia*, from the *Colossus* of the Sun. This statue was located there and was esteemed among the seven wonders of the world. 

Regarding this, the ancient philosophers—observing their crude Mercurial matter <note>The raw, unrefined substance used at the start of the alchemical process.</note> showing itself like a serpent, but after preparation and coction <note>original: "coction"; the process of heating or "cooking" a substance in alchemy.</note> assuming the purple color of a rose—took certain similarities from the Island of Rhodes. This was also why they ascribed a golden shower to it, representing *Sol* (or Apollo) having coition with *Venus*. 

This thing, first spoken of metaphorically, gave the Rhodians an excuse to grow proud because such great gods had fathered children among them. This led them to erect a massive idol of the Sun of stupendous size and cost. For that *Colossus*, as histories relate, was 70 cubits high, and placed so that ships might pass between its legs with full sails. Its fingers were as big as ordinary statues, and few men could grasp its thumb. The artist was Chares of Lindos, the student of Lysippus, by whom it was made in twelve years. After fifty-six years, it was overturned by an earthquake, and it remained a miracle even as it lay in ruins. It is reported that the Sultan <note>original: "Soldan"</note> of Egypt, after winning and possessing Rhodes, loaded 900 camels with the brass from this statue.

"What the Sun is among the planets," says a philosopher, "that is gold among metals." This is chiefly attributed to the sun in respect of its heat, color, power, and essence. For this reason, a golden rain is ascribed to the generation of *Sol*, when "little Suns" are conceived by *Venus*. For *Venus* has a rosy color in her face, and if she is infused into the "seed" of *Sol*, the offspring produced from this must really be a "Rhodian." 

He is beautiful, like roses, the "Son of the philosophers," who draws and allures the eyes and minds of all men to him and deserves love. At his birth, it is no strange thing if miracles happen, for he will be miraculous in all his actions and call forth a golden rain. This is the Brother of Augias, the Son of *Sol*.

<summary>The author explains that the "golden rain" of Rhodes is an alchemical allegory for the creation of the Philosopher's Stone, linking the island's history and its famous Colossus to the symbolic marriage of Sol and Venus.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Rhodes, Colossus of Rhodes, Sol, Venus, golden rain, Chares of Lindos, metaphorical interpretation, mercury, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mythological figures as allegories for alchemical processes, following a description of King Augeas.</meta>

Discourse 23.

...who had oxen for an inheritance <note>original: "patrimony"</note>, the dung of which Hercules purged away in one day; so also is the brother of Aeetes <note>original: "Aeta"</note>, who possessed the Golden Fleece obtained by Jason. Concerning Pallas <note>another name for Athena</note>, who emerged from the brain of Jupiter without a mother, they report that she was brought forth at the river Triton, and therefore called Tritonia. She is represented <note>original: "feigned"</note> to be the goddess of wisdom; springing from the brain—the seat of wisdom—she is not without reason so called. A golden rain also adorned her birth <note>original: "nativity"</note> at Rhodes, so that after her birth she might remain in the memory of men.

In times of public or solemn joy, such as at the coronation of a king or the birth of a king's heir, pieces of gold are sometimes thrown among the common people like golden rain; the same occurred at the birth of Pallas. For Pallas is wisdom, who carries health in her right hand and riches in her left, and provides for the health and welfare of humanity. To her, people brought the petrifying <note>original: "saxifying"</note> head of Medusa, terrifying with its serpentine and viperous hair, which she uses on a shield against her enemies—a rude and barbarous people—turning <note>original: "transmuting"</note> them into stone.

And indeed, natural wisdom or philosophy makes those skeptical <note>original: "incredulous"</note> and envious despisers dull <note>original: "stupid"</note> and void of sense and understanding by that very thing from which Chrysaor was produced (who was the father of the three-bodied <note>original: "tricorporeall"</note> Geryon mentioned before). This is the petrifying Gorgonian blood, which is nothing else but the tincture of the Philosophical Stone <note>the legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting immortality</note>.

<summary>This page interprets the myths of Pallas Athena and Medusa as alchemical allegories, equating the "Gorgonian blood" with the transformative Tincture of the Philosophical Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Hercules, Jason, Golden Fleece, Pallas Athena, Jupiter, Rhodes, Medusa, Chrysaor, Geryon, Philosophical Stone, Alchemy, Mythology</keywords>

## Emblem 24. Of the Secrets of Nature.
## A wolf devoured the King and, being burned, restored him to life again.

---

### Epigram 24.

Take the ravenous wolf, and let him feast
His hungry stomach on the deceased King:
Let Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire; in alchemical texts, this refers to the fire of the furnace used for transmutation.</note> then stoke a fire, so that he
By the flames to ashes may reduced be;
Repeat this method, which at length will bring
Life and a lion's courage to the King.

<vocab>Alchemy, Emblem, Secrets of Nature, wolf, King, Vulcan, Lion's courage, Michael Maier</vocab>

<summary>This emblem describes an alchemical process of purification where a "wolf" (symbolizing antimony) consumes a "king" (gold) and is then consumed by fire to leave behind a refined, resurrected sovereign. It represents the chemical refinement of metals and the spiritual cycle of death and rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens, symbolism, purification, antimony, gold, resurrection</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<header>Discourse 24.</header>

<margin>Those with the [P. of G.?] are extremely hungry</margin>
It is a commonly known fact how great the hunger and voracity of a wolf is; for when he lacks food, in extreme hunger he feeds upon the earth. He is also said to vomit this up if he comes among great herds of cattle; for being weighed down by that material, he cannot be easily shaken off, but resists more strongly and furiously. Having entered into the sheepfolds, he not only kills what is sufficient for his belly, but through greediness, he also destroys the entire flock on every side. He is consecrated to Apollo and Latona, because he stood by her when she was in labor; for Latona could not have been delivered without the presence of the wolf. Therefore, the wolf is reported, not without reason, to be pleasing to Apollo, whose birthday he celebrates; this is also because his eyes shine and cast forth light at night. 

The lifeless body of the King is thrown to him while he is extremely hungry—not so that he should wholly consume and destroy it, but to restore life and strength to it by his own death. For there is a certain amorous power <note>original: "amatory virtue"</note> in the wolf’s tail which is infused into the King while he is half-dead, which makes him very desirable to all men as he restores his original health and beauty. 

The Hyrcanians <note>An ancient Iranian satrapy</note> bred dogs for no other use than to devour the bodies of dead men thrown to them, as Cicero relates. So too, the Massagetae <note>An ancient Eastern Iranian nomadic tribal confederation</note> give men who die of diseases as prey to dogs. But the philosophers <note>The alchemists</note> expose their King to a wolf. Indeed, the customs of the Sabeans did not please them, who carried out the corpses of the deceased in the same manner as dung and threw their Kings onto dung hills; nor did the customs of the Troglodytes, who tie the neck of a dead man to his feet and hurry him along with jests and laughter, burying him without any consideration of the place. 

Instead, they preferred to follow the customs of the Magi, who did not bury their bodies before they were torn to pieces by wild beasts; or of the Indians, who cause themselves to be buried alive while wearing crowns and singing the praises of the Gods, lest old age should prevent it. However, this custom was fatally imposed upon them all without any hope of resurrection or the renewal of life. The philosophers determine far differently; for they have the most certain knowledge that their dead King, once devoured by the wolf, will appear again with life, strength, and youth, and the wolf must be burned in his place. For the wolf may easily be put to death if he has so gorged his belly, but the King—though dead—nevertheless has a Martial <note>Relating to iron or the planet Mars; in alchemy, often referring to "Martial Regulus of Antimony."</note> or Swan-like <note>The swan represents the "albedo" or white stage of the alchemical process.</note> power <note>original: "virtue"</note> so that he cannot be wounded or consumed. 

But where must this wolf be sought, and from where must the King be obtained? The philosophers answer that the wolf wanders everywhere in mountains and valleys to take his prey; he must be drawn out of his hiding place and preserved for use. The King also comes from the East, weary from a long and tedious journey; at length, he falls down, and grief hastens his death when he sees himself held in no honor and so little esteemed among strangers and foreigners as to be sold into servi- <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("servitude") and continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>This discourse uses the alchemical allegory of a wolf devouring a king to describe a process of purification and resurrection, comparing it to various ancient burial customs. It signifies the chemical reaction where antimony (the wolf) "purifies" gold (the king), leading to a renewed state.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Michael Maier, Atalanta Fugiens, Grey Wolf, Antimony, King, Gold, Resurrection, Apollo, Latona, Ancient burial rites</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical allegory of the "Indian King" and the chemical processes required for his transformation. # Discourse 24.

...[grati]tude for a little money. But the wolf must be obtained from a colder region, for those that are bred in a cold climate are more fierce than those in Africa or Egypt because of the greater hunger resulting from external cold. This wolf devours the King and is afterwards revived with a lion's courage, who is then able to subdue all wild beasts. And though he appears to be the lowliest among his six brothers—that is, the youngest of all—yet after many tribulations and miseries, he will at length attain a most powerful kingdom. Whereupon Gratian says in the *Rosary* <note>original: *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a foundational 16th-century alchemical text</note>: "In Alchemy there is a certain noble body which is moved from master to master; in its beginning there will be misery and harshness <note>original: "sharpnesse"</note>, but in the end joy and gladness." And Alanus <note>Alanus ab Insulis, or Alain de Lille, a medieval theologian and author of alchemical works</note> says the same: 

<margin>N.B. <note>Latin: "Nota Bene" or "Note well"</note></margin>

"One thing is to be chosen and preferred above all, which is of a leaden <note>original: "livid"</note> color, having a clear and liquid metallic form <note>original: "species"</note>. It is a thing hot and moist, watery and parched <note>original: "adustive"</note>, and it is a living oil and a living tincture, a mineral stone, and a *water of life* <note>original: "aqua vitae"</note> of wonderful efficacy." It is not always safe for Kings to travel outside the borders <note>original: "confines"</note> of their kingdom; for if they hide <note>original: "abscond"</note> and are discovered, they are taken by their adversaries as spies and imprisoned. Also, if they are detected and proceed without an army, they are in similar danger. So also has it happened, or would have happened, to this Indian King, if death had not been prevented. 

This is the first sublimation <note>the process of heating a substance into vapor and then condensing it back to solid form to purify it</note>, washing <note>original: "lotion"</note>, and refining <note>original: "nobilitation"</note> which the philosophers use so that the second and third stages may be performed with greater success. For without this, those stages are of no efficacy, as the King is as yet weak-spirited <note>original: "pusillanimous"</note>, sleepy, and sick. For he must necessarily first require subsidies and tribute from his subjects, by which he may purchase garments and other necessities for himself; and afterwards he will be rich enough to be able to bestow new garments upon all his subjects as often as he pleases. For great things, for the most part, proceed from small beginnings and may then advance small things, or also suppress great things if they wish—as, for example, small cities at the beginning which have afterwards governed great things, and have made spacious and populous towns out of villages.

<summary>This page describes an alchemical allegory where a "King" is devoured by a "Wolf" from a cold region to gain strength, representing the purification and refinement of matter. It cites authorities like Gratian and Alanus to explain that the "noble body" of alchemy begins in misery but ends in power and wealth.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, the King, the Wolf, Gratian, Rosarium Philosophorum, Alanus, sublimation, nobilitation, aqua vitae, transmutation</keywords>

# Emblem 25. Concerning the Secrets of Nature.

->The Dragon does not die, unless he is killed by his brother and sister,<-
->who are Sol and Luna.<-

## Epigram 25.

> To kill the dragon will require much art <note>In alchemy, "art" refers to the skilled practice and secret methods of the Great Work.</note>,
> So surely that he never breathes <note>original: respire</note> again:
> Brother and sister must jointly attack
> His life, because nothing else can subdue <note>original: degrade</note> him;
> Luna is <note>The original text uses a shorthand 'y' for 'is'.</note> his sister, who slew Orion,
> And his brother Phoebus <note>A name for the sun god Apollo.</note> overthrew Python <note>In Greek mythology, Python was a serpent or dragon killed by Apollo.</note>.

<vocab>Emblem, Dragon, Sol <note>The Sun</note>, Luna <note>The Moon</note>, Epigram, Orion, Phoebus, Python</vocab>

<summary>This emblem explains that the "alchemical dragon" can only be overcome through the combined influence of the Sun (Sol) and the Moon (Luna). It uses mythological references to Apollo (Phoebus) and Diana (Luna) to illustrate the necessity of dual forces in the transmutation process.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Sol, Luna, Dragon, Phoebus, Python, Orion, transmutation, chemical marriage</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse 25, focusing on the alchemical interpretation of the myth of Jason and the Golden Fleece. The scribe continues to use 'y' as a shorthand for 'is'.</meta>

# Discourse 25.

In acquiring the Golden Fleece, the Dragon had to be killed first of all, because many men attempting the labor were overcome by the Dragon and destroyed with deadly poison. The reason was that they were not sufficiently armed against the Dragon's poison, nor instructed by what engines <note>original: "engines"; here meaning clever devices, machines, or methods</note> he might be slain. But Jason (representing the physician) neglected no manner of remedies, receiving several from Medea (representing the advice of the mind). Among those were the images of Sol <note>the Sun</note> and Luna <note>the Moon</note>, which he used to successfully obtain the victory and the reward—that is, the Golden Fleece. The Dragon, therefore, was slain by Sol and Luna, or their images, a point which the philosophers emphasize in many places. For an author of the *Rosary* <note>original: *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a foundational 16th-century alchemical text</note>, among others, has it thus:

> Hermes says: The Dragon does not die unless he is killed by his brother and sister—not by one alone, but by both together: namely, by Sol and Luna.

The philosophers' Mercury never dies unless it is killed by its sister—that is, it must be congealed by Luna or Sol. Note that the Dragon is *Argent vive* <note>original: "Argent vive"; literally "living silver" or quicksilver/mercury</note> extracted from bodies, having in it body, soul, and spirit, whereupon he says:

> The Dragon does not die except by his brother and sister—that is, Sol and Luna. This is Sulphur extracted, containing in it the nature of moisture and coldness according to Luna.

By those the dragon dies—that is, *Argent vive* extracted from the same bodies at first, which is the philosophers' *Aqua permanens* <note>permanent water</note>, which is made after putrefaction and after the separation of the Elements. That water is, by another name, called *Aqua foetida* <note>fetid or stinking water</note>. Those are his words, with which all the rest agree, so that there is no need to cite the authority of every one.

The people of Epidaurus worshipped the Dragon in the Temple of Apollo in commemoration of Python being slain. War and hatred are natural to the dragon and the Elephant; the dragon strikes at the Elephant's eyes and throat until the Elephant, falling to the ground, kills the dragon with the weight of its own body. By this means, "Dragon's blood" <note>a red resin used in alchemy and medicine, often associated with the mineral cinnabar</note> is imported into our countries. The Dragon has eyes with the value of gemstones; his sight is very sharp and clear, and therefore he is much used for the guarding of treasures, such as the apples of the Hesperides and the Golden Fleece at Colchis mentioned before.

The ancients assigned a dragon to Esculapius <note>Asclepius, the Greek god of medicine and healing</note> as a hieroglyphic. But the Chymists <note>alchemists</note> appropriate Dragons to their operations—not literally, but allegorically. For a dragon, whether he be fixed or volatile, always denotes Mercury. For this reason, Mercury is to be seen with two serpents twisted about his wand (the Dragon being a great serpent), and Saturn with one which devours his tail <note>the Ouroboros symbol</note>, as also Janus. A serpent is consecrated to Esculapius, the son of Apollo and the inventor of medicine (philosophical medicine). In this form, he is reported to have been brought from Epidaurus to Rome and there always worshipped for the ending of the pestilence he was supposed to have caused. But the philosophers' dragon is always most vigilant and lively, and not easily wounded, both by reason of the thickness of his skin and the sharpness of his teeth and poison, with which he is armed. For though common dragons are said to lack poison, this one is not without it, venting it upon anyone...

<summary>This page provides an alchemical commentary on the myth of Jason and the Golden Fleece, identifying the Dragon as Mercury (Argent vive) and explaining that it must be "killed" or congealed by the union of Sol and Luna. It also explores the symbolic role of dragons in ancient mythology and medicine, linking them to figures like Apollo, Esculapius, and Saturn.</summary>

<keywords>Golden Fleece, Jason, Medea, Sol and Luna, Hermes, Rosarium Philosophorum, Mercury, Argent vive, Aqua permanens, Aqua foetida, Apollo, Python, Dragon's blood, Esculapius, Chymistry, Alchemy, Ouroboros</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "philosophical dragon," focusing on how it must be managed through both force and the cooperation of its "kin" (Sol and Luna).</meta>

# Discourse 25.

...in his way, if he is not cautiously managed; for he can rarely be overcome by force alone, unless the cunning of his blood relatives <note>original: "consanguine relations"</note> is also included. As the poet truly says: "It is a safe and common way to deceive under the name of a friend." Yet, although it is a safe and common way, it is a crime—a fact seen elsewhere and in other affairs, but not here. Jugglers and charlatans <note>original: "mountebanks"</note> are said to kill and expel worms from children using the powder of other such worms—that is, treating brothers by way of brother and sister. See how the dragon must be killed by the brother and sister together, which refers to <term>Sol</term> <note>The Sun, representing gold or the masculine principle</note> and <term>Luna</term> <note>The Moon, representing silver or the feminine principle</note>. From this, it appears that the dragon is also one of the planets—namely, <term>Mercury</term>—or that which comes from bodies, as has already been shown in the <term>Rosary</term> <note>likely the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical text</note>.

Some of the Greeks affirm that during the reign of **Herod**, King of **Judea**, a dragon fell in love with a beautiful young woman of marriageable age and lay with her in bed; they also say that the Emperor **Tiberius** took delight in another dragon, which he commonly fed with his own hand. So too, the philosophical dragon, if he is handled correctly, leaves his ferocity behind and becomes a friend to man, though he remains malicious if treated otherwise. 

**Xanthus** the historian, along with **Pliny**, testifies that when a young dragon was killed, the parent restored it to life with an herb called **Balin**. However, I attribute this to a <term>philosophical allegory</term> <note>a symbolic story rather than literal history</note> rather than true history; for life is restored to a dead dragon, and death to a living one, through the stages of <term>chemical processes</term> alone. 

But some may ask: where and how must the dragon be captured? The philosophers answer most concisely with a verse:

> ->The mountains, the lands, and the springs give dragons to you:<-
> <note>original Latin: "Dant vobis montes, dracones, terrae, fontes"</note>

But you may easily understand the method of capturing him by looking at the care and industry with which many men watched to take a mighty dragon spotted in **Africa** to bring him to the Emperor **Tiberius**. Specifically, the dragon's accustomed path among the stones was enclosed and gradually narrowed, or he was stopped by nets and ropes, and at length tamed by clubs and blows; then, as a load for many carts, he was brought to a ship in which he was carried to **Rome**.

<summary>This discourse explains that the "philosophical dragon" (mercury) must be subdued through the combined influence of Sol and Luna, using historical and mythological anecdotes to illustrate the dragon's dual nature as both a dangerous beast and a potential friend.</summary>
<keywords>dragon, Sol, Luna, Mercury, Rosarium Philosophorum, Herod, Tiberius, alchemy, allegory, chemical processes</keywords>

### Epigram 26.
> To greater wisdom man cannot attain,

> Her left hand holds <note>original: "comprehends"</note> vast heaps of treasure;

<meta>This page continues the author's discourse on the nature of wisdom, transitioning from a general philosophical definition to a specific endorsement of chemical practice as the highest form of human knowledge.</meta>

<header>### Discourse 26.</header>

<margin>
Sirach 4:13.
Wisdom 8:16.

Wisdom 4:10.
Proverbs 3:18.
Ecclesiastes 6:9, 13.
Proverbs 3:35.

Tully <note>original: "Tully"; the Roman statesman and philosopher Marcus Tullius Cicero</note> has excellently described the essential difference of man, by which he differs from all other animals, in this manner: just as a bird is intended by nature for flying, and a horse for <gloss>running</gloss> ~~practicing~~, so man is intended for reasoning. For as lions, bears, and tigers exercise and delight themselves in fierceness; elephants and bulls in strength of body; and eagles, falcons, and other hawks in preying upon birds and swiftness of wings; so man excels these and all others in reason, the discourse of the mind, and understanding. Thus, there is no fierceness, no physical strength, no swiftness, or any such thing in beasts so great that it cannot be mitigated, subdued, and prevented by reason alone. 

For reason is not a human thing, or something proceeding from the earth, but as the poet says, a particle of divine breath sent from heaven into the body of man. It is sometimes called memory and sometimes intellectual virtue; if use or experience are added to it, wisdom springs from that source, which is most precious to man. For "Use" <note>original: "Use"; here meaning practical experience or practice</note> is reported to be the father, and "Reason" or "Memory" the mother of such a noble offspring. From this comes that common saying: *Use begat me, and my mother-memory brought me forth.* 

But the question is: what is true wisdom, and what is most worthy of human inquiry? There are infinite opinions concerning it, as every man interprets it according to his own imagination. It may be answered that wisdom in human things (always excepting divine things relating to the welfare of the soul) consists not in sophistical arguments, not in ornate and decorated discourses, not in the poetic sound of verse, and not in the critical subtleties of grammarians. It does not consist in willfulness and obstinacy, in deceit and perjury, in deceptive speech <term>equivocations</term> and lies, nor in the oppression and exploitation <note>original: "sweat of the poor"</note> of the poor. It is not the craft of heaping up wealth and riches, nor any other thing, but the true knowledge of chemistry <note>original: "Chymistry"; in this period, the term referred to the "spagyric" art of alchemy and chemical medicine</note>, together with its practice, which is most beneficial to mankind. 

This is the wisdom that surpasses all things, which goes to the East on the right hand and to the West on the left, and encompasses the whole earth. Solomon, in his book of Wisdom, reasons distinctly about wisdom, saying in Chapter 8, verses 9 and 14: "They that are acquainted with wisdom have eternal perseverance, and they that are her friends have sincere pleasure." And he who diligently seeks after her shall receive much joy, for there is no unpleasantness in her company, but only mirth and joy. Although wine and music also cheer the heart of man, wisdom is more pleasant than they are. For she is a tree of life to those who lay hold of her, and blessed are they who retain her, sustaining her fully as nourishment <term>aliment</term> for their souls. 

The wise shall inherit glory, and he who esteems wisdom shall be exalted and honored by her. For wisdom advances her children, and he who firmly adheres to her shall be promoted to honor. An immortal name also proceeds from her from age to age. She is more powerful than all things and comforts a wise man more than ten mighty princes who are in the city. To this wisdom of the world may also be applied what is spoken by the prophet Baruch: "Learn true wisdom, and you will find who it is that gives long life, wealth, joy, and peace." And in the 9th Chapter, verse 17 of Wisdom, it is affirmed that Wisdom...

<summary>The author defines true human wisdom not as rhetoric or greed, but as the practical application of "Chymistry" (alchemy), supporting this claim with philosophical arguments from Cicero and extensive biblical citations.</summary>
<keywords>Cicero (Tully), Alchemy (Chymistry), Wisdom of Solomon, Baruch, Reason vs. Instinct, Definition of Wisdom, Spagyric Medicine</keywords>

### Discourse 26.

<margin>Wisdom 7:11</margin>
This is secret advice in the knowledge of God; wisdom confers all things, and infinite riches are produced from the labor of her hands. All good things proceed from her, and great riches and wealth are in her hands, and he who converses with her is raised to honor by her. <margin>Sirach 1:25</margin> And Sirach, Chapter 1, verse 25, says: In the treasures of wisdom is understanding and holy knowledge. Elsewhere he calls it the discipline of sense, or the doctrine of understanding. Concerning this, <term>Morienus<note>original: "Morienus"; Morienus Romanus was a legendary 7th or 8th-century hermit and alchemist, famously credited with teaching the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note></term> the philosopher says: "This knowledge is that which draws its owner from the misery of this world and brings him to the knowledge of good things to come." And that it is the gift of God, he himself affirms in these words: "God confers this divine and pure knowledge upon his faithful servants—that is to say, those whom he determines from the original nature of things to confer it upon through his wonderful generosity."

And a little after, he writes: "For this can be nothing else but the gift of the Most High God, who commits and reveals it as he pleases, and also to whichever of his faithful servants he pleases. They, therefore, ought to be humble and altogether subject in all things to the omnipotent God." And later: "It is also fitting for you, O King, to know that this <term>magistery<note>In alchemy, the 'magistery' refers to the mastery of the "Great Work," specifically the creation of the Philosopher's Stone or the transformation of base matter into gold.</note></term> is nothing else but the Most High and great God's <term>arcanum<note>Latin for "secret" or "mystery." In this context, it refers to a profound, hidden truth of nature or a divine secret of transformation.</note></term> and secret of secrets. For this secret he commits to his prophets, whose souls he has placed in his paradise." 

It is also called the "tree of life"—not because it has only a small amount of life in it, but because it shows, as it were, the way to life and bears fruit wholesome for this life, which cannot exist without it. These fruits include health and the blessings of fortune and the mind. For without these, a man, even while living, is dead and not much different from a brute animal; though outwardly he represents the person he ought to be, he is not truly that person in his higher nature.

<summary>This page discusses the divine and secret nature of alchemical wisdom, framing it as a gift from God revealed only to the faithful. Citing biblical and philosophical authorities like Morienus, the text links this "magistery" to health, wealth, and the spiritual "tree of life."</summary>
<keywords>Wisdom, Morienus, magistery, arcanum, tree of life, divine knowledge, alchemy, Sirach</keywords>

## Emblem 27. Of the Secrets of Nature.

->He who endeavors to enter into the <term>Philosophers' Rosary</term><note>original: "Philosophers Rosary"; likely referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical text that used the metaphor of a rose garden to describe the stages of the alchemical process.</note> without a key is like a person who would walk without feet.<-

## Epigram 27.

->Roses in wisdom's garden do abound,<-
->But always under locks and bolts they are found:<-
->One single key it has, of humble esteem,<-
->Yet without that you swim against the stream.<-
->He who can scarcely on a level surface stand,<-
->In vain attempts <note>Parnassus is a mountain in Greece sacred to Apollo and the Muses in classical mythology; it represents the difficult heights of intellectual or artistic mastery.</note>Parnassus to ascend.<-
